    Environment & Ecology

    Handwritten-style UPSC notes with PYQ analysis, diagrams and active recall.

    Built from the whole of Ghatna Chakra PREVIEW (Purvavlokan), Environment & Ecology, 2025 (9th edition), a chapter-wise solved paper. Organised as Subject → Chapter → Topic → Subtopic. Paragraphs, lists, tables, question stems, correct answers and explanations are merged into one compressed entry per fact.

    Page references are PDF file pages. For all content (file pp. 7–286), the printed number is the file page plus 2, so file p. 7 is printed F-9.

    Each chapter opens with a clickable map and closes with its PYQ table and revision cues. Every major topic ends with Test Your Memory (Original Practice, not PYQs). Answers stay hidden until Reveal Answer. Corrections and later developments are boxed, never silently merged.

    Repeated questionsRecurring themesRecent PYQs 2025–26Coverage note
    PYQ-LINKED FACT tested in at least one UPSC CSE paper (State-only links stay in the chapter tables to keep pages uncluttered)
    REPEATED FACT / QUESTION same proposition in at least two distinct papers
    RECENT PYQ — YEAR connected to a verified 2025 or 2026 UPSC question
    CORE CONCEPT essential for understanding, even without recurrence
    COMMON TRAP misleading qualifier, exception or easily confused pair
    Source check correction or conflict in the source
    Verified update dated change since the book
    Added concept needed for understanding, not in the PDF
    Memory aid mnemonic, with the facts it encodes

    Environment & Ecology

    Environment and Sustainable Development

    Environment

    Everything surrounding an organism; its biotic and abiotic parts interact with each other.

    • Definition: sum total of biotic and abiotic components that surround and influence an organism.
    • Word from French Environ = encircle / surround (घेरना).
    • Broad concept: totality of social, biological, physical, chemical elements, individually and collectively, composing nature and human-made surroundings.
    • Total of conditions surrounding man at a given point in space and time; social, cultural, physical conditions affecting survival, growth, development of people, animals, plants.
    • Environment (Protection) Act, 1986: includes water, air, land and the inter-relationship among and between them and human beings, other living creatures, plants, micro-organisms, property. Book's short form: physical/abiotic + biotic components and their interaction (p. 7).
    • Composite of biotic factors (all living things) and abiotic factors (temperature, light intensity, amount of available water, etc.).
    • COMMON TRAP Three categories only: physical, biological, cultural. "Operational environment" is not one.
    ComponentIncludes
    Physical / abioticSoil, air, water; landforms, water bodies, climate, soils, rocks, minerals
    Biological / bioticPlant, human, animal, microorganisms
    CulturalMan-made features: economical, social, political elements
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name the three broad components of environment, with one example of each.

      Physical (soil), biological (microorganisms), cultural (political elements). p. 7

    2. Correct this: "Operational environment is one of the general categories of environment."

      False. The categories are physical, cultural and biological. p. 8

    3. Fill the labels: the Hydrosphere box in the book's figure branches into which four?

      Glacier, lake, river, ocean. p. 8

    Sustainable Development

    Development that meets present needs without compromising future generations' ability to meet theirs; a balance of economic development and environmental conservation.

    • Definition (Brundtland): meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs; also phrased as saving energy and other resources for the future without sacrificing people's comfort in the present.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Important for the well-being of human society; the definition is the reason why.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Inter-generational sensibility in respect of use of natural resources.
    • Main objective: conservation, proper management and just, prudent use of natural resources.
    • Discussion began after Our Common Future = Brundtland Report, WCED (World Commission on Environment and Development), 1987. Book: this report coined the term (p. 8).
    • Gro Brundtland: Norwegian politician, three terms as prime minister of Norway, chair of WCED. Key figures of the concept: Gro Brundtland, Maurice Strong, WCED.
    • Emphasised by UNCED; fully supported in Agenda-21 at Earth Summit 1992; prominent issue / main point of discussion at Johannesburg Summit 2002.
    • Important for SD: conservation of biological diversity, prevention and control of pollution, decreasing poverty (all three). Book: SD includes poverty mitigation (p. 8).
    • Three-part concept: economic, environmental and social development together; banner Venn on p. 7: Environmental Protection and Enhancement, Social Progress, Economic Development.
    • Unsustainable development: pursuing immediate rewards without thinking of harm to others or the planet. Where development is sustainable, everyone has access to decent work, quality health care, education.
    • Now an alternative approach to the theories of development. Understand nature first (its beauty, boundaries), then prepare the road map of development.
    • Compelling terms for SD rest on availability of information: Remote sensing technology, Statistical method, Geographical Information System (GIS), Environment information system. Policy needs information on environment and ecology.
    Added concept

    Circular economy (UPSC 2025, reported official key; not in the book): reduces raw-material inputs and wastage in production, and both of these explain its lower greenhouse-gas emissions.RECENT PYQ — 2025

    Chronology of milestones
    EventDate
    Publication of 'The Limits to Growth' report1972
    Publication of Brundtland Commission report1987
    Adoption of Montreal ProtocolSeptember 1987
    Enforcement of Montreal ProtocolJanuary 1989
    Rio Earth SummitJune 1992
    Common trap Montreal has two dates. Adoption (September 1987) falls in the same year as Brundtland; enforcement (January 1989) is what fixes the order asked in the question.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Which report and which body, in which year, started the SD discussion?

      Our Common Future (Brundtland Report), WCED, 1987. p. 9

    2. Put in order: Rio Earth Summit, Limits to Growth, Montreal enforcement, Brundtland Report.

      Limits to Growth 1972, Brundtland 1987, Montreal enforcement January 1989, Rio June 1992. p. 9

    3. In one sentence, how is SD "inter-generational"?

      It concerns how natural resources are used so that one generation's use leaves the next able to meet its needs. p. 9

    4. Name two sources of the information on which SD road maps are based.

      Any two: remote sensing, statistical method, GIS, environment information system. p. 8

    Water & Carbon Cycle in Nature

    Biotic and abiotic factors interact in cycles; energy and matter flow between them.

    Carbon cycle
    • CORE CONCEPT Add CO₂: respiration, decay of organic matter, volcanic action / eruptions, burning of fossil fuels.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’11, ’14 · 3 papers
    • Photosynthesis does not add CO₂: plants consume CO₂ and release oxygen.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’11, ’14 · 3 papers
    • Most autotrophs (plants) use CO₂ and water, in presence of sunlight and chlorophyll, to produce carbohydrates.
    • Elemental carbon: three crystalline allotropes: graphite, fullerene, diamond. Compound forms: CO₂, carbonates, hydrogen carbonates.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’11, ’14 · 3 papers
    • Carbon is recycled by different physical and biological processes.
    Water cycle and humidity
    • Solar radiation plays the most important role in the water cycle: it initiates and drives the cycle by evaporating water from oceans, lakes, rivers.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Vapour forms water droplets and clouds; droplets gain enough mass and fall to earth.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Essentially a closed system: volume of water in the hydrosphere today = amount always present in the earth system.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Humidity: a measure of water-vapour content of air (or other gas); measured using a hygrometer or psychrometer.
    • Amount of rain depends on humidity in the atmosphere: higher humidity, more vapour, more chance of cloud and rain if temperature drops.
    Root hairs and transplanted seedlings
    • Many transplanted seedlings do not grow because most root hairs are lost during transplantation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Root hairs collect water and mineral nutrients from soil and take the solution up through the roots to the rest of the plant.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Made up of cellulose and nominal Pectin, which helps as a gelling agent with soil.
    • Another reason: fertilizers raise soil concentration, making it difficult to absorb water; causes desiccation of plant cells and leaves turn brown.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Of respiration, photosynthesis, decay, volcanic action, which one does not add CO₂ and why?

      Photosynthesis, since plants consume CO₂ and release oxygen. p. 11

    2. Why does the book call the water cycle essentially closed?

      Water in the hydrosphere today equals the amount always present in the earth system. p. 11

    3. Link root-hair loss to seedling failure in one sentence.

      Root hairs absorb water and minerals, so losing them during transplantation stops uptake. p. 12

    4. Fill the label: water moves by imbibition from soil solution of ___ concentration to root-hair vacuole of ___ concentration.

      Less; high. p. 11

    Atmosphere

    The thin layer of gases surrounding the earth; air is a mixture of gases.

    • Thin layer of gases around earth = atmosphere; mainly Nitrogen, Oxygen, Carbon dioxide, etc.
    • Air is a mixture; by volume about 78% nitrogen, about 21% oxygen; CO₂, argon, methane, ozone, water vapour in very small quantities.
    • Nitrogen: most abundant gas (78.08% in dry air).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Noble gas keyed as not present in air: Radon (not in the permanent-gas table, p. 12). Naturally occurring radioactive gas, product of the Uranium decay chain; odourless, colourless, tasteless, inert. Moves through voids in rocks and soils; book adds it enters homes with soil gases via cracks and openings in building foundations (p. 13), so read the key as "not a listed constituent".
    • Smart Radon Monitor (SRM): advanced real-time, portable radon monitor for multiple applications in radon studies.
    Permanent gas (NCERT, book's table)%
    Nitrogen (N₂)78.08
    Oxygen (O₂)20.95
    Argon (Ar)0.93
    Carbon dioxide (CO₂)0.036
    Neon (Ne)0.002
    Helium (He)0.0005
    Krypton0.001
    Xenon0.00009
    Hydrogen0.00005
    Plants, trees and oxygen
    • Photosynthesis takes in CO₂ and releases oxygen: if all plants and trees vanished, oxygen would decrease. Book's figure: balance of O₂ and CO₂ between Plants and Animals.
    • Tree functions: providing oxygen, improving air quality, climate amelioration, conserving water, preserving soil, supporting wildlife; clean the environment; roadside trees reduce sound pollution.
    • Book: Amazon forest is estimated to produce about 20% of total oxygen in the atmosphere, hence named 'Lungs of the Earth'; contains nearly a third of all tropical rainforests left on Earth (Raj. PCS 2023 key).
    Source check Book says Amazon produces about 20% of atmospheric oxygen (p. 13). FactCheck.org calls this false: estimates are closer to 6–9%, and the Amazon consumes nearly all it produces. Keep 20% only as that paper's keyed answer; for the ocean's share see → Ch 2. source
    Environmental degradation and related terms
    • Degradation: deterioration through depletion of resources (air, water, soil), destruction of ecosystems, extinction of wildlife; includes adverse change by human activity, overall lowering of quality, ecological imbalance.
    • Weathering: natural process breaking rocks at the Earth's surface into sediments, clays, soils, dissolved substances. Typically begins when crust is uplifted by tectonic forces; loosened fragments then carried away by erosion.
    • Major environmental issues: climate change, pollution, environmental degradation, resource depletion.
    • Poverty alleviation = efforts to reduce or eliminate poverty, improve well-being, give access to resources for a dignified life; government policies to grassroots programs, symptoms and root causes.
    Source check U.P. UDA/LDA 2013 key (p. 14) says poverty alleviation is not related to environmental protection, yet the book lists poverty mitigation within SD (p. 8) and poverty eradication among Agenda 21's key elements (p. 19). Treat as that paper's key only.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the book's percentages for the first four gases in dry air.

      N₂ 78.08, O₂ 20.95, Ar 0.93, CO₂ 0.036. p. 12

    2. What kind of substance is air: compound, element or mixture?

      Mixture of gases. p. 12

    3. Correct this: "The Amazon is reliably shown to supply 20% of atmospheric oxygen."

      That is the book's keyed figure; FactCheck.org puts it nearer 6–9% and notes the Amazon consumes nearly all of it. p. 13

    4. What carries away the fragments loosened by weathering?

      Erosion. p. 14

    Sustainable Development Goals

    Seventeen interlinked goals adopted by the UN in 2015, to be realised by 2030.

    • Global Goals: collection of 17 interlinked objectives adopted by UN in 2015 as a "shared blueprint for peace and prosperity for people and the planet, now and into the future". Space research is not a goal.
    • COMMON TRAP SDGs were proposed in 2012 at Rio+20, not in 1972 by the Club of Rome.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Officially 'Transforming our World: the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development'; 17 aspirational goals with 169 targets; to be achieved by 2030.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Nodal institution for coordination and implementation in India: NITI (National Institution for Transforming India) Aayog.
    #GoalAim (compressed)
    1No PovertyEnd poverty in all forms everywhere
    2Zero HungerEnd hunger; food security, improved nutrition, sustainable agriculture
    3Good Health and Well-beingHealthy lives, well-being for all at all ages
    4Quality EducationInclusive, equitable education; lifelong learning
    5Gender EqualityEmpower all women and girls
    6Clean Water and SanitationAvailability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all
    7Affordable and Clean EnergyAffordable, reliable, sustainable, modern energy
    8Decent Work and Economic GrowthSustained, inclusive growth; full, productive employment
    9Industry, Innovation and InfrastructureResilient infrastructure, sustainable industrialization, innovation
    10Reduced InequalitiesWithin and among countries
    11Sustainable Cities and CommunitiesInclusive, safe, resilient, sustainable settlements
    12Responsible Consumption and ProductionSustainable patterns
    13Climate ActionUrgent action on climate change and its impacts
    14Life Below WaterConserve, sustainably use oceans, seas, marine resources
    15Life on LandCombat desertification, manage forests sustainably, halt and reverse land degradation, halt biodiversity loss
    16Peace and Justice Strong InstitutionsPeaceful, inclusive societies; access to justice
    17Partnerships for the GoalsGlobal Partnerships for Sustainable Development
    • SDG-6 context (book): approx. 2.2 billion people lack safely managed drinking water; more than 4.2 billion lack safely managed sanitation.
    SDG Index and SDG India Index

    Global index measures countries' progress towards the goals. Ranks are as printed for each edition.

    • Overall country score: 71 in 2023-24, up from 66 in 2020-21 and 57 in 2018.
    • Fastest-moving States, 2018 to 2023-24 (score gain): Uttar Pradesh 25, J&K 21, Uttarakhand 19, Sikkim 18, Haryana 17, Assam, Tripura and Punjab 16 each, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha 15 each.
    • Underlying SDG dimensions closely tied to education and health: positive correlation between HDI (Human Development Index) and SDG rankings of Indian States.
    Source check Under the 2019-20 question (p. 16) the book prints Kerala 75 and Himachal 74, but its next answer gives 2019-20 as Kerala 70 / Himachal 69 and assigns 75 to the 2020-21 edition. Either way Gujarat is outside the top five.
    Sustainable agriculture
    • Production of food, fibre, other plant or animal products by techniques protecting environment, public health, human communities, animal welfare; in short, using land so that its quality remains intact.
    • Chemical fertilizers and pesticides have adversely affected soil health; response: National Mission for Sustainable Agriculture (NMSA), for agricultural productivity, water use efficiency, nutrient management, livelihood diversification; part of National Action Plan on Climate Change.
    • Best environment-friendly strategy for Indian agriculture: mixed cropping, organic manures, nitrogen-fixing plants, pest-resistant crop varieties.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’93
    • UN declared 2022 the International Year of Basic Sciences for Sustainable Development.
    • Conservation Agriculture (FAO): resource-saving crop production seeking acceptable profits with high, sustained production while conserving the environment.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Principles: minimum tillage and soil disturbance; permanent soil cover with crop residues and live mulches; crop rotation and inter-cropping. Avoiding plantation crops and avoiding monoculture were outside the keyed set (IAS 2018).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • GACSA (Global Alliance for Climate-Smart Agriculture): set up 2014, not an outcome of Paris 2015; concept developed by FAO in 2010 paper "Climate Smart Agriculture: Policies, Practices and Financing for food security, Adaptation and Mitigation". Membership creates no binding obligations; book: India just a signatory.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Club of Rome
    • Born April 1968 when 30 people (scientists, educators, economists, humanists, industrialists, government officials) met at Academia dei Lincei, Rome; informal organization. Propounded 'Limits to Growth'.
    • Association of economists, civil servants, scientists promoting understanding of global challenges. Five priority areas: emerging New Civilisations; Planetary Emergency; Reframing Economics; Rethinking Finance; Youth Leadership and Intergenerational Dialogues.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Which SDG covers water availability and sanitation for all, and how many targets do the SDGs have in all?

      SDG 6; 169 targets. p. 15, 17

    2. Correct this: "SDGs were first proposed in 1972 by the Club of Rome."

      Proposed in 2012 at Rio+20; the Club of Rome is linked to 'Limits to Growth'. p. 15, 30

    3. Which two States were jointly first in SDG India Index 2023-24, and with what score?

      Uttarakhand and Kerala, 79. p. 15

    4. Name the three principles of Conservation Agriculture given in the book.

      Minimum tillage; permanent soil cover; crop rotation and inter-cropping. p. 31

    Earth Summit (RIO Summit)

    UNCED, Rio de Janeiro, 3–14 June 1992: a blueprint for international action on environment and development.

    • UN Conference on Environment and Development (UNCED) = 'Earth Summit', Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, 3–14 June 1992; 20th anniversary of first Human Environment Conference, Stockholm, Sweden, 1972.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Primary objective: broad agenda and new blueprint for international action on environment and development issues.
    • More than 100 heads of states met. Leaders signed convention on climate change and convention on Biodiversity, endorsed the Rio Declaration and the Forest Principles, adopted Agenda 21.
    • Commission on Sustainable Development (CSD) created to monitor and report on implementation of the Earth Summit agreement.
    • Earth Summit +5: special session of UN General Assembly, New York, 23–27 June 1997; five-year review of how countries, international organizations and civil society responded.
    • Rio+10 = World Summit on Sustainable Development (WSSD), Johannesburg, South Africa, 2002 (26 August to 4 September); biggest UN event of its kind to date; major objective: strategies for greater and more effective implementation of Agenda 21.
    • Rio+20 held in Rio de Janeiro, 2012.
    • Book's keyed name for the 1992 Rio meeting: "United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development" (see check below).
    Source check Book's answers name the 1992 Rio meeting "United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development" (pp. 18–19), but its notes call 1992 UNCED and reserve that name for Rio+20, 2012 (pp. 17, 19). Remember: 1992 = UNCED; 2012 = UNCSD.
    Global Environment Facility (GEF)
    • Established on the eve of the 1992 Rio Earth Summit to tackle the planet's most pressing environmental problems.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • CORE CONCEPT Financial mechanism for five conventions: Minamata (Mercury), Stockholm (Persistent Organic Pollutants), UN CBD, UNCCD (Combat Desertification), UNFCCC.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • International partnership of 186 countries and 18 implementing agencies; supports multi-stakeholder alliance to preserve threatened ecosystems on land and oceans, build green cities, boost food security for a prosperous, climate-resilient world.
    • Not a research body and not an OECD agency (IAS 2014 distractors, rejected by the key).
    • UNDP, UNEP and World Bank are associated with GEF. Book's position: Carlos Manuel Rodriguez is Chief Executive Officer and Chairperson.
    • Provides the Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF): provision and coordination of National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs).
    • Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF): established by COP7 (Marrakesh, 29 October to 10 November 2001); helps developing countries in adaptation and technology transfer.
    Source check Book's text says GEF serves five conventions (p. 18), while its figure on the same page also lists the Montreal Protocol under Conventions.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Distinguish the 1992 and 2012 Rio conferences by full name.

      1992: UN Conference on Environment and Development (UNCED). 2012: UN Conference on Sustainable Development (Rio+20). p. 17, 19

    2. Where and when was Earth Summit +5?

      UN General Assembly special session, New York, 23–27 June 1997. p. 17

    3. Name the five conventions for which GEF is the financial mechanism.

      Minamata, Stockholm, CBD, UNCCD, UNFCCC. p. 18

    4. Correct this: "GEF is an OECD agency doing scientific research."

      It is a financial mechanism, an international partnership of countries and agencies. p. 18

    Agenda - 21

    A voluntary UN action plan for sustainable development, product of the 1992 Earth Summit.

    • Legally non-binding, voluntarily implemented action plan of the UN on Sustainable Development (a global action plan).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • COMMON TRAP Originated at the Earth Summit, Rio 1992, not at Johannesburg 2002.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Related to sustainable development. Adopted with the Rio Declaration on Environment and Development and the Statement of Principles for the Sustainable Management of Forests by more than 178 Governments at UNCED, 3–14 June 1992.
    • Full implementation of Agenda 21, the Programme for Further Implementation and commitments to Rio principles strongly reaffirmed at WSSD, Johannesburg, 2002.
    • Key elements (figure): survey of biological diversity and protection of endangered species; poverty eradication and population control in developing countries; stress on generous capital transfer; food, clean water and social security to everyone.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Is Agenda 21 binding? Where did it originate?

      Non-binding and voluntary; Earth Summit, Rio de Janeiro, 1992. p. 19

    2. State the four key elements in the book's figure.

      Biodiversity survey and species protection; poverty eradication and population control; capital transfer; food, clean water, social security. p. 19

    3. Fill the sequence: Stockholm 1972 → ___ 1992 → Agenda 21 → ___ 1997.

      Earth Summit; Earth Summit +5. p. 18

    RIO + 20 Conference

    UN Conference on Sustainable Development, Brazil, June 2012, marking 20 years since UNCED.

    • Rio+20 = United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development (UNCSD; also Rio 2012, Earth Summit 2012), Rio de Janeiro, June 2012: 20th anniversary of 1992 UNCED and 10th anniversary of 2002 WSSD, Johannesburg.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Outcome: non-binding document 'The Future We Want', a 49-page action plan. Book: third international conference on sustainable development.
    • Document called on governments, businesses and the UN to support countries in transition to a green economy; PAGE (Partnership for Action on Green Economy) created as the UN's direct response.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. What was the title of the Rio+20 outcome document?

      The Future We Want. p. 19, 20

    2. From which conference did PAGE emerge, and why?

      Rio+20, 2012, as the UN's direct response to its call on green-economy transition. p. 19

    3. Rio+20 marked which two anniversaries?

      20th of UNCED 1992; 10th of WSSD 2002. p. 19

    Organizations & Institutions

    National and international bodies established for environmental conservation and protection.

    National bodies
    • NEERI (National Environmental Engineering Research Institute): Nagpur, Maharashtra; established 1958 as Central Public Health Engineering Research Institute (CPHERI).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • CPHERI focus: water supply, sewage disposal and related diseases, to some extent industrial pollution. Pioneering laboratory in environmental science and engineering; part of Council of Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • Five zonal laboratories: Chennai, Delhi, Hyderabad, Kolkata, Mumbai. Under Ministry of Science & Technology, Government of India.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • National Environment Appellate Authority (NEAA, "better known as NEA"): set up by Ministry of Environment and Forest under NEAA Act 1997 to hear appeals on restriction of areas in which industries, operations or processes may or may not be carried out, subject to safeguards under Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.
    • NEAA stands dissolved on establishment of NGT (18 October 2010); role taken over by NGT.
    • NITI Aayog: nodal for SDGs ↗.
    Source check Book keys the N.E.A. question "(*)" (p. 21), giving no listed option as correct; it only explains NEAA. Do not treat "National Environment Authority" as the expansion.
    International bodies and agencies
    BodyFacts (book's position where a post-holder is named)
    WMO (World Meteorological Organization)HQ Geneva, Switzerland. Framework for international cooperation in meteorology and operational hydrology. 193 Member States and Territories. Originated from International Meteorological Organization (IMO), founded 1873 for exchange of weather information across borders; became WMO 17 March 1951. Secretary-General: Prof. Celeste Saulo
    UNEPEstablished 1972; HQ Nairobi, Kenya. Executive Director: Inger Andersen of DenmarkREPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    EPA (Environment Protection Agency), U.S.Federal agency established 2 December 1970; protects human health and environment by writing and enforcing regulations based on laws passed by Congress
    Greenpeace InternationalNon-governmental organization; HQ Amsterdam, Netherlands; offices in over 55 countries; goal "ensure the ability of the Earth to nurture life in all its diversity"
    • US EPA: largest SO₂ source = burning of fossil fuels by power plants and other industrial facilities. Smaller: industrial processes such as extracting metal from ore; natural sources such as volcanoes; locomotives, ships, other vehicles and heavy equipment burning high-sulfur fuel.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’24
    • UNEA-4: Nairobi, Kenya, 11–15 March 2019; theme "Innovative solutions for Environmental Challenges and Sustainable Consumption and Production".
    • UNEA-5: (virtually) Nairobi, February 2021; theme "Strengthening Actions for Nature to Achieve the Sustainable Development Goals".
    • UNEA-7 (book, as scheduled): 8–12 December 2025, UNEP headquarters, Nairobi.
    Added concept

    UPSC 2026 asked FAO's "Four Betters" for "Blue Transformation" (context: UN Ocean Conference, France, June 2025; sustainable fisheries and aquaculture). Coaching-consensus answer, not an official key: better production, better nutrition, better environment, better life. Not in the book.RECENT PYQ — 2026

    Source check The book's UNEA-7 theme breaks off mid-phrase: "Advancing sustainable solutions for a resilient" (p. 23). Check the full wording before quoting.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Match: NEERI, WMO, UNEP, Greenpeace with Nairobi, Geneva, Amsterdam, Nagpur.

      NEERI Nagpur; WMO Geneva; UNEP Nairobi; Greenpeace Amsterdam. p. 21

    2. What replaced the National Environment Appellate Authority?

      The National Green Tribunal; NEAA stood dissolved on its establishment. p. 22

    3. Under the US EPA, what is the largest source of SO₂ emissions?

      Burning of fossil fuels by power plants and other industrial facilities. p. 22

    4. From which body and in which year did WMO originate?

      International Meteorological Organization, 1873. p. 21

    Acts - Related to Environment Conservation

    The 1986 Act is India's umbrella statute; the NGT is the specialised forum for disputes.

    Constitutional basis
    • 42nd Amendment Act, 1976: added provisions in Directive Principles and Fundamental Duties.
    • Article 48A (DPSP): State to "protect and improve the environment and to safeguard the forests and wildlife of country".
    • Article 51A(g) (Fundamental Duty): every citizen to "protect and improve the natural environment including forest, lakes, rivers and wildlife and to have compassion for living creatures".
    Environment (Protection) Act, 1986
    • Enacted in 1986; extends to the whole of India; objective: protection and improvement of the environment.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • Known as Umbrella Legislation: framework for Central Government coordination of central and state authorities under previous laws such as the Water Act and the Air Act.
    • Enacted after the Bhopal Gas Tragedy under Article 253 of the Constitution; implements decisions of UN Conference on the Human Environment, Stockholm 1972.
    • Empowers Central Government to establish authorities [section 3(3)] with mandate to prevent pollution in all forms and tackle problems peculiar to different parts of the country. Book's dated statement: last amended in 1991 (pp. 23–24).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC): constituted under this Act; renamed from Genetic Engineering Approval Committee on 22 July 2010; apex body in Ministry of Environment and Forest under 'Rules for manufacture, use, import, export and storage of hazardous microorganism/genetically engineered organism or cells 1989'.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Central Ground Water Authority (CGWA): constituted under section 3(3); regulates and controls development and management of ground water.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • CGWA powers: (i) section 5 directions on matters in section 3(2); (ii) penal provisions, sections 15 to 21; (iii) regulate and control ground water, issue regulatory directions; (iv) section 4 appointment of officers.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Protection of ecological balance: directly Forest Policy and Environment (Protection) Act; Industrial Policy and Educational Policy also include environmental approach (key: all four).
    National Green Tribunal (NGT)
    • National Green Tribunal established on 18 October 2010 under NGT Act, 2010: effective, expeditious disposal of cases on environmental protection and conservation of forests and other natural resources, including enforcement of legal rights relating to environment.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Enacted in consonance with Article 21: right to healthy environment construed as part of Right to Life. Not Article 275(1) or Article 243(A) (IAS 2012 key).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Provides compensation to victims for property damage; helps reduce trials pending in high courts.
    • First chairman: Justice Lokeshwar Singh Panta (18 October 2010). Book's position: present chairperson Justice Prakash Shrivastava.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    Source check Book says the Tribunal must solve applications and petitions within one month of filing (p. 23). NGT's own site says it is mandated to endeavour for final disposal within 6 months of filing. source
    India's climate instruments (as cited)
    • Clean Development Mechanism (CDM): Article 12 of Kyoto Protocol of UNFCCC; lets Annex-B Party with an emission commitment implement emission-reduction project in developing countries; projects earn saleable certified emission reduction (CER) credits, each = one tonne of CO₂, counted towards Kyoto targets.
    • National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC): established August 2015 to meet the adaptation cost of States and Union Territories particularly vulnerable to climate change.
    Source check On "not true" for India's climate steps (J.P.P.C.S. 2016, p. 25), the book keys "not attended the 19th BASIC meeting, Sun City" only because it has "no relation" to climate impact; it does not say whether India attended.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Why is the 1986 Act called umbrella legislation, and under which Article was it enacted?

      It frames Central coordination of authorities under earlier laws; Article 253. p. 24

    2. Explain the NGT–CPCB distinction on origin.

      NGT under the NGT Act 2010; CPCB is also statutory, under the Water Act 1974, so it is not a creation of executive order. p. 26

    3. Which constitutional Articles did the 42nd Amendment add on environment?

      Article 48A and Article 51A(g). p. 24

    4. Correct this: "NGT must decide every case within one month."

      The book says one month, but NGT's own site says it endeavours for disposal within 6 months of filing. p. 23

    5. Name one body each constituted under section 3(3) and under the 1989 Rules of the EP Act.

      CGWA; GEAC. p. 24

    Environment - Some Important Facts For Exams

    Standalone facts on books, labels, days, programmes and places.

    Authors and pioneers
    • 'Silent Spring' (1962): Rachel Carson, American marine biologist and ecologist; set the tone for the world environmental movement.
    • 'Green Development: Environment and Sustainability in the Third World': W.M. Adams (William M. Adams); first edition 1990.
    • Masanobu Fukuoka: Japanese farmer and philosopher, discoverer of Natural Farming, re-vegetation of desertified lands; book "The One-Straw Revolution" (1975): farming in harmony with nature, minimal intervention, rejecting chemicals, tilling, excessive manipulation of land.
    World Environment Day
    • Celebrated on 5 June; decided at Stockholm Summit 1972 (1st World Summit on Environment); first celebrated 1973. 2022 marked 50 years since Stockholm.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    YearHostTheme
    2018IndiaBeat Plastic Pollution
    2019ChinaAir Pollution
    2020Colombia in partnership with GermanyBiodiversity
    2021PakistanEcosystem Restoration
    2022Only One Earth: living sustainably in harmony with nature
    2024Land restoration, desertification and drought resilience; slogan 'Our Land. Our Future. We are #GenerationRestoration'
    Labels, greenwashing and consumer indices
    • Eco-Mark (Ecomark, 'ECOMARC'): eco-labelling scheme started 1991 for environment-friendly products; certification mark issued and administered by Bureau of Indian Standards, for products ensuring least impact on the ecosystem.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Covers: soaps and detergents, food items, lubricating oils, packing/packaging materials, architectural paints and powder coatings, batteries, electric and electronic goods, food additives, wood substitutes, cosmetics, aerosols and propellants, plastic products, textiles, fire-extinguisher, leather, coir and coir products.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Environment-friendly product: made, used or disposed of in a way that significantly reduces the harm it would otherwise cause.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • COMMON TRAP Greenwashing: an unsubstantiated or misleading claim of environmental benefit, conveying a false impression that a company's products are eco-friendly and environmentally sound.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’22 · 2 papers
    • Greendex: National Geographic Society with GlobeScan; first survey January 2008, 14 countries; measures environmentally sustainable consumer behaviour. India ranked first in 2009, retained 2014.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    Programmes and cases
    • Australia's Green Army: six-month programme for 17–24-year-olds; restoring native vegetation, heritage restoration, protecting animal habitats, regenerating wetlands in urban, rural, remote areas. Closed 30 June 2018.
    • Plachimada: village in Palakkad district, Kerala; Chittur block's Perumatty Panchayat. 2000: licence to Hindustan Coca-Cola Beverages Private Limited (HCCBPL) for Coca-Cola (Coke), Fanta, Limca, Thums Up, Sprite, Kinley.
    • Groundwater contaminated by the company's chemical waste; local protest, supported by Panchayat; became an icon of such movements worldwide against a multinational company (MNC).
    • Greenhouse farming launched in Punjab: agreement between Markfed of Punjab and Sayag Group of Israel, April 2001.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Urbanisation, capital and climate index
    • Excess urbanization and industrialization harm balanced development, environment and ecology, and biodiversity.
    • Negative aspects of development discussed in mid-80s: degradation of natural resources, environment pollution, displacement and rehabilitation of masses. Politics and Development was not among them (book's key).
    • India is not over-urbanised (Assertion false), but most large cities lack adequate infrastructure (Reason true): Drinking Water, Transport, Sanitation, Electricity, etc.
    • Natural capital: all renewable and non-renewable environmental resources (air, water, land, minerals, forests) created by nature without human interference. Roads excluded.
    • Natural vegetation is the true index of climate; water-loving / aquatic plants occur in moist climate, mostly muddy land and near ponds; reduced moisture leads to their erosion; increased atmospheric CO₂ also affects vegetation.
    • Smart City Mission: drive economic growth, improve (not merely stabilise) quality of life through local development and technology for smart outcomes.
    • Core elements: adequate water supply; assured electricity; sanitation incl. solid waste management; efficient urban mobility and public transport; affordable housing, especially for the poor; robust IT connectivity and digitalization; good governance, especially e-Governance and people's participation; sustainable environment; safety and security of citizens, particularly women, children, elderly; health and education.
    Source check Citing the census of 2011, book says "121 crore people (68.9%)" live in rural areas (p. 31), which clashes with its own 37.7 crore (31.1%) urban: 37.7 crore at 31.1% implies a total near 121 crore, so 121 crore is the country total, not the rural count.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

      5 June; 1973; India. p. 26

      Petrol. p. 31

    Ecology

    Ecology

    Ecology studies organisms in relation to their environment; the ecosystem is its functional unit.

    • Ecology: branch of biology on the relationship between organism and environment, including interaction with abiotic components.
    • Term ('Oekologie') coined by German zoologist Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919; book also prints "Hackle"), 1860s, to describe the 'economics' of living forms. Greek Oikos (house / living space) + logos (science / study).
    • Haeckel: study of relation of the animal to its organic and inorganic environment. Eugene Odum: study of structure and function of nature.
    • Now broader: plants, animals, climate, and also human beings, society and physical surroundings.
    • Book's figure (p. 32): Climate, Vegetation, Animal and Soil all interact in both directions.
    Ecosystem
    • Term proposed by A.G. Tansley, 1935: category of physical systems of organisms and inorganic components in relatively stable equilibrium, open and of various sizes and kinds.
    • Other names for the idea: Karl Mobius, Biocoenosis; Forbes, Microcosm.
    • Community of living organisms (plants, animals, microbes) with non-living components (air, water, mineral soil), interacting as a system through nutrient cycles and energy flows.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Size varies: a water droplet / drop in a pond (micro ecosystem) to an ocean; Earth itself is a large ecosystem.
    • COMMON TRAP Ecosystem is an open system, not closed: continuous but variable influx and loss of materials and energy.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Functional unit of nature; integrates the whole mosaic of interacting organisms and environment; capable of energy transformation, circulation and accumulation; has its own productivity; occupies a definite area in the Biosphere.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Four basic components: abiotic materials, producers, consumers, decomposers or reducers (last three = biotic).
    • Best description: a community of organisms together with the environment in which they live.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Components are interdependent: if the population of one organism rises or falls the rest is affected (foxes killed, rabbits increase). Human activities impact the environment through overpopulation, pollution, burning fossil fuels, deforestation, triggering climate change, soil erosion, poor air quality, undrinkable water.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Book says an ecosystem "covers a definite area" (p. 32) yet in an answer calls it a unit "with no limits of boundaries" (p. 33).
    Biosphere
    • Largest ecosystem of the earth = Biosphere; sum total of all ecosystems on the Earth; part of earth where life exists.
    • Integrated zone of atmosphere (air), hydrosphere (water), lithosphere (land); thin layer of soil, rock, water, air; narrow layer around the surface.
    • Absent at extremes of the North and South Poles, the highest mountains and the deepest oceans.
    • Book's key (UP RO/ARO 2017): Biosphere is an example of an open system (true); the Reason denying reinvestment of new substances is false, since input and output of substances occur.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Who coined Oekologie and who proposed the term ecosystem, with years?

      Ernst Haeckel, 1860s; A.G. Tansley, 1935. p. 32

    2. Correct this: "An ecosystem is a closed system."

      It is an open system, with continuous but variable influx and loss of materials and energy. p. 33

    3. Name the four basic components of an ecosystem.

      Abiotic materials, producers, consumers, decomposers. p. 33

    4. Where is the biosphere absent?

      Extremes of the North and South Poles, highest mountains, deepest oceans. p. 34

    Components of Ecosystem

    All organisms and abiotic components of a place form its ecosystem; levels of organisation and ecosystem types build on this.

    • All components in mutual interaction in the biosphere form an ecosystem; living organisms form biosphere by different biological associations.
    • Biotic components: living things shaping an ecosystem. Producers (plants), Consumers (animals, humans), Decomposers (fungi, bacteria).
    • Autotrophs produce their own food. Consumers are heterotrophs, getting energy by consuming other organisms, not by photosynthesis.
    • Decomposers break organic matter of dead bodies into inorganic material (not inorganic to organic).
    • Air is abiotic; plants, bacteria, animals are biotic.
    • Habitat: actual location where an organism lives; provides food, water, air, shelter. Biome: plant and animal community covering a large geographical area; major biomes: forest, grassland, desert, tundra.
    Levels of biological organisation
    • Order, organism to biosphere: Population, Community, Ecosystem, Landscape.
    • Population: individuals of the same species, same area, capable of reproducing with one another.
    • Community: populations of two or more species in the same area and time period.
    • Ecosystem: community plus abiotic components. One or more ecosystems in a fixed terrain form a Landscape of a particular region.
    • Biome: large ecological area of similar climate, can hold multiple ecosystems. Biosphere: all living organisms with the spheres they interact with (lithosphere, hydrosphere, etc.).
    Types of ecosystem
    • Artificial: fulfils criteria of a natural ecosystem but made and controlled by humans: orchards, farmlands, field, rice field, gardens, human-made reservoirs. Forests, ponds, lakes are natural.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Agriculture under man-made ecosystem has been instrumental to the existence of society and development.
    • Non-terrestrial: aquatic, marine, wetlands. Five major terrestrial ecosystems: desert, forest, grassland, taiga, tundra. Further classified by geographical region and dominant plant type. Marine is not a sub-category of terrestrial.
    • Aquatic: living and non-living parts of a water body and their interactions; marine + freshwater. Marine is the biggest type: about 71% of earth's surface, about 97% of planet's water.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • CORE CONCEPT Marine / ocean = most stable ecosystem: remains unchanged over a long period, irrespective of geological and anthropogenic processes.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • About 85% of dissolved materials in seawater are sodium and chlorine. Stratification is a feature of the marine ecosystem.
    • Oceans: cradle of life on Earth, highest level of biodiversity; no terrestrial ecosystem matches the biological richness of a coral reef, in variety and originality of survival strategies.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    Remember Two "largest" answers: Biosphere = largest ecosystem overall; Marine / ocean = largest among the types listed.
    Source check The book's figure prints "Aphotic Zone" directly below Sea Level (p. 35), though aphotic means without light. Treat this as the figure as printed, and check zone order before using it.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Reconstruct the order from organism to biosphere asked in exam.

      Population, Community, Ecosystem, Landscape. p. 35

    2. Distinguish habitat from biome.

      Habitat is where an organism lives; a biome is a plant and animal community over a large geographical area. p. 35

    3. What share of the earth's surface and of its water do marine ecosystems hold?

      About 71% and about 97%. p. 35

    4. Fill the labels: three man-made ecosystems in the book's figure.

      Farmland, aquarium, space ship. p. 35

    5. Correct this: "Decomposers convert inorganic matter into organic matter."

      They break down organic matter of dead bodies into inorganic material. p. 36

    Ecological Imbalance

    Disruption of an ecosystem's natural balance by natural or human-caused disturbances.

    • Wildlife conservation and prevention of environmental pollution help maintain balance. Cutting forest / deforestation adds to imbalance.
    • Deforestation: one of the main causes of ecological imbalance in India. Desertification, floods and famines, rainfall variations are other causes.
    • Lumbering disturbs the balance; social forestry, Vanamahotsav, afforestation do not.
    • Associated with balance: water management, afforestation, wildlife protection. Book's key: industrial management is not.
    • Causes of deforestation (figure): natural disasters; heavy demand of papers; conversion of forest into grazing land; conversion of forest land into agricultural land; urbanization; Jhum cultivation; wood cutting for commercial and industrial use; forest fire; lack of concept of sustainable development in policy making.
    Source check Book's note calls desertification, floods, famines and rainfall variations "secondary causes of deforestation" (p. 37), but its answer calls them "other causes of ecological imbalance" (p. 38). The answer's reading is used above.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name one main cause of ecological imbalance in India.

      Deforestation. p. 38

    2. Which disturbs balance: lumbering, social forestry, Vanamahotsav?

      Lumbering. p. 38

    3. List three of the book's causes of deforestation.

      Any three: heavy demand of papers, Jhum cultivation, urbanization, forest fire, conversion to grazing or agricultural land, wood cutting, natural disasters. p. 37

    Carrying Capacity

    The largest population an environment can sustain indefinitely.

    • Maximum population size of a species the environment can sustain indefinitely, given food, habitat, water and other necessities; the number of organisms an environment can sustainably support.
    • Population above carrying capacity pressures the ecosystem; excessive growth can lead to failure of the ecosystem.
    • Biotic potential: maximum reproductive capacity under optimum environmental conditions, denoted r. Carrying capacity is the limit beyond which growth cannot be sustained (K in the figure).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Define carrying capacity in one sentence.

      The maximum population size of a species that the environment can sustain indefinitely. p. 38

    2. Which letter denotes biotic potential?

      r. p. 38

    3. Fill the label: the dashed line in the S-curve is ___.

      K, the bearing (carrying) capacity. p. 38

    Food Web or Food Chain

    Energy passes from one living organism to another along a feeding sequence.

    • Food chain: order in which organisms feed on each other; usually only four to five links. Some animals feed on living organisms, some on dead organic matter (the latter = detritus food chain).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • COMMON TRAP Food chains are not found within the population of a species; a chain also does not show the number eaten.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Food = any substance consumed for nutritional support. Plants are self-feeding; animals depend on plants. Flow of energy through a chain is unidirectional.
    • Food chain: flow of carbon and energy within an area from the feeding sequence of a community.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Grass, goat, human: Grass (Autotroph), Goat (Herbivorous consumer), Humans (Omnivorous consumer).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Common marine chain: Diatom, Crustaceans, Herring.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    Producers, consumers, decomposers
    • Green plants = Primary Producers: autotrophs making complex organic compounds (carbohydrates, fats, proteins) generally using light; fundamental to the food chain of all ecosystems.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Trophic level-I = autotrophs / green plants, which fix solar energy for heterotrophs.
    • Primary consumer = herbivores; secondary = small carnivores; tertiary = large carnivores. Man is both primary and secondary consumer (plant products and meat).
    • Primary consumers (book's key): Deer (herbivorous) and Ants (book: decomposer, primary consumer and scavenger; eats plants, gets energy from cellulose). Tiger is carnivorous, fox omnivorous.
    • Phytoplankton (micro-algae): main marine primary producers; contain chlorophyll, need sunlight; most are buoyant and float in the upper ocean where sunlight penetrates; need nitrates, phosphates, sulfur, converted into proteins, fats, carbohydrates.
    • Decomposers: nature's recyclers, breaking organic matter of dead bodies. Organic matter = matter from living organisms. Mainly bacteria and fungi (yeasts, moulds); release compounds usable by producers. Virus not keyed as a decomposer.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Pond ecosystem: fungi, bacteria and flagellates act as decomposers; consumers are zooplankton, free-swimming and bottom-dwelling forms.
    • Autotroph-level production = primary productivity; heterotroph-level production = secondary productivity (herbivores are secondary producers).
    • Huge fall in butterflies: pollination of some plants adversely affected; lower food-chain member, meal of spider, bird, snake, so wasp, spider and bird populations could fall. Rise in fungal infections not keyed as a consequence (IAS 2017).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Book's "correct match": Green plants with Ecosystem (plants are living organisms, part of an ecosystem). Bacteria are biotic, not abiotic.
    Primary ProducerEcosystem
    PhytoplanktonOcean or deep water
    Rooted PlantsShallow water
    Grasses of different kind and forest plantsGrassland
    Crop PlantsCropland
    Added concept

    UPSC 2025 (reported official key; not in the book): marine phytoplankton and photosynthetic bacteria produce about 50% of the world's oxygen. Rain forests do not exceed the oceans, and well-oxygenated surface water does not hold several-fold more oxygen than air. See the Amazon check at → Ch 1.RECENT PYQ — 2025

    Source check M.P.P.C.S. 2024 (p. 41): book gives (c), decomposers, for fungi, bacteria, flagellates, and reports that the Madhya Pradesh Public Service Commission's final key accepted both (b) consumers and (c).
    Source check R.O./A.R.O. 2017 (p. 42): keyed "(*)". Book says producers and decomposers both act as intermediaries between biotic and abiotic components, so no single option is keyed.
    • Those who link biotic and abiotic components (book): producers (photosynthesis) and decomposers (recycling), together.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Reconstruct the grassland chain with the role of each member.

      Grass (producer), grasshopper (primary), frog (secondary), snake (tertiary consumer). p. 39

    2. Why can't a food chain exist within one species' population?

      That would mean a species feeding on itself, as with human eating human. p. 40

    3. Name the main primary producers in the ocean.

      Phytoplankton. p. 41

    4. Primary productivity is production at which level?

      Autotroph level. p. 42

    5. Fill the table: primary producer in cropland and in shallow water.

      Crop plants; rooted plants. p. 39

    Flow of Energy

    Energy enters by photosynthesis, moves one way, and diminishes at every trophic level.

    • Solar energy = primary source and driving force of an ecosystem.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Flow is unidirectional: enters plants through photosynthesis, passes along the chain, is lost as heat and does not return to plants; always lower (producer) to higher (herbivore, carnivore) level.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • 1st law of thermodynamics: energy can neither be created nor destroyed, only changed in form.
    • 2nd law: in energy conversion part is always lost as heat; product has less energy than its constituents.
    • Energy magnitude always decreases from one trophic level to the next (unusable heat lost at each level).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Ten percent law: Raymond Lindeman, first half of 1940s. Only about 10% of energy in organic food is stored as flesh at the next level; the rest is lost in transfer, respiration or incomplete digestion.
    • At every level (first, second, third, top) the consumer converts only 10% of accumulated energy into body weight. Book: herbivores can assimilate only 10% of gross productivity.
    Trophic Levels
    • Trophic levels are formed by organisms linked in a food chain, each level showing transfer of energy and nutrients; producers convert sunlight into chemical energy.
    • Book's four levels: First, Producer (autotrophs); Second, Primary consumers (herbivorous); Third, Secondary consumers (carnivorous); Fourth, Tertiary consumers (omnivorous). Decomposers feed on all levels.
    Source check Book's level table and key treat the highest level as omnivores (pp. 43, 45), but p. 42 puts "tertiary consumer (large carnivores)" at the fourth level and p. 39 calls tertiary consumers large carnivores. Keep "omnivores" only as the U.P. UDA/LDA 2002 key.
    Pyramids
    • Pyramid of energy: always upright, never inverted, since some energy is lost as heat at each step. Each bar shows energy at a level in a given time or annually per unit area.
    • Pyramid of Biomass: biomass per unit area at each trophic level. Grasslands and forests: decrease from producers to top carnivores, so upright.
    • Inverted in a pond (producers are small organisms, biomass least, increases towards the apex) and generally in marine ecosystem (biomass of fishes far exceeds phytoplankton).
    • Generally more producers than primary consumers, and more primary consumers than secondary consumers.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Why is energy flow unidirectional?

      Energy given out as heat is lost to the environment and does not return for plants to use. p. 43

    2. Who gave the Ten Percent Law and when?

      Raymond Lindeman, first half of 1940s. p. 43

    3. Which pyramid is always upright, and where is the biomass pyramid inverted?

      Energy pyramid; pond and marine ecosystems. p. 44, 45

    4. Fill the label: in the pond pyramid, plankton is ___ Kg and big fishes ___ Kg.

      5; 1000. p. 43

    Biological Magnification (Biomagnification)

    Concentration of a persistent toxin rises at successive trophic levels.

    • CORE CONCEPT Increase in concentration of toxic materials at successive trophic levels; substance is accumulated, cannot be metabolized or excreted, passes to next level. Well known for Mercury (Hg) and DDT.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’97
    • DDT: insecticide, non-biodegradable, deposited in fatty tissues; concentration increases from producers through primary and secondary to tertiary consumers. Highest of grasshopper, toad, snake, cattle = snake (tertiary consumer).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’97
    • Bionomics (Greek bio = life, nomos = law): comprehensive study of an organism and its relation to its environment; synonymous with Ecology; stresses value of natural systems which influence human systems; not related to the management of life.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Why does DDT build up along a food chain?

      It cannot be degraded biologically, metabolized or excreted, so each level receives what the one before stored. p. 45, 46

    2. Correct this: "Bionomics means management of life."

      It is the study of an organism and its relation to its environment, synonymous with ecology. p. 46

    3. Name two substances the book links with biomagnification.

      Mercury and DDT. p. 45

    Biogeochemical Cycle

    Chemical substances move through biotic and abiotic parts of Earth; communities themselves change in sequence.

    • Biogeochemical (nutritional) cycle: pathway by which a chemical substance moves through biotic and abiotic components of Earth; e.g. nitrogen, phosphorus, carbon.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Weathering of rocks is the main release route in the Phosphorus cycle: phosphate ion (PO₄³⁻) in water, soil, sediments; much is tied up in rock and sedimentary deposits, released by weathering, leaching, mining.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Passes through freshwater and terrestrial ecosystems via plants, grazers, predators, parasites; returned by death and decay. Much deposited in the sea, in shallow sediments or ocean deeps; back to land through fish harvests and guano deposited by seabirds.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    Ecological succession
    • Gradual process by which ecosystems change and develop over time.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Grassland: trees do not replace grasses because of water limits and fire. Past fires cleared trees and changed the ground composition; water washes away most soil, underlying ground becomes pioneer population replaced by grass; grass absorbs most water before it reaches shrub roots.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • F.E. Clements (book also prints F. Clement) studied wetland communities; gave a generally agreed definition in 1916: succession as development of the climax. E. Odum described biotic succession as a sequenced process.
    • Monoclimax theory: Clements (1936): every region has one climax community toward which all communities develop; climax is solely a function of climate.
    • Entire sequence of communities that successively change in an area = Sere.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Book notes biotic succession as displacement of one community by another in an area.
    • Order: Nudation, Migration, Ecesis, Reaction, Stabilization (U.P.P.C.S. 2024 asked it without Nudation).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Invasive species (book): non-native, spread widely in a community; high reproductive rates and better dispersal mechanisms (sentence is cut at the page break, p. 47–48).
    • Ecesis: early colonizers proliferate by germination, growth, reproduction; pioneer species survive the dispersal mechanisms; book: due to allogenic mechanisms alone.
    • Climax community: mature, self-sustaining, stable, final stage; best adapted to the region; structure likely continues until another disturbance. Physical and chemical conditions are altered and stabilized to support the entire community.
    • Ecological Transition: first used by John W. Bennett, The Ecological Transition: Cultural Anthropology and Human Adaptation. Development of an anthropocentric orientation to nature emerging in the Western Renaissance; growing incorporation of Nature into Culture and breakdown of local self-sufficiency (ability of a local group to satisfy needs with existing resources in a particular geographical range).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. For which cycle is weathering of rocks the main nutrient entry route?

      Phosphorus cycle. p. 47

    2. Sequence the five stages of biotic succession.

      Nudation, Migration, Ecesis, Reaction, Stabilization. p. 46

    3. What is a sere?

      The entire sequence of communities that successively change in an area. p. 48

    4. Explain why trees do not replace grasses in grassland.

      Water limits and fire: past fires cleared trees and grass takes water before shrub roots reach it. p. 47

    Environmental Movements

    Local struggles over forests and rivers shaped India's modern environmental movement.

    • Chipko: forest (plant) conservation movement started 1973. On 26 March 1974 Gaura Devi gathered women of her village in Garhwal Himalayas and stopped loggers by sticking to the trees; illiterate tribal and village women reclaiming traditional forest rights; non-violent; gave birth to the modern Indian environmental movement.
    • Slogan of Chipko: "Ecology is permanent economy".
    • Narmada Bachao Andolan: against construction of a multi-modal dam (book's words) on the Narmada river.
    • Appiko Movement: started in Karnataka for environmental protection.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Whose slogan was "Ecology is permanent economy"?

      Chipko Movement. p. 49

    2. Give the year Chipko started and the date of Gaura Devi's action.

      1973; 26 March 1974. p. 48

    3. Match Appiko to its State.

      Karnataka. p. 48

    Ecological Footprint and Millennium Ecosystem Assessment

    Ways of measuring what people demand from ecosystems and what ecosystems provide.

    • Ecological footprint: measure of human demand on Earth's ecosystems, contrasted with the planet's ecological capacity to regenerate; biologically productive area needed to provide everything people need to sustain life (minimum land area for a person's life).
    • Includes fruits, vegetables, wood, fibres, fossil fuel use, release of carbon dioxide. Unit: Global hectares. Book's figure: emission from Motor vehicle, Sewage, Electric energy, Water-use.
    • Social Cost of Carbon: monetary value of long-term damage done by a tonne of CO₂ emission in a given year; EPA (Environmental Protection Agency) and other US agencies use it to evaluate climate impacts of rule making.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • According to World Development Report 2010, Millennium Ecosystem Assessment describes five groups of ecosystem services. Nutrient recycling and crop pollination are examples.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Supporting services: necessary for production of all others; impacts on people indirect or over a very long time; e.g. soil formation and retention, nutrient cycling, crop pollination, water cycling.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. In what unit is ecological footprint measured?

      Global hectares. p. 49

    2. What does Social Cost of Carbon measure?

      In monetary value, long-term damage from a tonne of CO₂ emitted in a given year. p. 49

    3. Match: production of food and water; maintenance of diversity; control of climate and disease.

      Provisioning; preserving; regulating. p. 50

    Ecosystem: Some Facts for Exam

    High-frequency terms, laws, and marine facts collected at the end of the chapter.

    Niche and ecotone
    • Ecological niche: position of a species in an ecosystem, comprising habitat requirements and functional role. Term by Joseph Grinnell, 1917, who called it Micro-Habitats; "the ultimate distributional unit of species and subspecies".PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Range of conditions tolerated, resources used, functional role; each species has a distinct niche; subsumes all interactions with biotic and abiotic environment.
    • If resources are abundant more species can coexist; species are fewer when resources are insufficient, since competition for food, space and other essentials restricts them.
    • Ecotone: region of transition between two biological communities; steep transition along an environmental or other gradient. Recognition of a transition zone by Clements could be called the beginning of ecotone research.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    Aquatic facts
    • Lentic: static water habitats: ponds, lakes, swamps, marshes, ditches; ponds may be temporary, lakes large.
    • Lotic: flowing waters: rivers, streams, brooks, springs; higher oxygen level from continually moving current; clearer water.
    • Decreasing productivity: Mangrove, Grasslands, Lake, Ocean. Book: more diversity, more productivity.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’13 · 2 papers
    • Pycnocline: density gradient. Halocline: salt gradient. Thermocline: change in temperature with depth.
    • Oceanic upwelling: winds push surface water away; colder, nutrient-rich water rises to replace it (book p. 51 "Oceanic outflow": warm, nutrient-less surface water replaced by cold, nutrient-rich water by air flow). Brings nutrients to the surface and raises productivity, hence good fishing grounds.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Corals: invertebrate animals; reefs are built by hard corals secreting a skeleton (Australia's 'Great Barrier Reef').PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Most reefs lie in tropical oceans near the equator; more than one-third are in the territories of Australia, Indonesia, Philippines.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Occupy less than 1% of ocean floor, yet host more than 25% of known marine fish species; 32 of 34 recognized animal phyla on coral reefs versus 9 in tropical rainforests.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Added concept

    UPSC 2026 asked why mangroves matter for climate resilience. Coaching-consensus answer, not an official key: they withstand tidal surges and offer biomass, acting as natural bio-shields and livelihood bases. Claims of freshwater storage for paddy and of salt-sensitive roots filtering seawater were rejected. Not in the book.RECENT PYQ — 2026

    Eucalyptus, biomes and economics
    • Eucalyptus: 'Environmental evil' / 'enemy of the environment'. Consumes more ground water than other trees; water level goes down around plantation.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • Mostly found in Australia; cultivated widely in Europe, Africa, Indian sub-continent. Recommended for poor soils with deficient moisture where little else grows.
    • Human-made biome: crop land.
    • Chhattisgarh: India's first State linking forest ecosystem services (clean air, water conservation, biodiversity) with Green GDP.
    • Green GDP: concept coined in late 1980s; from Net Domestic Product (GDP minus depreciation of produced assets such as machines and buildings), deduct cost of depletion of natural resources and degradation of ecosystems.
    • 'Deep ecology': Arne Naess, Norwegian philosopher, early 1970s.

    ActYear

    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's dated name: Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980 (p. 51). Section 3 of the Forest (Conservation) Amendment Act, 2023 (No. 15 of 2023, assent 4 August 2023) substitutes the short title "Van (Sanrakshan Evam Samvardhan) Adhiniyam". source

    PermittedOngoing agriculture and horticulture by local communities; rainwater harvesting; organic farming; green technology; renewable energy sources
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the decreasing-productivity order of mangrove, ocean, lake, grassland.

      Mangrove, grasslands, lake, ocean. p. 51

    2. Distinguish pycnocline, halocline and thermocline.

      Density, salt and temperature-with-depth gradients. p. 51

    3. Under which Act are eco-sensitive zones mentioned, and is rainwater harvesting allowed there?

      Environment (Protection) Act, 1986; yes, it is a permitted activity. p. 55

    4. Correct this: "More species can coexist in a niche when resources are insufficient."

      Species are fewer when resources are insufficient; abundance allows more to coexist. p. 53

    5. How many of the 34 recognized animal phyla are found on coral reefs, and in tropical rainforests?

      32 and 9. p. 55
