    Biodiversity

    Biodiversity: Meaning and Levels

    Variety of living organisms in an ecosystem, measured at three spatial scales and organised at three levels.

    • Biodiversity = diverse range of organisms (plants & animals) in an ecosystem; species organised into communities found in a particular ecosystem.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • CORE CONCEPT Most significant aspect = maintenance of the ecosystem. Functions at levels of species, communities, ecosystems.
    • α, β, γ classification described by Whittaker, 1960.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Species richness = measure of species found in a community.
    Origin of the term
    • "Biological diversity": Raymond F. Dasmann, book A Different Kind of Country, 1968.
    • COMMON TRAP "Biodiversity" (contraction of "biological diversity"): first coined 1985 by Walter G. Rosen, National Research Council, as title word for a seminar on biological diversity.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Days, decade and targets
    • International Day for Biological Diversity (IDB) = 22 May (UN); to increase understanding and awareness of biodiversity issues.
    • History: late 1993, Second Committee of UNGA first designated 29 December (date CBD entered into force, 29 Dec 1993). December 2000: UNGA adopted 22 May, marking adoption of CBD text on 22 May 1992 by the Nairobi Final Act. Partly because many holidays around 29 Dec made celebrations hard to plan. Book: many countries still celebrate 29 Dec.
    • UN Decade on Biodiversity = 2011–20 (UN General Assembly Resolution 65/161, 22 December 2010). Goal: mainstream biodiversity at different levels; governments encouraged to develop, implement and report national strategies under the Strategic Plan for Biodiversity.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • India's Land Degradation Neutrality (LDN) target: 2030. LDN balances expected loss of productive land with recovery of degraded areas; a paradigm shift in land management.
    • India's first National Centre for Marine Biodiversity (NCMB): Jamnagar, Gujarat; goal: safeguard biodiversity of coastal areas.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Q0341 (R.A.S. 2012): options included 29 December but not 22 May; book marks the answer "(*)" (p. 90) and gives 22 May as the current date. Do not treat 29 Dec as the current IDB.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. A student counts bird species along transects in a wood and in a field, then compares the two counts. Which diversity index does each step give?

      Each count = alpha; the comparison = beta. Gamma would need the whole region. p. 56

    2. Separate the contributions of Dasmann and Rosen.

      Dasmann used "biological diversity" (1968). Rosen coined the contraction "biodiversity" (1985). p. 56

    3. Why did the UN move the biodiversity day away from 29 December?

      Holiday clashes made it hard to plan events, so in 2000 UNGA chose 22 May, the date the CBD text was adopted in 1992. p. 90

    4. Fill the three levels of biodiversity and what each describes.

      Genetic (gene variation), species (among different species), community & ecosystem (found in a region). p. 56

    Biodiversity Gradient

    Biodiversity is uneven: it rises towards the equator and towards lower altitudes.

    • Not uniform on Earth; depends on climate, soil, temperature, rainfall, humidity, and on precipitation, altitude, geography and presence of other species.
    • Maximum biodiversity: tropical rain forest (book: 23.5°N–23.5°S, near the equator). Heavy rain + high temperature all year, so also called the optimum biome.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’94 · 5 papers
    • Biodiversity increases towards the equator; varies greatly across the globe and within regions.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • CORE CONCEPT Latitudinal diversity gradient (LDG): species number increases from poles to Equator; recognised for over a century, reasons long a mystery. New study: higher equatorial diversity may come from higher speciation rates, driven by changes in precipitation across space and time.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Left standing by the key: tropics harbour more species than temperate areas; greatest biodiversity in Amazonian rain forest; diversity falls from temperate areas towards poles. False statement was the reverse gradient (rising towards poles).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Normally greater at lower latitudes than higher, and at lower altitudes than higher along mountain gradients (valleys richer than high mountains).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Book's figures: on Earth, low biodiversity at the poles, high at the equator (p. 58). On a mountain, high at the base, low at the top, with temperature falling 6.5°C per km of height (p. 59).
    • Himalaya rich in species mainly because it is a confluence of different biogeographical zones.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    Source check

    U.P.P.C.S. 2018 (higher species diversity): book records key "(b & c)". Tropical rain forest on land and coral reefs in the sea are both among the most diverse systems, and UPPSC accepted both (p. 59).

    Silent Valley and Valley of Flowers
    Silent ValleyValley of Flowers
    LocationKerala (Palakkad, Malappuram); part of Western GhatsChamoli district, Uttarakhand; ~3,600 mREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    StatusNP declared 1984, formally inaugurated 1985; last remaining rain forest of Kerala; now in the biosphere reserve listNP (87 sq km), UNESCO World Heritage Site; one of two core zones of Nanda Devi BR (other: Nanda Devi NP)REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    DetailRichest biodiversity among Q's options. Core 89.52 sq km; 148 sq km added as buffer in 2007Over 600 flower species: orchids, poppies, primulas, marigold, daisies, anemonesREPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    Note'Silent Valley Project' (Kerala): people's movement saved moist evergreen forest from a hydroelectric projectFauna: gray langur, flying squirrel, Himalayan weasel, black bear, red fox, lime butterfly, snow leopardREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Correct this: "Species diversity rises from the equator towards the poles."

      Reverse it. Species number rises from the poles to the Equator (LDG). p. 60

    2. Why is the tropical rain forest called the optimum biome?

      Heavy rain and high temperature all year suit growth of flora and fauna. p. 58

    3. Give the most appropriate reason the Himalaya is species-rich.

      Confluence of different biogeographical zones. p. 61

    4. Valley of Flowers is one of two core zones of which biosphere reserve, and what is the other core?

      Nanda Devi BR; other core = Nanda Devi NP. p. 60

    Biodiversity Hotspots

    Regions that are both exceptionally rich in endemics and severely threatened. Concept: Norman Myers.

    • Hotspot: biogeographical region, extremely biologically diverse and under severe threat (habitat loss, climate change, extensive species loss). Rich in endemic, rare, threatened species in relatively small areas; protection there might prevent extinction of more species than in similar-sized areas elsewhere.
    • CORE CONCEPT To qualify (Conservation International): at least 1,500 vascular plant species as endemics and at least 70% of original habitat lost. Criteria behind Western Ghats–Sri Lanka and Indo-Burma recognition: species richness, endemism, threat perception.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Globally 36 hotspots (Conservation International): just 2.5% of land surface, but over 50% of endemic plant species and nearly 43% of bird, mammal, reptile, amphibian species as endemics.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • February 2016: CEPF declared North American Coastal Plains the 36th hotspot.
    • India: nearly 7.6% of mammals, 12.6% birds, 6.2% reptiles, 4.4% amphibians (book: of those found in 17 mega-diverse countries). Western Ghats = "biodiversity saturated" area among Q's options.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Hotspots are not confined to tropics: temperate examples California Floristic Province, Japan.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    • Mediterranean Basin (not "Eastern Mediterranean"): Portugal to Jordan, northern Italy to Morocco; second-largest hotspot; many unique reptiles, key for endemic plants. Southwest Australia (not "North-western").PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’09
    • Sikkim: full-fledged State by 36th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1975. "Botanist's paradise" as it lies under E. Himalaya hotspot. People mainly Lepchas, Bhotias, Nepalis.
    Source check Book prints "1,500 species of vascular plants (70.5% of the world's total)" (p. 64). This is a misprint: 1,500 species cannot be 70.5% of the world's plants. Usually quoted as 0.5%; CEPF's page could not be opened to confirm, so verify before quoting a percentage.
    Common trap

    Not among India's four: Western Himalaya, Eastern Ghats, Vindhyas, Aravalli Hills, Gangetic and Indo-Gangetic plains, Sundarbans, Thar Desert, Central India, Deccan Plateau, Naro Hills. "Andaman and Nicobar" is not one hotspot: the groups fall in two.

    Source check Q0238 (I.A.S. 1996, 'ecological hot spot'): book records key "(b & c)", i.e. Eastern Himalayas and Western Ghats both (p. 64).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. State the two numerical thresholds for hotspot status.

      At least 1,500 endemic vascular plants; at least 70% of original habitat lost. p. 64

    2. Which Indian island group belongs to which hotspot?

      Andaman: Indo-Burma. Nicobar: Sundaland. p. 63

    3. Correct this: "Hotspots occur only in the tropics."

      False. Temperate hotspots include California Floristic Province and Japan. p. 62

    4. Name the three criteria the book links to the recognition of the Western Ghats–Sri Lanka and Indo-Burma hotspots.

      Species richness, endemism, threat perception. p. 65

    Causes of Biodiversity Loss

    Human influence on ecosystems is the root cause; habitat destruction leads every list.

    • CORE CONCEPT Main cause: destruction of natural habitat. Transforming natural areas loses plant species and the animal species tied to them. Forests, swamps, plains, lakes cleared for agriculture, housing, roads, pipelines.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Habitat destruction = degradation or complete elimination of natural environment. Loss of vegetation (deforestation, conversion for agriculture or urban use) removes food, shelter, breeding grounds, leading to decline or extinction.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Habitat examples: cutting forests for fields, filling wetlands for houses, dams changing river flow.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Influence of humans is the main driver. Threats summarised: alteration and loss of habitats; introduction of exotic species and GMOs; pollution; climate change; overexploitation.
    • Five main threats commonly recognised: invasive alien species, climate change, nutrient loading and pollution, habitat change, overexploitation.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Book's figure of threats (p. 65): habitat loss and degradation; "rehabilitation of exotic species"; climate change and pollution; extinction of species, which may be natural, mass or human-induced.
    • Book's six main threats: human activities and habitat loss; deforestation; desertification; marine pollution; increasing wildlife trade; climate change.
    • Jhum / shifting cultivation (eastern and north-eastern India): extravagant, unscientific land use; devastating, far-reaching degradation.
    • Global warming, habitat fragmentation, alien species invasion = threats. Promotion of vegetarianism is not.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’12 · 2 papers
    • India's birds declining: habitat reduction; pesticides, chemical fertilizers, mosquito repellents. DDT enters food chain, shells crack prematurely, population falls.
    • Uttarakhand loss factors: expansion of roads, urbanization, extension of agriculture. Afforestation of barren land is not a cause.
    • COMMON TRAP Fewer trophic levels indicates abundance of one type of organism, so biodiversity falls. Productivity, intermediate-disturbance and ecosystem age add richness.
    • Genetic assimilation (a wrong option, not a threat cited): a phenotypic character at first produced only in response to an environmental influence is, by selection, taken over by the genotype, so it forms even without that influence.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Explain why habitat destruction removes animals as well as plants.

      Animals lose the plants that give food, shelter and breeding grounds, so their species decline too. pp. 65, 67

    2. Relate DDT to falling bird numbers in one sentence.

      DDT passes up the food chain and shells crack prematurely, so fewer birds hatch. p. 65

    3. Which of productivity, ecosystem age, intermediate disturbance or fewer trophic levels does not add richness?

      Fewer trophic levels. p. 67

    Conservation of Biodiversity

    In-situ keeps species in their natural home; ex-situ moves them out. Biosphere reserves are the flagship in-situ strategy.

    Types of conservation
    • In-situ: conserving animals and plants in natural habitats. Examples: National Parks, Sanctuaries, Biosphere Reserves, Reserved Forest, Protected Forest, Nature Reserves. Genetic diversity in NPs and sanctuaries is maintained in-situ.
    • Ex-situ ('off site'): conserving outside areas of natural occurrence, moving target species from native habitat. Examples: seed banks, botanical gardens, zoological parks. COMMON TRAP Botanical garden is not in-situ; biosphere reserve, NP, sanctuary and sacred grove are in-situ.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’11 · 2 papers
    • Two ex-situ methods. (1) Primary facilities holding collections: aquaria, botanical gardens, zoos.
    • (2) Storage of seeds, pollen, tissue, embryos in liquid nitrogen (cryo-bank): virtually indefinite storage without deterioration over a much longer period.
    • In-situ and ex-situ techniques are both adopted for conservation.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Book's Q0258 explanation (p. 69) says ex-situ means "restricting them to natural habitat". This contradicts the book's own definition on p. 67 (outside the areas where they naturally occur). Use p. 67.
    Biosphere reserves
    • CORE CONCEPT Biosphere reserves = most important strategy for conserving biodiversity together with traditional human life (cultural diversity). Areas of terrestrial and coastal ecosystems reconciling conservation with sustainable use; an integral part of the human system; multifaceted ecosystem development given prominence.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’14 · 2 papers
    • Ecosystem with plants and animals of unusual scientific and natural interest; helps protect genetic variations of organisms and plants.
    • Objectives: (a) conserve landscapes, ecosystems, species, genetic variations; (b) foster socio-culturally and ecologically sustainable economic and human development; (c) support research, monitoring, education, information exchange on local, national, global issues.
    • India: 18 Biosphere Reserves, protecting larger areas of natural habitat. 12 of 18 in the World Network (UNESCO MAB Programme list): Nilgiri, Gulf of Mannar, Sundarbans, Nanda Devi, Nokrek, Pachmarhi, Simlipal, Great Nicobar, Achanakmar-Amarkantak, Agasthyamala, Khangchendzonga, Panna.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’14 · 2 papers
    • Gulf of Mannar BR: world's richest region from a marine biodiversity perspective; 21 islands with estuaries, beaches, nearshore forests, marine component with algal communities, sea grasses, coral reefs, salt marshes, mangroves. → Ch 7REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check The 18 and 12 are the book's undated counts (pp. 68, 69). A November 2026 search suggested a later UNESCO addition, but no page could be opened to confirm it. Check the current World Network list before quoting.
    Coral reefs
    • Four major regions: Lakshadweep, Gulf of Mannar, Gulf of Kachchh, Andaman & Nicobar Islands. Sundarbans is known for mangrove forest, not reefs.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Book's two dated areas: about 5,790 km² (2008); about 2,384 km² (2019). Keep each with its year. Reefs deteriorating worldwide, India no exception.
    • Fringing reefs common: Gulf of Mannar, Gulf of Kachchh, Andaman & Nicobar. Atoll reefs: Lakshadweep.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Coral bleaching: warmer water makes corals expel the algae (zooxanthellae) in their tissues, so coral turns completely white. Most effective factor: rise in sea-water temperature / global warming.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Book's p. 68 list of bleaching causes: increase in temperature and acidity of sea, global warming, atmospheric pressure.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Sort as in-situ or ex-situ: sacred grove, botanical garden, cryo-bank, national park.

      In-situ: sacred grove, national park. Ex-situ: botanical garden, cryo-bank. pp. 67–69

    2. Why are biosphere reserves preferred when traditional human life must also continue?

      They reconcile conservation with sustainable use and treat people as an integral part of the system. pp. 68–69

    3. Explain coral bleaching in one sentence.

      Overheated corals expel zooxanthellae and turn white. p. 70

    4. Which reef type is characteristic of Lakshadweep?

      Atoll reefs. p. 68

    Uses of Biodiversity

    Biodiversity underpins human existence directly and through ecosystem services.

    • Basis for human existence: soil formation, prevention of soil erosion, recycling of waste, pollination of crops. Also listed for medicinal purposes.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. List four ways biodiversity underpins human existence as stated in the book.

      Soil formation, prevention of soil erosion, recycling of waste, pollination of crops. p. 71

    2. Name the drugs and plant product listed under pharmaceuticals in the figure.

      Quinine, Taxol, Botanochemical, Morphine. p. 70

    3. Under which branch does "climate control by forests and seas" fall?

      Ecosystem services. p. 70

    IUCN

    A membership union, not a UN organ, whose Red List tracks extinction risk.

    • COMMON TRAP IUCN (created 1948) is a membership union of public, private and non-governmental organisations; not part of the UN. Runs a large portfolio of conservation field projects worldwide.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Works for nature conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. The only international observer in UNGA with expertise on environment, specifically biodiversity, nature conservation and sustainable resource use.
    • CITES: legally binding on States that join it, but does not take the place of national laws (statement left correct by key).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Red Data Books (IUCN) list threatened plant and animal species. Endangered species are listed there. They are not lists of endemics of hotspots or of protected sites.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’11 · 5 papers
    • IUCN Red List: established 1964; world's most comprehensive source on global extinction risk status of animal, fungus and plant species.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Called the Barometer of Life: as a barometer measures atmospheric pressure, the List measures pressure on species, guiding action to prevent extinctions.
    • Benefits of the Red List (figure, p. 71): raises awareness of endangered species; identifies and records them; gives in-situ direction to conservation work; forms a written list of loss of biodiversity.
    • Great Indian Bustard: highly endangered among Q's options.
    • Book's IUCN definition: species not seen during last 50 years is considered extinct.
    Source check The 50-year test (p. 73) is the book's answer. IUCN's own page was blocked, so it could not be checked; current Red List wording is generally described as "no reasonable doubt that the last individual has died", without a fixed period. Treat 50 years as the exam key only.
    Source check Q0276 (I.A.S. 2012): key (a) groups Great Indian Bustard, Musk Deer, Red Panda and Asiatic Wild Ass as Endangered "at the time of the question" (p. 72). The book's 2015 statuses differ (table above). Keep the two apart.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Correct this: "IUCN is an organ of the United Nations."

      False. It is a membership union, not part of the UN. p. 71

    2. Why is the Red List called the Barometer of Life?

      It measures pressure on species as a barometer measures atmospheric pressure. p. 72

    3. State the book's status for Musk Deer and Wild Ass on the June 2015 list.

      Musk Deer: Vulnerable. Wild Ass: Near Threatened. p. 71

    Biodiversity and Endangered Species

    Case studies and range facts: know each species' place and threat.

    Vultures and diclofenac
    • Cause of vulture decline: Diclofenac Sodium given to diseased cattle. May 2003: Peregrine Fund scientist's paper. January 2004: joint study with Ornithological Society of Pakistan confirmed it as primary reason.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’12 · 5 papers
    Species case notes
    • Dodo: giant bird, short stumpy legs, large hooked beak, on Mauritius; vanished by end of 17th century. Helped propagate seed of Tambalacoque (Dodo plant); seed eaten, dispersed via faecal matter. Without Dodo the tree failed to reproduce.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’98
    • Indian flying fox (Pteropus giganteus) = bat, greater Indian fruit bat, family Pteropodidae. Bangladesh, China, India, Maldives, Nepal, Pakistan, Sri Lanka.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’09
    • Dugong ('Sea Cow'): herbivorous marine mammal, vulnerable; feeds on seagrass in sheltered waters, lagoons, bays. Eastern Africa, South & Southeast Asia, Australia. Schedule I, Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972. India: Gulf of Mannar, Palk Bay/Strait; also Saurashtra coast of Gulf of Kachchh, Andaman & Nicobar. Not along the entire coast.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’09, ’15
    • Indian wild ass: Rann of Kachchh (sandy, saline). No predators there; threatened by habitat destruction.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Prosopis juliflora: exotic plant; wipes out most native plants and animals around it; reduces biodiversity where it grows.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Lion-tailed macaque: endangered; Western Ghats, mostly Kerala, Karnataka, border region of Tamil Nadu. Not Andhra Pradesh.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • COMMON TRAP Cheetah declared extinct in India 1952, so not "naturally found" at question time (2012). Book adds: Supreme Court earlier withheld the rehabilitation plan; government later introduced it in Madhya Pradesh from Namibia.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Snow leopard: habitat 3000–4500 m; surveys in Uttarakhand, Himachal Pradesh confirm presence. Black-necked crane: book gives as State bird of J&K.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Flying squirrel: India, Russia, Japan, China, Europe, Africa, North America. South Asia high diversity: about 17 species, 7 genera. In India most in eastern Himalaya and north-east, at Himalayan and Indo-Malayan confluence. Arunachal Pradesh study: 14 species; red giant most common, then particoloured (Hylopetes alboniger).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Ceylon frogmouth (Batrachostomus moniliger), Coppersmith barbet (Megalaima haemacephala), Grey-chinned minivet (Pericrocotus solaris), White-throated redstart (Phoenicurus schisticeps): all birds.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • Andaman & Nicobar: saltwater crocodile, whales, dolphins, dugong, marine turtles, sea snake, fish, prawns, lobsters, corals, seashells including rare, endangered Trochus. Key: shrew and tapir are not of Western Ghats' Malabar region (book places them in Himalayan ranges).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Red Panda (Ailurus fulgens): territorial; temperate deciduous and coniferous forests with bamboo understory and hollow trees; key species, indicator of forest health. Himalaya: Nepal, Bhutan, Myanmar; India: Sikkim, West Bengal, Meghalaya, Arunachal Pradesh. Most world population in China; most Indian ones in Arunachal Pradesh. Not western Himalaya only.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’07
    • Slow Loris: dense forests of NE India (Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, Manipur, Meghalaya, Mizoram, Nagaland, Tripura); also Bangladesh, Cambodia, China, Myanmar (incl. Mergui Archipelago), Thailand, Vietnam except south.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’07
    • Mahseer, dolphin, cat: Blue-finned Mahseer: Cauvery River. Irrawaddy Dolphin: sea coasts, estuaries, rivers of Bay of Bengal and SE Asia, and Chilika Lake; not Chambal (Gangetic Dolphin there). Rusty-spotted Cat: Eastern Ghats.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Indri (Indri indri, babakoto): among largest living lemurs, endemic to Madagascar (NE coast rainforests), diurnal, CR. Elk (Cervus canadensis, wapiti): second largest of deer family Cervidae; North America, Central and East Asia; not Indonesia. Bonobo (Pan paniscus, pygmy chimpanzee): endangered great ape, Congo Basin (Democratic Republic of the Congo). None was correctly matched to Brazil/Indonesia/Madagascar in the Q.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’24
    Added concept Recent papers, not in the book. Coaching keys are not official.
    • Peacock (Gooty) tarantula, Prelims 2025, reported official key II and III: natural habitat in India limited to some forest areas; arboreal in its natural habitat. "Omnivorous crustacean" was not accepted.RECENT PYQ — 2025

    Source check Tambalacoque: p. 73 says the plant has "been extinct"; p. 75 only says it failed to reproduce. Stick to "failed to reproduce". The p. 73–74 note also mentions pollination, while the mechanism it describes is seed dispersal.

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Reconstruct the diclofenac chain from cattle to ban.

      Treated cattle die → vultures eat carcasses → kidney poisoning → population crash → DCGI bans veterinary use. pp. 73–75

      False. Gulf of Mannar and Palk Bay mainly, plus Saurashtra coast and Andaman & Nicobar. pp. 74–75

    4. Why was the cheetah excluded in a 2012 "naturally found" question?

    Ramsar Convention

    Convention on Wetlands, signed 2 February 1971 at Ramsar, Iran. Wise use, not exclusion.

    • Associated with conservation and sustainable use of wetlands; also called the Convention on Wetlands.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • World Wetlands Day = 2 February: date of adoption in 1971 at Ramsar, on the shores of the Caspian Sea. Raises awareness of the vital role of wetlands for people and planet. Themes: 2020 'Wetlands and Biodiversity'; 2023 'Wetlands Restoration'; 2024 'Wetlands and Human Wellbeing'; 2025 'Protecting Wetlands for Our Common Future' (need for immediate action, for all people and future generations).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Ramsar Site = wetland designated of international importance, protected under strict Ramsar guidelines.
    • CORE CONCEPT India, a party, must conserve Ramsar sites through an ecosystem approach and allow their simultaneous sustainable use.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    • Montreux Record: register of Ramsar-listed sites where changes in ecological character have occurred, are occurring or are likely to occur from technological developments, pollution or other human interference. Maintained as part of the Ramsar list.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • India: 89 sites as of January 2025 (book). Four then added: Sakkarakottai and Therthangal Bird Sanctuaries (Tamil Nadu), Khachoedpalri Wetland (Sikkim), Udhwa Lake (Jharkhand).
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's position: 89 sites (January 2025, pp. 77, 80). Reported total on 25 October 2026: 103, after Sukhna Wetland (Chandigarh) and Sauj Jheel (Uttar Pradesh) were designated on 15 October 2026. Secondary report citing PIB and Ramsar.org; the table below stays the book's 89. source

    Wetland functions and Indian rules
    • Wetlands hold water like a sponge, prevent flooding, keep river levels normal, filter and purify surface water; take water in floods, release slowly at low levels; release vegetative matter that feeds fish; rejuvenate rivers and surrounding ecosystems. Useful for nutrient recovery and cycling, heavy-metal uptake by plants, reducing river siltation by retaining sediments.
    • CORE CONCEPT Wetlands = Earth's kidneys: aquatic plants absorb heavy metals and excess nutrients. Plants like verticillata and water hyacinth remove heavy metals from waste water.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Ecosystem services: water storage, purification, flood mitigation, storm buffers, erosion control, aquifer recharge, microclimate regulation, aesthetic value, recreational, social, cultural activities. Vital parts of hydrological cycle; highly productive.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Hydrophytes thrive with fluctuating water thanks to aerenchyma: air channels in leaves, stems, roots give buoyancy and a low-resistance pathway for oxygen, carbon dioxide, ethylene between aerial and submerged tissues.
    • Q0315, I.A.S. 2012: key (a) "at the time when question was asked": Gujarat has largest wetland share. Now: inland 69.23%, coastal 27.13%, small wetlands (<2.25 ha) 3.64%. Gujarat 17.6%, West Bengal second. Coastal does not exceed inland.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Not mandatory under Ramsar for GoI to protect all wetlands in India. Wetlands (Conservation and Management) Rules, 2010: framed on recommendation of an Expert Panel under National Environment Policy 2006, not on Ramsar recommendations; cover drainage area or catchment as determined by the authority.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Wetlands (C&M) Rules, 2017 moved management focus from central authority to State bodies.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give date and place of the Convention on Wetlands.

      2 February 1971, Ramsar, Iran. p. 78

    2. Relate the Montreux Record to the Ramsar List.

      It is part of the Ramsar list, registering sites whose ecological character has changed or is likely to, through human interference. p. 79

    3. Why are wetlands called the Earth's kidneys?

      Aquatic plants absorb heavy metals and excess nutrients. p. 81

    4. Correct this: "The 2010 Wetlands Rules were framed on Ramsar recommendations."

      False. They followed an Expert Panel under National Environment Policy 2006. p. 82

    5. Which tissue lets hydrophytes survive fluctuating water levels?

      Aerenchyma (air channels). p. 81

    Various Acts and Agreements

    India's biodiversity law flows from the CBD; other treaties and NGOs act alongside it.

    Biological Diversity Act and NBA
    • Biological Diversity Act, 2002: passed by Parliament December 2002; President's assent February 2003. Preservation of biological diversity; equitable sharing of benefits from traditional biological resources and knowledge.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Covers conservation, use of biological resources and associated knowledge; framework for access and benefit sharing; includes transfer of research results and application for Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) relating to Indian biological resources.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • NBA: established 2003, HQ Chennai; statutory autonomous body under MoEFCC. Checks biopiracy, protects indigenous and traditional genetic resources; IPR application on genetic/biological resources needs its approval.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • COMMON TRAP GM crop research is monitored by the Genetic Engineering Appraisal Committee (GEAC), under MoEFCC, not NBA.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • BMCs (Biodiversity Management Committees): constituted in local bodies under the Act and State rules. Key to realising the Nagoya Protocol (2010), under which benefits from genetic resources and associated traditional knowledge reach indigenous and local communities on mutually agreed terms. May levy collection fees for commercial access within jurisdiction.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • The Act gives effect to India's commitments under CBD (1992); three goals: conservation of biological diversity; sustainable use of its components; fair and equitable sharing of benefits.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • CEBPOL (Centre for Biodiversity Policy and Law): India with Norwegian Government, at NBA Chennai; builds professional expertise and capacity in biodiversity policies and laws.
    Treaties and NGOs
    • Cartagena Protocol on Biosafety (to CBD): safe handling, transport, use of living modified organisms (LMO) from modern biotechnology; covers biodiversity and risks to human health. Adopted 29 January 2000; in force 11 September 2003.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05, ’09
    • India signed 23 January 2001 (ratified 2003); implemented by Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change. MOP 1 (First Meeting of the Parties): 23–27 February 2004, Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia. USA is not a member.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05, ’09
    • IT PGRFA ('International Seed Treaty'): in harmony with CBD; food security through conservation, exchange, sustainable use of plant genetic resources. UN Convention to Combat Desertification and World Heritage Convention also bear on biodiversity.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • BirdLife International: world's largest global partnership of non-governmental conservation organisations; conserves birds, habitats, global biodiversity. Book: over 115 national partners; Friends of BirdLife raises funds for a partnership of 119 organisations in more than 100 countries. Identifies Important Bird and Biodiversity Areas: over 13,000 so far.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • COMMON TRAP Hotspot concept: British environmentalist Norman Myers, not BirdLife.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • TRAFFIC (Trade Records Analysis of Fauna and Flora in Commerce): NGO, established 1976, strategic alliance of WWF and IUCN; not a UNEP bureau. Mission: trade in wild plants and animals should not threaten nature conservation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Ecuador's referendum rejected oil drilling in Yasuni National Park (Amazon); UNESCO biosphere reserve since 1989.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Which body supervises GM crop research: NBA or GEAC?

      GEAC. NBA handles access, benefit sharing and IPR approval. p. 84

    2. State the three objectives of the CBD.

      Conservation; sustainable use of components; fair and equitable benefit sharing. p. 83

    3. Who originated the hotspot concept?

      Norman Myers. p. 84

    4. Correct this: "TRAFFIC is a UNEP bureau."

      False. It is an NGO alliance of WWF and IUCN. p. 84

    Biodiversity: United Nations and Other International Organizations

    CBD COPs by venue, with UN-REDD, TEEB and Wetlands International.

    • UN-REDD (2008): FAO, UNDP, UNEP; book also says under the aegis of UNFCCC member countries. Mitigates climate change by reducing net GHG emissions through enhanced forest management in developing countries. REDD+ focuses on conservation and sustainable management of forests.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Well-designed REDD+ can significantly help protect biodiversity, maintain resilience of forest ecosystems, and reduce poverty via technical and financial support.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • TEEB (The Economics of Ecosystems and Biodiversity): "making nature's values visible". Mainstreams biodiversity and ecosystem service values into decision-making at all levels; helps decision makers recognize, demonstrate, capture value. Launched by Germany and EU, March 2007; led by Pavan Sukhdev 2007–2011.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Wetlands International: independent, not-for-profit global organisation, Government and NGO membership; not intergovernmental. Head office Netherlands. Leading scientific expert on peatlands and climate change, and on wetlands and waterbird migration.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Sustains and restores wetlands for people and biodiversity. Work: research, community-based field projects, advocacy with governments, corporates, international policy fora, conventions. Partnerships, specialist expert network, tens of thousands of volunteers.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    Added concept Recent papers, not in the book.
    • Nature Restoration Law, to tackle climate change and biodiversity loss: enacted by the European Union (Prelims 2025, reported official key).RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • Mains 2026 GS-III asked: aim and goals of the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework; India's commitments and initiatives, with examples. No key exists. The book gives only COP 15's dates and venues.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Sequence these CBD COP venues: Cali, Hyderabad, Nagoya, Cancun.

      Nagoya (10), Hyderabad (11), Cancun (13), Cali (16). pp. 85–86

    2. Which three UN bodies run UN-REDD?

      FAO, UNDP, UNEP. p. 86

    3. Correct this: "Wetlands International is formed by Ramsar signatory countries."

      False. It is an independent not-for-profit with Government and NGO members. p. 87

    Biodiversity: Miscellaneous

    Standalone facts the book collects at the end of the chapter.

    • Sea buckthorn: medicinal plant of Himalayan region; fruits grow in cold deserts of Ladakh, Lahaul-Spiti (HP), Uttarakhand, parts of Arunachal Pradesh. Bushy, dense branches check soil erosion, stabilise soil, help nitrogen fixation in cold desert areas; fruit gives nutritious juice. Well adapted to cold high altitude. Q stem: berries rich in vitamins and nutrients; Genghis Khan's troops; Russian cosmonauts' oil against radiation. Key rejects biodiesel and valuable timber.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Tallest known tree: Redwood (Sequoia), California (U.S.A.), up to 381 feet (116.1 m).
    • Niche: an organism's role in a community: physical and environmental conditions needed and interactions (predation, competition). Narrow niche = specialist (koalas, only eucalyptus leaves, Australia). Broad = generalist (coyotes, raccoons). Broad niche does not cause extinction; narrow niche, large body size, lack of genetic variability do, per key.
    • Hibernation (winter sleep): dormant inactivity to conserve energy in unfavourable weather; in cold- and warm-blooded animals. Squirrels, bears, some bats; metabolism slows, temperature plunges, breathing slows. Fat stored by eating in summer and autumn. Rodents: squirrels, muskrats, mice.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Bhitarkanika (protected area): Brahmani and Vaitarni merge before the sea. Brahmani known as South Koel in Jharkhand; formed by confluence of South Koel and Sankh near Rourkela. Vaitarni rises in Guptaganga hills, Keonjhar, Odisha.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Butterflies: climate regulates most insect life cycles; temperature drives flight, foraging, movement. They cannot tolerate low temperatures, so most species are tropical.
    • Psychrophiles (cold loving): grow best at 0–15°C; usually do not survive above 20°C; permanently cold habitats such as deep ocean waters. Extreme psychrophiles reported from Lake Whillans.
    • Limnetic zone: open, well-lit water of a freestanding freshwater body; primary habitat of plankton; highest phytoplankton density, so most photosynthetically active and main oxygen producer. Littoral zone is the shallow near-shore area, excluded. → Ch 2
    • Tomorrow's Biodiversity: Vandana Shiva.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Biomass: all organic materials arising from plants (incl. algae, trees, crops); produced through photosynthesis; includes land and water vegetation and all organic wastes.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Place the biomes in descending annual biomass order: taiga, deep sea, prairie, deciduous forest.

      Deciduous forest, taiga, prairie, deep sea. p. 88

    2. Distinguish a specialist from a generalist with one example each.

      Specialist: narrow niche (koala). Generalist: broad niche (coyote, raccoon). p. 89

    3. Why is sea buckthorn valued in cold deserts?

      Dense bushy growth checks soil erosion, and its fruit is nutritious. p. 88

    4. Which two rivers merge at Bhitarkanika?

      Brahmani and Vaitarni. p. 88
