    Greenhouse Effect and Climate Change

    Green House Effect

    Short-wave sunlight enters; Earth re-emits long-wave heat; greenhouse gases trap part of it and warm the surface and lower atmosphere.

    Mechanism
    • Solar energy reaches Earth as electromagnetic waves; very hot Sun → high-energy short wavelengths that penetrate the atmosphere.
    • Solar radiation = Ultraviolet (UV) + visible + infrared. Harmful UV absorbed by ozone layer; visible and infrared reach Earth as thermal radiation.
    • Thermal radiation heats Earth by conduction, convection and radiation.
    • Heated Earth re-emits energy as long-wave radiation, because Earth is cooler than Sun.
    • GHGs absorb much of this long-wave energy, then re-emit it in all directions → warm surface and lower atmosphere. Short-wave passes in; long-wave cannot pass out freely.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • Like the glass of a greenhouse, which does not release warm air inside (hence "greenhouse effect").
    • CORE CONCEPT Greenhouse effect = natural phenomenon raising a planet's temperature. Global warming = rise in average atmospheric temperature due to increase in GHGs.
    • Without greenhouse effect, Earth's average surface temperature ≈ –18 °C.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key: all three correct): without the atmosphere, temperature would be well below freezing everywhere on Earth's surface; heat absorbed and trapped by the atmosphere maintains average temperature; gases like CO₂ are particularly good at absorbing and trapping radiation.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • Concept given by Joseph Fourier (1820s).
    • IAS 2012 key: rising CO₂ warms the atmosphere because it absorbs the infrared part of radiation. Book: CO₂ molecules absorb and re-emit infrared; excess from human activity (mostly burning fossil fuels) is warming climate at a problematic rate.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    Source check Q explanation (p.95) says CO₂ absorbs "about 90%" of infrared "received from Sun"; the book's own mechanism (p.91) has GHGs absorbing long-wave energy emitted from Earth's surface. Learn the p.91 mechanism; treat the 90% figure as unverified.
    Greenhouse gases and their shares
    • Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) meaning: a gas that absorbs and emits radiation within the thermal infrared range.
    • Present in Earth's atmosphere: CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs), water vapour; ozone also listed. Main set: Carbon dioxide, Methane, Nitrous Oxide, Water vapour. Fluorinated gases are synthetic.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • Found naturally and most important: CO₂, methane, ozone, water vapour (Nitrogen oxide is not a naturally found GHG per UPPCS 2020 key — do not confuse with nitrous oxide).
    • Water vapour = most abundant GHG; NASA: responsible for about half of Earth's greenhouse effect.
    • Water-vapour feedback: warmer → more evaporation → more vapour → more heat trapped → warmer (positive feedback loop). Its concentration is linked to temperature; natural part of water cycle.
    • CO₂ = main GHG behind global warming (66% share in table) as its concentration is more than other GHGs.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • Carbon dioxide + Methane = the pair chosen as main contributors. CF₄ and N₂O are relatively less responsible.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • IPCC basket: directly related – CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, CFCs, SF₆, NF₃. Indirectly related – NOₓ, CO, NMVOC, SO₂. SO₂ also responsible for acid rain.
    Common trap Water vapour = largest share of the natural greenhouse effect (Q on "naturally occurring", p.93). CO₂ = main driver of global warming in the WMO table of long-lived gases. Read which one the stem asks.
    • Use the table above for share questions: UKPSC 2024 incorrect pair = CO₂ 40%. CGPSC 2020 order = CO₂ > CH₄ > CFCs > N₂O.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    Source check Book prints "NF6" among direct gases (p.91) but "NF3" on p.95. Use NF₃. Book says IPCC named 6 major gases; the list covers CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, CFCs, SF₆ and this fluoride.
    Gases that are not greenhouse gases
    • Argon (Ar): not a GHG, not responsible for global warming.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Nitrogen: not a GHG.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Oxygen: not a GHG.
    • Hydrogen (H₂): not a GHG; diatomic gas; main component of outer layer of atmosphere.
    • Propane: UPPCS 2017 key = not a GHG. Substituting Propane for gasoline and fuel oil is an economical, viable step to cleaner air; cuts CO₂ and air pollutants (CO, nitrogen oxide).
    • Oxides of Sulphur and Nitrogen do not contribute to global warming directly.
    • NO₂ and O₂ are both named by the book as the non-GHG answer in a question it marks (*).
    Source check SO₂ (Q0370, CGPSC 2016, p.95): Chhattisgarh Public Service Commission's key was (e) "None of these" in this paper but (c) SO₂ in the earlier answer sheet. Book says SO₂ is not a GHG, and suggests (e) rests on direct vs indirect gases. Keep as disputed.
    Source check Q0404 (UPPCS 2016, p.103) is marked (*): book says both NO₂ and O₂ are the answer "(C & d)". Primary list there = CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, ozone.
    Source check Q0371 (UPPCS 2017, p.95) is marked (*): book rejects (a) rise in CO₂ as a result of the effect (no evidence found), rejects biological population as not atmospheric, and says both (b) humidity and (d) wind speed are correct. Its reasoning: more heat → more evaporation → more humidity; more temperature → more pressure → more air mobility (book's wording). Not resolved; do not treat a single option as settled.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Who first gave the concept of the greenhouse effect, and when?

      Joseph Fourier, 1820s. p. 91

    2. Why does the Earth re-emit energy as long-wave rather than short-wave radiation?

      Earth is much cooler than the Sun, so it radiates at long wavelengths, which GHGs can absorb. p. 91

    3. Name the four gases listed as indirectly related to the greenhouse effect.

      NOₓ, CO, NMVOC and SO₂. p. 95

    4. Correct this statement: "CO₂ is the most abundant greenhouse gas in the atmosphere."

      Water vapour is the most abundant. CO₂ is the main contributor to global warming in the WMO share table. pp. 91–93

    Cause of Greenhouse Effect

    Human activity adds CO₂, CH₄ and N₂O faster than sinks remove them, upsetting the balance of atmosphere, ocean and biosphere.

    Carbon dioxide
    • CO₂ is both useful and harmful: needed for photosynthesis (all life depends on it), but excess drives global warming. Plants take CO₂, release Oxygen.
    • CO₂ released by anthropogenic activities is absorbed by soil on land and by marine plants. A lot does not stay in the lower atmosphere because of photosynthesis by phytoplankton in oceans and plants on land (IAS 2011 key: only this reason; not escape to stratosphere or air trapped in polar ice caps).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • World Meteorological Organization (WMO) GHG Bulletin 2024: global average CO₂ in 2023 = 420.0±0.1 ppm (Parts Per Million).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • Annual rise: 2.3 ppm (2022→2023), slightly above 2.2 ppm (2021→2022), slightly below past-decade average 2.4 ppm per year.
    • Pre-industrial level ≈ 278 ppm (278.3 ppm, p.98) ≈ 0.03%: a balance of fluxes among atmosphere, oceans and land biosphere. Now disturbed by combustion of fossil fuels, industries etc.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • UPPCS 2017 key: optimum CO₂ for natural balance = 0.03 percent by volume.
    • Book's graph (p.96; axis 300–440 ppm): CO₂ climbs every year 1959→2023, from a little above 310 ppm to about 420 ppm, steeper in later decades (read off the curve).
    • Carbon fertilization = more atmospheric CO₂ raises the rate of photosynthesis and plant growth. Varies with plant species, temperature, availability of water and nutrients. A quarter to half of Earth's vegetated lands showed significant greening over 35 years (1982–2015), largely due to rising CO₂.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's figure: 420.0±0.1 ppm for 2023 (Bulletin 2024). WMO Bulletin No. 22 (published 27 Oct 2026): CO₂ reached 426.8±0.2 ppm in 2025. WMO
    Source check Book says CO₂ "is confined to troposphere only" (p.98). This absolute wording was not verified here, and p.99 only rejects stratospheric escape as the reason CO₂ leaves the lower atmosphere. Do not reproduce it as a rule.
    Methane and nitrous oxide
    • CH₄ = Marsh Gas. Sources: biogas, organic/bacterial decomposition and remnants, cud-chewing animals. It is both a GHG and an air pollutant.
    • Man-made: paddy (rice) fields, coal mines, reared cattle/domestic animals. Natural: wetlands, oceans, hydrates.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Wetlands emit the highest proportion: about 75–76% of methane from natural sources.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Natural detail: Wetlands are the largest source (bacteria decompose organic matter without oxygen); smaller – termites, oceans, sediments, volcanoes, wildfires.
    • Human detail: Agriculture – livestock such as cattle, paddy, manure stored in lagoons; globally the primary source. Industry – Methane is the primary component of natural gas. Waste – decomposition in landfills.
    • Paddy: anaerobic conditions emit methane.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’10 · 2 papers
    • Nitrogen-based fertilisers → N₂O emitted from cultivated soil.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    • Rice = most important anthropogenic crop source of both methane and nitrous oxide (IAS 2022).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Methane hydrate: crystalline solid, methane molecule surrounded by water molecules; usually exists as "ice". Large deposits in Arctic Tundra and under the seafloor. Global warming might trigger release.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Atmospheric methane is relatively short-lived: oxidises to CO₂ after a decade or two.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Primary drivers of climate change
    • Two groups: factors raising carbon emission (excess burning of fossil fuels, exploding numbers of oil-driven automobiles using petrol and diesel) and factors cutting carbon absorption (excessive deforestation).
    • COMMON TRAP Solar flares: NASA and other studies do not treat them as a major cause.
    • Burning crop/biomass residue (cheapest, easiest disposal, for land clearing and pest control) releases CO, SO₂, CH₄ and O₃ (IAS 2019 key; O₃ is strictly a secondary pollutant formed from precursors in the smoke).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Halocarbons, refrigerants, foam-blowing agents, CFCs, HCFCs, halons deplete the ozone layer; these are also constituents of GHGs. → Ch 5REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Key for "cause of climate change" (UPPCS, Jharkhand 2013) = all three: greenhouse gases, depletion of ozone layer, pollution. Anthropogenic environmental pollution may lead to climate change.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key (b)): premise that cement accounts for more than 5% of global carbon emissions; silica-bearing clay is mixed with limestone, and limestone turns into lime in clinker production. Both are correct but only one explains the CO₂: conversion of limestone to lime (calcination releases CO₂).RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Source check Causal wording conflicts: p.96 note says GHGs are directly responsible for climate change and some GHGs for ozone depletion; Q explanation (p.96) says GHGs and pollution are directly responsible for ozone depletion, with ozone depletion only indirectly responsible for climate change. Learn the p.96 note; the key is unaffected.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Define carbon fertilization and give two conditions on which it depends.

      Higher CO₂ raises photosynthesis. It varies with plant species, temperature, water and nutrients. p. 97

    2. Distinguish human and natural methane sources, naming the largest natural source.

      Human: paddy, coal mines, cattle, landfills. Natural: wetlands (largest, about 75–76% of natural), oceans, hydrates, termites. pp. 96, 98

    3. Explain in one sentence why rice cultivation is linked to both CH₄ and N₂O.

      Anaerobic conditions in rice cultivation emit methane, while nitrogen-based fertiliser releases nitrous oxide from soil. p. 99

    4. Correct this: "Solar flares are a primary cause of present climate change."

      NASA and other studies do not treat solar flares as a major cause. pp. 96–97

    Effects of Greenhouse Gases

    Warming melts ice, raises seas, shifts species and weather. IPCC thresholds at 2 °C and 3 °C are the most tested part.

    IPCC temperature thresholds
    • Scientific view: rise should not exceed 2 °C above pre-industrial level. IAS 2014 key rejects "all global wetlands permanently disappear" and "cereal cultivation impossible everywhere".PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    Sea level and temperature estimates
    • Melting polar ice caps → rise in sea level, submerging coastal regions; book: many European countries have lost coastal regions.
    • IPCC estimate (book's dated figures): 1.4–5.8 °C rise and 0.33–0.45 m sea-level rise during 1990–2100; 142 out of 144 polar ice caps may melt. "One metre by 2070" statement was rejected.
    • Sea level up approx 390 feet since last ice age; now rising about 3 mm per year.
    • Temperature: book says about 1 °C in the past century (1.8 °F since 1900), over half of it since the mid-1970s. UPPCS 2012 key = 0.9 °C.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Evidence: reduced Arctic sea ice extent, increased ocean heat content, poleward shifts of temperature-sensitive fish, mammals, insects.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Book: Fiji's sea level rises 0.15 m (about 6 inches) per year, Fiji "likely submerged by 2044" (pp.100, 102), beside a global 3 mm per year on the same page: 50 times the global rate, so the figure looks garbled. Q0402 key (c) also accepts "all coral islands will be submerged", which the explanation does not support. Keep these as UKPSC 2002 key only, and the IPCC range as dated (1990–2100). No external source was opened for this.
    Observed and expected impacts
    • Impacts: rise in sea level, melting of glaciers, spread of diseases, bleaching of coral reefs, changes in weather conditions, rise in global temperature. Main gases named: water vapour, CO₂, CH₄, CFC.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’09 · 2 papers
    • Species may go out of sync, e.g. plants bloom before pollinating insects are active (key accepted early flowering of mango).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Health risks: (i) direct-acting effects; (ii) effects via climate-related changes in ecological systems (crop fields, mosquito ecology, marine productivity).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Arctic and Greenland Ice sheet = most fragile ecosystem, first affected; melt there affects the entire Earth.
    • Storms: ocean surface absorbs heat and releases it to atmosphere (creating wind and rain clouds); as surface temperature keeps rising, more heat released → stronger, more frequent storms like tropical cyclones and hurricanes.
    • Rising gases named here: CO₂, methane, nitrous oxide, HCFCs, HFCs, ozone in lower atmosphere. Causes: burning fossil fuels, farming.
    • Measuring instruments show ocean surface water temperatures going up.
    • Corals: warming stresses them, they lose colour and die. A 2 °C rise in Indian Ocean temperature caused catastrophic bleaching and mass coral deaths in Andaman and Nicobar Islands.
    • Plankton is as important to sea ecosystems as grasses to terrestrial ones. Increased CO₂ uptake lowers plankton's nutritional quality → consequences for entire ocean food webs.
    • If an ocean's phytoplankton were destroyed: ocean as carbon sink and ocean food chains adversely affected; density of ocean water would not drastically decrease. Phytoplankton (microalgae) have chlorophyll, need sunlight.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key: both correct), on a World Bank warning that India could be among the first places where wet-bulb temperatures routinely exceed 35 °C: peninsular India most likely to suffer flooding, tropical cyclones and droughts; survival of animals including humans affected as shedding body heat through perspiration becomes difficult.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Which effect separates a 3 °C rise from a 2 °C rise in the book's IPCC account?

      The terrestrial biosphere shifts towards a net carbon source. pp. 100–101

    2. Fill the gaps: at 2 °C, __ of ecosystems transform and __ of known species go extinct.

      1/6 and 1/4. p. 99

    3. Explain how warmer seas strengthen storms.

      A warmer surface releases more heat to the atmosphere, feeding stronger and more frequent cyclones and hurricanes. p. 101

    4. Correct this: "Destroying an ocean's phytoplankton would drastically lower seawater density."

      Density would not drastically decrease; the carbon sink and food chains would suffer. p. 102

    Climate Change

    Natural orbital cycles, the ice-core record, social stress, top emitters, net zero and ways of storing carbon.

    Astronomical theory and climate records
    • Change in Earth's position relative to Sun varies solar radiation received: astronomical principle = Milankovitch Hypothesis (Milutin Milankovitch, Serbian astronomer, climatologist, geophysicist; 1920s).
    • Three orbital factors: eccentricity of orbit, obliquity (axial tilt) of rotation axis, precession of equinoxes → cyclical variation in radiation = orbital forcing.
    • COMMON TRAP Solar irradiance is not part of this theory.
    • Ice core = cylindrical sample drilled from a glacier or ice sheet (Antarctica, Greenland, high mountain glaciers); gives the cryogenic indicator of climate; most direct and detailed record of past climate and atmosphere.
    • Annual snow layers: lower layers older than upper. Ice properties and inclusions, normally via isotopic analysis, reconstruct local temperature and atmospheric composition.
    Agriculture and social stress
    • Agriculture is the activity most affected. Rising temperature affects soil moisture and efficiency, raises soil salinity, cuts productivity; also changes water availability and needs.
    • Climate change raises pressure on resources (forest, grassland, agricultural land, river water, land water): conflict over them shows it as a factor in social tension.
    • Chain: extreme weather → food insecurity → unnecessary storing of food → social tension. UPPCS 2017: A and R both true, R explains A.
    Emitters, net zero and CCPI
    • Largest CO₂ emitter: China. Latest data in book (2023): China 34%, USA 12%, India about 7%, Russia 5%, Japan 2% of world total.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Bhutan: world's top carbon-negative country; absorbs more than it emits.
    • Net zero (= carbon neutrality) does not mean zero emissions: a country's emissions are compensated by absorption and removal of GHGs from the atmosphere.
    • Absorption via carbon sinks such as forests; removal via futuristic technology such as carbon capture and storage. Negative emissions possible if these exceed emissions.
    • Net-zero targets: India 2070, China 2060. International Energy Agency's roadmap "Net Zero by 2050" (2021) was the World's first comprehensive energy road map, ahead of the UN's November 2021 climate meeting.
    • Climate Change Performance Index (CCPI): published by Germanwatch, NewClimate Institute and Climate Action Network International. Measures 4 parameters: GHG Emissions, Renewable Energy, Energy Use, Climate Policy.
    • India's CCPI rank: 20th of 58 (2017), 14th (2018), 11th (2019), 10th (2022 edition), 8th (2023), 10th (2025).
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key: II and III only): India ranks second in Asia-Pacific in CO₂ emissions from fuel combustion; electricity and heat producers are India's largest CO₂ source. The claim that India's emissions are under 0.5 t CO₂ per capita was rejected.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Source check Q0410 (UPPCS 2023, p.104) asks India's rank in world GHG emission in 2022; key (d) 10th, but the explanation gives only the CCPI rank, a performance index. Book's own p.103 puts India third in CO₂ emission. Do not read 10th as an emissions rank.
    Carbon sequestration and farm practice
    • Carbon sequestration = capturing and long-term storing of atmospheric carbon in plants, geologic structures and sea.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Potential sites: abandoned and uneconomic coal seams, depleted oil and gas reservoirs, subterranean deep saline formations (IAS 2017: all three).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • IAS 2023 key: all three discussed for capture and sequestration: finely ground basalt on farmlands, adding lime to raise ocean alkalinity, pumping captured CO₂ as carbonated water into abandoned mines.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • Basalt: University of California, Davis and Cornell University field study: crushed volcanic rock stored carbon in soil even in an extreme California drought.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • Captured CO₂: utilisation (urea manufacturing; methane, methanol, ethanol; aggregates) or permanent storage (depleted oil and gas reservoirs for enhanced oil production/EOR, deep saline aquifers, basaltic rock).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • IAS 2012: all three aid soil carbon storage. Contour bunding: mechanical measure, minimises soil erosion in rainfed areas. Relay cropping: new crop sown before previous is harvested (nitrogen, shade, support, discourages pests). Zero tillage: least disturbance, retains water, nutrients, topsoil; stores soil organic matter.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Climate-Smart Village: part of a project led by CGIAR (Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research) Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security (CCAFS), built on Climate Smart Agriculture principles; technological, organisational and systemic support.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • CGIAR headquartered in France. ICRISAT (International Crops Research Institute for the Semi-Arid Tropics, India) is a CGIAR centre: non-profit, non-political public international body, research for development in Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa; awarded 2021 Africa Food Prize.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Rural roads, to cut carbon footprint: copper slag, cold mix asphalt technology, geotextiles preferred (IAS 2020).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key: all three correct): Direct Air Capture can serve carbon sequestration, plastic production and food processing, and supply carbon to combine with hydrogen for synthetic low-carbon aviation fuel.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • Mains 2025 asked what Carbon Capture, Utilization and Storage (CCUS) is and its potential role against climate change. Book's utilisation and storage routes above are the raw material; the paper has no official answer.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Source check Book's "Carbon overlay" (p.103) is defined exactly as carbon sequestration (p.106): merged here. Q0409's explanation does not discuss statement 2 (lime); the key (c) still counts it.
    Asia-Pacific Partnership (APP)
    • APP on Clean Development and Climate: international, voluntary, public-private partnership of Australia, Canada, India, Japan, People's Republic of China, South Korea, United States; announced July 2005.
    • Members account for over 45% of world GHG emissions, energy consumption, GDP and population. (UPPCS 2005 key also accepted 48% for energy and GHG.)
    • COMMON TRAP Not intended to directly support Kyoto. Unlike Kyoto's mandatory limits, it accelerates clean-energy technology with no mandatory enforcement, hence criticised by governments, climate scientists and environmental groups as worthless.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. List the three orbital variables in Milankovitch's theory.

      Eccentricity, axial tilt (obliquity) and precession. pp. 102, 105

    2. Correct this: "Net zero means a country will emit nothing by the target year."

      Emissions may continue if compensated by absorption and removal. p. 104

    3. Reconstruct the chain from extreme weather to social stress.

      Extreme weather → food insecurity → unnecessary food storage → social tension. pp. 103, 105

    4. Why are lower ice-core layers useful for old climate?

      Layers build up yearly, so deeper ice is older and preserves past conditions. p. 105

    Climate Change Conference

    UNFCCC (1992) is the parent treaty; COPs meet under it; Paris (2015) set the temperature goal; the Green Climate Fund carries finance.

    UNFCCC and the COPs
    • UNFCCC: international environmental treaty adopted at UN Conference on Environment and Development (UNCED, the Earth Summit), Rio-de-Janeiro, 3–14 June 1992.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    • In force 21 March 1994, after support of more than 50 countries. Ratified by 198 countries (book's figure).
    • Deals with mitigation of GHG emissions. Ultimate objective of all three agreements under it: stabilise GHG concentrations at a level preventing dangerous human interference with the climate system, in a time frame letting ecosystems adapt naturally and enabling sustainable development.
    • COP21: Paris, 30 November–12 December 2015; 21st yearly COP and 11th session of Meeting of the Parties to the 1997 Kyoto Protocol.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • COP25: 2–13 December 2019. COP16: 29 November–10 December 2010.
    • COP26: Glasgow, 31 October–12 November 2021. 120 world leaders, over 40,000 registered participants: 22,274 party delegates, 14,124 observers, 3,886 media representatives.
    • UN Climate Action Summit 2019: UN headquarters, New York City, 23 September 2019. Theme: "A Race We can Win. A Race We Must Win."
    • "Momentum for Change: Climate Neutral Now": launched by UNFCCC secretariat, to showcase successful climate action.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Matched correctly: First World Climate Conference – 1979; First Earth Summit – Agenda-21; Earth Summit Plus 5 – 1997. Carbon Trading belongs to Kyoto, not Montreal.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Geo-engineering idea: Manchester University scientists suggested cloud brightening over the Pacific Ocean using sea water, for maximum reflection of sun rays.
    Paris Agreement
    • Agreed by 194 nations (book’s figure; adoption is usually reported as 195 countries, 196 Parties with the EU) at Paris on 12 December 2015 (not "signed by all UN members").PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • CORE CONCEPT Aim: keep temperature rise this century well below 2 °C and drive efforts to limit it to 1.5 °C above pre-industrial levels.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Developed countries accepted responsibility; climate finance roadmap to USD 100 billion by 2020 (not 1000 billion).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Book's count: 195 Parties (194 states + EU) out of 198 parties to the convention.
    • India at COP26 (per book): by 2030, 50% of energy requirement from renewable energy (not 60%), non-fossil capacity 500 GW, carbon emission cut by one billion tons. → Ch 8
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key (a)): Article 6 of the Paris Agreement sets out principles of carbon markets and also promotes inter-country non-market strategies; both explain why it is so often discussed. Not in the book.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • IPCC: gradual or limited GHG cuts are insufficient for Paris goals; deep, rapid and sustained reductions are essential.
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 tested the COP28 Declaration on Climate and Health and India not signing it. Reported official key (c): only one of the two reasoning statements (binding and mandatory decarbonisation; risk to health-care resilience) is correct, and it explains India's stand.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Green Climate Fund (GCF)
    • Formally established at COP16, Cancun, 2010 (not Durban), within framework of UNFCCC. Based in Songdo Business district, Incheon, South Korea.
    • Purpose: assist developing countries in adaptation and mitigation practices to counter climate change. Developed nations to provide 100 billion dollars per year by 2020.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Governed by GCF Board, 24 members; not founded under aegis of UNEP, OECD, ADB and World Bank.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    Earth Hour
    • Worldwide movement by World Wide Fund for Nature (WWF), not UNEP/UNESCO; awareness on climate change and need to save the planet.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Non-essential lights off for one hour, 8:30–9:30 pm, usually last Saturday of March. Began as a lights-off event in Sydney, Australia, 2007.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Dates in book: 26 March 2022; 23 March 2024; 22 March 2025.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. When was the UNFCCC adopted, and when did it enter into force?

      June 1992 at Rio; in force 21 March 1994. pp. 107–108

    2. Name the COP and city where the Green Climate Fund was formally established.

      COP16, Cancun, 2010. pp. 107, 109

    3. Correct this: "Earth Hour is a joint UNEP and UNESCO programme."

      It is organised by WWF. pp. 108, 110

    4. Complete the Paris goal: well below __ °C, with efforts towards __ °C.

      2 °C and 1.5 °C above pre-industrial levels. p. 108

    The Kyoto Protocol & Other Protocol

    Kyoto set binding cuts and created market tools (CDM, CER, carbon credits); a wider family of funds, metrics and alliances grew around it.

    Kyoto Protocol
    • International treaty extending the 1992 UNFCCC; relates to climate change; aims to reduce GHG concentration through internationally binding targets.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Adopted Kyoto, Japan, 11 December 1997; in force 16 February 2005 after Russia's ratification.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05
    • Binding agreement: high-emitting countries to collectively cut GHGs by 5.2% during 2008–2012 (1990 baseline).
    • Deals with GHG reduction, not ozone depletion.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05
    • 192 Parties (book's figure). It operationalises the UNFCCC by committing industrialized countries and economies in transition to agreed individual targets; the Convention itself only asks them to adopt policies and report periodically.
    • Annex-I countries took part in the 1st Kyoto round; Annex-I countries and the EU agreed second-round targets.
    • Kyoto is unratified by the United States (book's wording: "currently").
    • GWP of CO₂ fixed at 1. Book: methane 81.2 (p.111) and about 86 times CO₂ (p.113); so methane is the more harmful gas per unit.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05
    Source check Book gives two methane values, 81.2 "in the last 20 years" (p.111) and about 86 (p.113), with no reconciliation; "last 20 years" reads like a 20-year time horizon. It also says GWP depends on "efficiency of the atoms" and "environmental life-cycle". Remember the qualitative point (methane far stronger than CO₂); not externally verified here.
    CDM, CER and carbon credits
    • CDM: Article 12 of Kyoto Protocol. Lets an Annex-B party (with an emission commitment) implement an emission-reduction project in developing countries.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Market-driven device under UNFCCC: developing countries get funds/incentives from developed countries for better technologies. It does not prohibit developed-country investment; it promotes it.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’09
    • COMMON TRAP Projects are not limited to Annex-I: Annex-I (industrialized) countries fund them in Non-Annex-I (developing) countries.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Two aims: help Non-Annex-I achieve sustainable development and UNFCCC's objective; help Annex-I comply with quantified emission caps.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • One of the Protocol's flexible, "project-based" Mechanisms.
    • CDM project types: afforestation, carbon sink, minimisation of carbon.
    • CER: tradable unit of CDM; 1 CER = 1 ton CO₂. Issued by CDM Executive Board for reductions verified by a DOE (Designated Operational Entity). Counted towards Kyoto targets.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08, ’16
    • Carbon credit concept originated from Kyoto. One credit permits emission of one ton of CO₂ (or other GHG). Carbon trading encouraged internationally; economic incentive to cut CO₂.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Credits awarded for cutting below quota; aim to limit rise in CO₂. Traded privately or on open market at current market value, not at a price fixed by UNEP.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Mechanics: caps set under Kyoto → national registries (validated and monitored by UNFCCC) → operators get allowances; those under quota sell, those over buy; so firms invest in cleaner practice or buy credits.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Emission Reduction Purchase Agreement, May 2011: for CERs, signed by Hubert Nove Josserand (World Bank deputy country director, for International Bank for Reconstruction and Development) and Sudripto Roy (Himachal Pradesh additional chief secretary): with Himachal Government, not Government of India. Book treats remaining options as correct: ten-year validity; Himachal Pradesh project; one ton CO₂ = one credit.
    Carbon metrics, tax and blue carbon
    • Carbon footprint: total GHGs produced, directly or indirectly, to support human activities.
    • GHG Protocol: most widely used international accounting tool for Government and business leaders to understand, quantify and manage GHG emissions. Partnership of World Resources Institute (WRI) and World Business Council for Sustainable Development (WBCSD); works with businesses, governments and environmental groups.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Common Carbon Metric (UNEP-supported): measures energy use and reports GHG from building operations; universal method for a building's carbon footprint; defines measurement, reporting, verification. Not a building rating tool; consistent with WBCSD and WRI GHG Protocol.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Blue carbon: carbon captured by world's ocean and coastal ecosystems. Sea grasses, mangroves and salt marshes act as sinks; much smaller than forests but sequester at a much faster rate, and can continue for millions of years.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Carbon tax: levied on energy resources releasing CO₂; a form of Pollution tax; makes fossil-fuel users pay for climate damage.
    • Singapore announced (February 2018) a carbon tax from 2019, under Paris commitments: facilities with 25,000 tons or more GHG per year.
    • New Zealand: book says it introduced a Carbon Tax for the first time in 2005 (IAS 2006 key).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’06
    Source check Same explanation (p.116) says Sweden, Finland, the Netherlands and Norway had "already introduced" Carbon Tax, yet calls New Zealand first. The question asks who first proposed one for its people. Keep "New Zealand" as the question-time key only.
    Forest and climate funds
    • Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF): World Bank, June 2008. Global partnership of governments, businesses, civil society, indigenous people. Readiness Fund and Carbon Fund.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Helps developing countries in REDD+ (Reducing Emission from Deforestation and Forest Degradation: cut deforestation and forest degradation, conserve and enhance forest carbon stocks, sustainably manage forests) with financial and technical assistance. Does not fund universities, individuals or institutes.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • BioCarbon Fund ISFL (Initiative for Sustainable Forest Landscapes): multilateral fund managed by World Bank; donors Norway up to $135 million, UK $120 million, USA $25 million. Promotes reduced land-sector GHG.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Global Climate Change Alliance (GCCA): European Union (EU) initiative, 2007 (operational 2008), with developing countries, in particular LDCs and SIDS. Coordinated by European Commission, not WRI/WBCSD.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Gives technical and financial support to integrate climate change in development policies and budgets. Started in 4 countries; budget more than £300 million (book); aim: a new treaty between EU and poor developing countries.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2026 asked which is India's first Plan Vivo certified REDD+ project. Coaching consensus, not an official key: Khasi Hills Community REDD+ project.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Matching treaties
    InstrumentSubjectKey facts
    Kyoto ProtocolClimate change1997 / 2005REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Montreal ProtocolOzone Layer→ Ch 5
    Stockholm ConventionPersistent organic pollutants–
    Cartagena ProtocolBiosafety: safe handling, transport, use of living modified organisms (LMOs); supplementary to CBDAdopted 29 January 2000; in force 11 September 2003; India confirmed 23 January 2003PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    Nagoya ProtocolAccess to genetic resources and fair sharing of benefits; supplementary to CBDIn force 12 October 2014PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • IAS 2016: only CDM – Kyoto matched. Annex-I is not Cartagena; CER is not Nagoya.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    Observatories and coalitions
    • Deep Carbon Observatory: global community of more than 1000 scientists, a ten-year quest (2009–2019) on quantities, movements, forms and origins of carbon in Earth. Conducts field observation of deep microbial ecosystem.
    • DCO communities: Extreme Physics and Chemistry (carbon at extreme conditions in deep interiors of Earth and other planets); Reservoirs and Fluxes (deep reservoirs, movement, total carbon budget); Deep Energy (abiogenic hydrocarbons and organic species in crust and mantle); Deep Life (deep microbial and viral biosphere).
    • Climate Action Tracker: independent scientific analysis by coalition of Climate Analytics and NewClimate Institute; since 2009; tracks pledges against the Paris aim.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • The Climate Group: international non-profit, founded 2003; offices London, New Delhi, Amsterdam, Beijing, New York; networks of businesses and governments.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • EP100: led by Climate Group (not IEA partnership per key); over 120 energy-smart businesses reporting efficiency gains. Indian members: Dalmia Cement, Godrej and Boyce, JSW Cement, Mahindra and Mahindra, Mahindra Heavy Engines, Swaraj Engines, UltraTech Cement.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Under 2 Coalition: largest global network of states, regions, provinces and other subnational governments for net zero by 2050 at the latest. Secretariat = Climate Group, not IEA.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. State the Kyoto adoption date, entry-into-force date and collective cut.

      11 December 1997; 16 February 2005; 5.2% during 2008–2012. p. 110

    2. In the CDM figure, what flows from Annex-I to Non-Annex-I and what flows back?

      Capital for CDM projects goes out; carbon credit comes back. p. 107

    3. Correct this: "Carbon credit prices are fixed periodically by UNEP."

      They trade at market value, privately or on the open market. pp. 111, 115

    4. Distinguish the GHG Protocol from the Common Carbon Metric.

      GHG Protocol is a general accounting tool for governments and businesses; the Metric covers building operations. pp. 113–114

    5. Who manages the BioCarbon Fund ISFL, and which donor gives most?

      World Bank; Norway, up to $135 million. pp. 111, 116

    Action Plan of India in perspective of climate change

    NAPCC (2008) gives eight missions; INDCs set 2030 goals; Jharkhand is the book's state-level example.

    National Action Plan on Climate Change
    • India's first NAPCC: released by then PM Manmohan Singh, 30 June 2008; focuses on eight missions, to mitigate and adapt.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    1. National Solar MissionREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    2. National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency
    3. National Mission on Sustainable Habitat
    4. National Water Mission
    5. National Mission for Sustaining the Himalayan Ecosystem
    6. National Mission for Green IndiaREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    7. National Mission for Sustainable Agriculture
    8. National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change
    Common trap Atomic/nuclear energy is not a mission (key in both 2010 and 2016 papers). The list also has no separately named waste-to-energy mission; afforestation sits under Green India.
    • Green India Mission (GIM): launched February 2014; protects, restores and enhances forest cover by combined adaptation and mitigation. Recognises forests' role in mitigation, water security, food security, biodiversity conservation and livelihood security of forest-dependent communities.
    • National Executive Council's second meeting (October 2015) approved annual plans of Mizoram, Manipur, Kerala and Jharkhand; Jharkhand PSC 2016 key = Jharkhand.
    India's INDC
    • Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs): post-2020 climate action countries publicly outlined under the Paris agreement at COP21 (December 2015); India's are for GHG reductions under UNFCCC.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Centre on clean, especially renewable, energy; energy efficiency; less carbon-intensive and resilient urban centres; waste to wealth; safe, smart, sustainable green transport; abatement of pollution; enhancing carbon sink through forest and tree cover.
    Source check Book's INDC box (33–35%, 40%; p.118) is the 2015 position, but later pages give different 2030 goals: 50% renewable energy (p.109), carbon intensity 45% and 500 GW (p.208). Compare → Ch 8 and state which commitment a question means. Later official figures could not be opened in this pass.
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • Mains 2025 asked for a review of India's Paris commitments, how COP26 strengthened them, and how the first NDC was updated in 2022. The book gives the INDC box and COP26 lines but not the 2022 text; the paper has no official answer.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • UPSC 2026 asked how three statements relate: LT-LEDS as a tool for net-zero by 2070; BUR-4 (December 2024) recording around 8% fall in GHG emissions in 2020 over 2019; and resilience depending on quick short-term cuts. Coaching consensus (b), not an official key: the first is supported by the second, and the third contradicts the first's approach.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Jharkhand climate action
    • State Climate Change Centre/Cell: Jharkhand with UNDP (May 2011); advises state government.
    • Functions: knowledge products for stakeholders (issue briefs, mitigation and adaptation options); outreach and network with other organisations; capacity development of government officers for mainstreaming climate concerns.
    • Jharkhand Action Plan on Climate Change: published 2013 and 2014 (see check below).
    • Most sensitive district (2014 report): Saraikela Kharsawan, score 0.78 on a scale of (–1) to (+1).
    Source check Q0467 (Jharkhand PCS 2016) is marked (*): the book says the State plan was published in 2013 & 2014, and both years are options. No single answer.
    Monitoring and institutions
    • Global Atmosphere Watch (GAW), a WMO programme: network of Global and Regional stations plus Contributing stations; about 30 global stations. Algeria, Brazil and Kenya listed; India had none as at UPPCS 2012; book adds Brazil is now out of the list.
    • SAFAR (System of Air Quality and Weather Forecasting): state-of-the-art Air Quality and Weather Forecast System, unveiled by Dr Harsh Vardhan at Chandni Chowk, Delhi, 2018. Built indigenously by Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology, Pune; operationalised by India Meteorological Department (IMD).
    • True-colour LED display: real-time air quality index 24x7 with colour coding, plus 72-hour advance forecast. Monitors PM2.5, PM10, Sulfur Dioxide, Ozone, Nitrogen Oxides, Carbon Monoxide; also Benzene, Toluene, Xylene.
    • National Green Tribunal: set up 18-10-2010 under NGT Act 2010, for effective and expeditious disposal of environmental protection and forest conservation cases. Headquarters: New Delhi. → Ch 11
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name the date NAPCC was launched and the Prime Minister then in office.

      30 June 2008; Manmohan Singh. p. 118

    2. Fill the missing INDC box: carbon sink of __ billion tonnes of CO₂ by 2030.

      2.5 to 3. p. 118

    3. Correct this: "NAPCC includes a National Mission on Nuclear Energy."

      Nuclear energy is not among the eight missions. p. 119

    4. Who built SAFAR, and who operationalised it?

      IITM Pune built it; IMD operationalised it. p. 121

    Ozone Layer

    Ozone and the Ozone Layer

    Same gas, two roles: harmful near the ground, protective high up.

    • Ozone (O₃): three oxygen atoms; found in very small amount. Stratospheric ozone forms naturally from solar UV acting on O₂ molecules.
    • CORE CONCEPT At Earth's surface a pollutant, harmful to living organisms ("deadly poison"); at higher levels it shields Earth from UV, which is highly damaging and known to cause skin cancer. O₂, by contrast, is essential for aerobic life.
    • Ozone layer absorbs ultraviolet radiation; protects life and the biosphere from it, preventing UV from reaching the Earth.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • Found mainly in the stratosphere: lower portion, about 15–30 km (9–18 miles); thickness/density varies seasonally and geographically.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • So "around 20 km above the Earth" fits among Q's options.
    • About 90% of atmospheric ozone is in the stratosphere; about 10% in the troposphere (book: "10% of the ozone layer").REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Key: Ozone layer–Troposphere is the mismatch. Left standing: Renneting–Cheese; Genetic Engineering–Plasmids; Golden rice–Vitamin A.
    • Ozone molecules are constantly formed and destroyed in the stratosphere; book adds that the total "has remained relatively stable during the decades that it has been measured" (p. 123), a statement about natural balance that sits beside the depletion pages.
    • Naturally regulated by Nitrogen dioxide (NO₂). Thickness highest in spring, lowest in the rainy season (book).
    • Unit: Dobson Unit (DU). 1 DU = 0.01 mm thickness of pure ozone at 0°C and 1 atm. Good indicator of total ozone concentration. Other options: decibel (sound level); ppb, parts per billion (pollutant concentration in air, volume ratio); ppm, parts per million (book: mass of contaminant per million units of total mass).
    • Total mass of ozone in atmosphere: about 3 billion metric tons.
    Homosphere and heterosphere
    • Order upward: troposphere (lowest), stratosphere, mesosphere, thermosphere, exosphere. Thickness differs slightly around the globe, varies with temperature and season. Troposphere is thickest at the equator.
    • Homosphere (uniform chemical composition): up to 56 mi (90 km); three thermal layers: troposphere, stratosphere, mesosphere, each separated by a shallow transitional zone. COMMON TRAP Ionosphere is not in the homosphere.
    • Heterosphere: heterogeneous, layered nitrogen, oxygen, helium, hydrogen, respectively. Book's graph places thermosphere, exosphere and ionosphere in the upper atmosphere, with curves for Active Sun and Average Sun.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Define one Dobson Unit.

      0.01 mm thickness of pure ozone at 0°C and 1 atm. p. 122

    2. Explain why ozone is called both a pollutant and a protector.

      Harmful at the surface; high in the atmosphere it shields Earth from UV. p. 122

    3. Name the three layers of the homosphere, from the ground up.

      Troposphere, stratosphere, mesosphere. p. 124

    4. Correct this: "The ozone layer lies in the troposphere."

      False. It lies mainly in the stratosphere, about 15–30 km up. p. 123

    Ozone Layer Depletion

    Man-made chlorine and bromine compounds, activated over the poles, destroy ozone faster than it forms.

    • British Antarctic Survey team first reported Antarctic ozone hole: Joe Farman, Brian Gardiner, Jonathan Shanklin, journal Nature, May 1985.
    • Ozone hole: not literally a hole; a region of exceptionally depleted ozone in the stratosphere over Antarctica. Ozone forms very slowly over polar regions, so depletion effect is maximum over the poles.
    • CORE CONCEPT Chlorofluorocarbons are the main cause: released CFCs break ozone down through chemical reactions, cutting its UV-absorbing capacity.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • CFCs: man-made compounds of carbon, chlorine, fluorine (book also says oxygen in one note); non-toxic, non-flammable, colourless, odourless, easily liquefied. Very stable, lasting 80 to 100 years. Book: one of the major causes of the greenhouse effect.
    • Uses: refrigerants in refrigerators, air conditioners; aerosol sprays (pressurizing agents), blowing agents for plastic foams and packing materials, solvents; trichlorofluoromethane dissolves grease and cleans electronic equipment. Tubeless tyres not a listed use.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Book: use rising since the 1960s with refrigerators, air conditioners, spray cases, solvents, foam constructions, pressed toiletries, micro-marine works in industries.
    • Green-house source pairs: key marks CFC–Automobile as the mismatch; left standing: CO₂–Thermal Power Stations, Nitrous Oxide–Waterlogged Paddy fields, Sulphur dioxide–Brick Kilns.
    • Refrigerator gas sold as Mafron, commonly a halonic hydrocarbon (book's key). Ammonia also used as coolant in big plants.
    • Chlorine and bromine atoms destroy stratospheric ozone. One chlorine atom can destroy over 100,000 ozone molecules before removal. ODS emitted at surface may take two to five years to reach the stratosphere.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • ODS releasing chlorine: CFCs, HCFCs, carbon tetrachloride, methyl chloroform. Releasing bromine: halons, methyl bromide.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION8 papers
    • Book's list (pp. 125–126): CFC, Halon-1211,1301, Nitrous oxide, Trichloroethane etc. Uses named in Q: CFC-12 refrigerators, Halon-1211 fire fighting.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Antarctic hole: formed with a prominent polar front, polar stratospheric clouds and inflow of CFCs. Nitric acid in the clouds reacts with CFCs, giving chlorine that destroys ozone photochemically. Clouds help convert chlorine compounds into chlorine atoms.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    Source check Book says the 1985 British team used a "total ozone mapping spectrometer" (pp. 125, 129). The British Antarctic Survey page could not be read to confirm this. Standard accounts credit ground-based Dobson spectrophotometer readings at Halley Bay, so avoid naming the instrument without checking.
    Source check Q0504 key (b) says methyl chloroform is not responsible for depletion (p. 126), but the book's own Q0503 note lists it as an ODS, and US EPA lists it too. Do not memorise it as harmless. Also, the book's list reads "Tricloroethlyn" (p. 125) and "Trichloroethane" (p. 126). source
    Source check

    U.P.P.C.S. 2008 keys carbon monoxide as not causing "ozone depletion in the troposphere", and its note lists water vapour, CO₂, methane, ozone, N₂O, CFCs (p. 126). That list is of greenhouse gases; the book locates depletion in the stratosphere elsewhere. Treat as a doubtful question.

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Explain why depletion is most severe near the poles.

      Ozone forms very slowly over polar regions, and polar stratospheric clouds help release chlorine. p. 125

    2. Sort into chlorine and bromine sources: halons, HCFCs, methyl bromide, carbon tetrachloride.

      Chlorine: HCFCs, carbon tetrachloride. Bromine: halons, methyl bromide. p. 126

    3. How long may an ODS take to reach the stratosphere, and how many ozone molecules may one chlorine atom destroy?

      Two to five years; over 100,000. p. 126

    4. Name the journal and year in which the Antarctic ozone hole was announced.

      Nature, May 1985. p. 125

    Measures for the Preservation of the Ozone Layer

    The Montreal Protocol phases out ozone-depleting substances; Kigali extends it to HFCs.

    • Montreal Protocol on Substances that Deplete the Ozone Layer: protocol to the Vienna Convention for the Protection of the Ozone Layer; phases out production and use of ODS.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’15 · 2 papers
    • Main ODS: chlorofluorocarbons, halons, carbon tetrachloride. Book: all banned after the Protocol.
    • Landmark multilateral agreement regulating production and consumption of nearly 100 man-made ODS chemicals. Parties meet once a year. COMMON TRAP Carbon Trade is not Montreal; it belongs to the Kyoto Protocol. → Ch 4
    • Other pairs left standing by key: First World Climate Conference–1979; First Earth Summit–Agenda 21; Earth Summit + 5–1997.
    • Tibetan Plateau ozone halo, 2005: team led by G.W. Kent Moore (University of Toronto, Canada). Ozone low over the centre, with a concentrated ring of excess ozone around the Plateau.
    • At high concentrations ozone can cause coughing, chest pain, lung damage. Book calls it highly volatile.
    • Day's themes: 2022 "Global cooperation protecting life on Earth"; 2024 "Montreal Protocol: Advancing Climate Action".REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    Source check Book's note to Q0514 says the ozone layer is depleted "mainly by carbon dioxide and chlorofluorocarbons" (p. 128). CO₂ is on neither of the book's ODS lists (pp. 125–127). Keep only the CFC half.
    Source check Q0511 asks about the Tibetan "ozone halo", but the book calls it an "ozone hole" (p. 127). Its description (low centre, surrounding ring of excess ozone) fits a halo. Record the name as asked.
    Source check

    Uttarakhand Mains 2002: key (b) says Sulphur dioxide and nitrogen oxides, "not nitric acid", cause acid rain (p. 129). The book's pollution chapter names nitric acid as an acid in acid rain (p. 232, → Ch 9). The gases are precursors; the acid is what falls. Its other pairs: Ozone–CFC; Rocket fuel–highly refined kerosene; Greenhouse Effect–CO₂, second largest cause after water vapour.

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Why is the ozone day fixed on 16 September?

      It marks the 1987 signing of the Montreal Protocol. p. 128

    2. Which substances does the Kigali Amendment phase down?

      Hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs). p. 129

    3. Correct this: "Carbon Trade comes under the Montreal Protocol."

      False. Montreal regulates ozone-depleting substances; carbon trading belongs to Kyoto. pp. 121, 128

    Effects of Ozone Layer Depletion

    Less ozone lets more biologically harmful UV reach the surface.

    • Harmful solar UV causes skin cancer. Long absorption by skin may cause genetic mutation in DNA, which can lead to skin cancer.
    • Assertion-Reason: ozone essential for biotic life because the layer protects the surface from high-energy radiation; R explains A. As a gas, ozone is poisonous to biotic life (direct, immediate harm to lungs and respiratory system).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Ozone layer absorbs 93–99% of the Sun's high-frequency UV light, potentially detrimental to life.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Without the ozone layer, UV would cause excessive damage to biological life.
    Source check P. 129 says the layer blocks "high wavelength" UV, while the Q0524 note says "high-frequency" UV on the same page. High frequency means short wavelength, so use the Q0524 wording.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the wavelength ranges of the three UV bands.

      UV-A 315–400 nm; UV-B 280–315 nm; UV-C 100–280 nm. p. 129

    2. Fill the missing labels: UV-B causes skin cancer, cataracts, sunburn and which plant effects?

      Hindrance in the growth of plants; harm to leaves. p. 129

    3. Why does UV-C not cause sunburn at the surface?

      Diatomic oxygen or ozone blocks it before it reaches the surface. p. 129

    Forest and Wildlife

    Book's reference sites (p. 130): WII-ENVIS Centre on Wildlife & Protected Areas; Wildlife Institute of India; State of Forest Report (FSI); World Wildlife Fund; Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change; Environment, Forest and Climate Change Department, Uttar Pradesh; National Tiger Conservation Authority.

    Forest and their types

    Forest type tracks rainfall, altitude and the coast: evergreen above 200 cm, moist deciduous at 100–200 cm, mangroves on tidal shores.

    Tropical evergreen and equatorial forests
    • Tropical evergreen: rainfall >200 cm; temp 15–30 °C; about 7% of Earth's land surface; more than half of world's plants and animals; mostly near equator.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • India: Western Ghats (western slope; Kerala, Karnataka); north-east (Arunachal Pradesh, Mizoram, Nagaland, Tripura, Jaintia & Khasi hills of Meghalaya); Andaman & Nicobar Islands.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • COMMON TRAPHimachal Pradesh: mainly Sub-Tropical Pine + Himalayan Moist Temperate forests, so not Tropical Wet Evergreen.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Maximum plant diversity: tropical evergreen forests. Greatest plant + animal diversity: tropical moist forests.
    • Tropical rainforest biome: 23.5°N–23.5°S; book: largest biome of world, about 8% of Earth's surface; >50% of world's biodiversity; rain all year; high heat + humidity make broad-leaved evergreen trees dominant.
    • Over 200 tree species per hectare; shade-tolerant shrubs, herbs, small trees, large woody vines climbing for sunlight; about 70–80% of known insect species.
    • Equatorial = tropical rain forests (>200 cm). All three features correct (IAS 2013): tall closely-set trees with continuous canopy; co-existence of many species; numerous epiphytes on upper canopy.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Densest + highest biomass: equatorial forest biome; high rainfall and high temperature all year is the correct reason (book: warm, wet climate, much sunshine).
    • Maximum plant biomass among ecosystems: tropical rain forest.
    • Amazon rain forest (Amazonia): moist broad-leaf forest over most of Amazon basin, South America; "Lungs of the Planet Earth" for absorbing CO₂ and releasing oxygen.
    Source check Book's rainforest figures are loose: about 7% of land (evergreen) and about 8% of surface (biome), both p. 130; biodiversity ">50%" and "about 80%" (p. 130; 80% also Q0527). Don't treat either as the only figure.
    Source check Book states the Amazon produces more than 20% of Earth's oxygen (pp. 130, 134). This popular figure is widely disputed; no source page was opened in this run, so no link is given. Verify before quoting.
    Tropical moist deciduous (monsoon) forests
    • Rainfall 100–200 cm; Teak (Sagaun) is the dominant tree.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Other commercially important species: Bamboo, Sal, Shisham, Sandalwood, Khair, Kusum, Arjun, Mulberry.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Broad leaves shed once a year; about 6–8 weeks in spring/early summer when moisture is short.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Also called monsoon forests; found in India, Myanmar, Thailand, areas of south-east Asia.
    • Maximum area under teak: Madhya Pradesh (book cites "IFSR 2023, % of RFA").
    • Tropical deciduous forests most extensive in Asia. Book's explanation describes deciduous forests broadly: warm, moist summers + mild winters; mainly Northern Hemisphere, Eastern North America, East Asia, Europe.
    Himalayan and southern mountain forests
    • Book labels temperate forests as "deciduous forests". Himalaya shows succession from tropical to tundra with altitude.
    • Southern mountain forests (Peninsular India): Western Ghats + Nilgiris. Closer to tropics, only 1,500 m high: temperate on higher regions, subtropical on lower regions, especially Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka.
    • Naturally seen in the Himalayas: Oak, Rhododendron. Not Sandalwood (tropical deciduous; mainly South India; negligible chance in Himalaya).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Subtropical forests: North-West (except Kashmir), Khasi hills, Nagaland, Manipur. Pine main tree; broad-leaved Oak in highly humid areas; rhododendron shrubs.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Uttarakhand: Oak-Rhododendron are characteristic of temperate forests. Species: Banj Oak (Quercus leucotrichophora), Burans (Rhododendron arboreum), Kaphal (Myrica esculenta), Aynor (Lyonia ovalifolia), Ilex dipyrena, Kharsu oak (Quercus semecarpifolia).
    Notable tree species
    • Red Sanders (Pterocarpus santalinus): endemic, southern Eastern Ghats, South India; tropical dry deciduous forests; Palkonda and Seshachalam hills. Not a tropical rainforest tree.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Red Sanders wood white, turning red through a red gummy juice; valued for rich red colour; mainly exported to China and Japan; used in traditional herbal medicine, toys, worship materials.
    • Taxus tree: naturally in Himalaya; listed in Red Data Book; source of anti-cancer drug Taxol (breast and lung cancer per book).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    Source check Book keys IAS 2010 as (d), accepting that Taxol is effective against Parkinson's disease (pp. 131, 135). NCI's list of FDA-approved paclitaxel uses covers Kaposi sarcoma, breast, non-small cell lung and ovarian cancer, with no mention of Parkinson's. Treat Parkinson's as the book's claim only. source
    Mangrove forests
    • "Mangrove" means two things: tidal swamp ecosystem (tropical deltas, estuaries, lagoons, islands) and its characteristic tree species.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Specialised ecosystems of tropical + subtropical tidal coasts in saline water. Stabilise shoreline, bulwark against the sea (IAS 1996: R correctly explains A).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’96
    • Protect biodiversity; link between sea and coast; check coastal erosion and wave damage. Unique adaptations to harsh coastal conditions.
    • CORE CONCEPTSafety hedge after 2004 Tsunami: trees are not uprooted by storms and tides because of extensive roots (not because of canopy shelter).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Largest mangrove area: Sunderbans delta; Sundari tree popular.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • IAS 1995 quotation (monoculture, palm cultivation, mangrove destruction, drying freshwater pockets): book keys Sunderbans, saying only that the place "seems near" Sundarban.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’95
    • Coringa: Godavari delta; among largest mangrove extensions; diverse mangrove trees and birds.
    • Protected mangrove region: Chorao Island, Goa.
    • Andaman & Nicobar has mangrove + evergreen + deciduous forests together; about 608.29 km² mangrove (ISFR 2023).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Bhitarkanika: deltas of Brahmani and Baitarni, Kendrapada district, Odisha (not West Bengal; not Vansadhara/Subarnarekha); Ramsar site 2002. → Ch 3
    • Coastal Odisha: cyclones of Bay of Bengal hit more often than any other state; massive deforestation in Mahanadi delta added to the risk.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Book keys Q0547 as (a), "R explains A" (p. 135), but its own text says only that Mahanadi delta deforestation "added the risks arising due to cyclones" (p. 135). Mangrove loss raises vulnerability; it isn't the cause of cyclone frequency.

    Eco-regions and forest matches
    • Book's incorrect pair: Tarai Duar – Broadleaf forests. Correctly matched: South-Western Ghats – Moist; Rann of Kachchh – Grasslands; Eastern Deccan Plateau – Moist.
    • Western Himalayan region: Kashmir to Kumaon; temperate zone rich in Chir, Pine, broadleaf forests.
    • Book's match: Tropical – Silent Valley; Coniferous – Himachal Pradesh; Mangroves – Sunderbans; Deciduous – Rajasthan.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. State the rainfall band for tropical evergreen and for tropical moist deciduous forests.

      Above 200 cm; 100–200 cm. Teak dominates the second. p. 130

    2. Correct this: "Himachal Pradesh has Tropical Wet Evergreen forests like Mizoram."

      False. Himachal is mainly Sub-Tropical Pine and Himalayan Moist Temperate; Mizoram and Arunachal have Tropical Wet Evergreen. p. 131

    3. Fill in the missing band: foothills deciduous → 1,000–2,000 m ____ → 3,000–4,000 m alpine.

      Wet temperate (pines at 1,500–1,750 m; blue pine and spruce at 2,225–3,048 m lie between). p. 130

    4. In one sentence, why do mangroves act as a safety hedge?

      Their extensive roots keep them from being uprooted by storms and tides. p. 135

    5. Name Bhitarkanika's river deltas and district.

      Brahmani and Baitarni; Kendrapada, Odisha. p. 131

    Benefits and Uses of Forest

    Natural resources are graded by development stage; forests count as renewable because they can be reused after sustainable exploitation.

    • Resources by development stage: Potential, Actual, Reserve, Stock.
    • Potential: located in an area, usable in future. Actual: surveyed, quantity and quality determined, in use now.
    • Development of an actual resource depends on technology and cost of production (A and R both true; R does not explain A).
    • Forest is renewable because it can be reused after sustainable exploitation. It also enhances environment (absorbs CO₂, produces oxygen), but that is not why it is renewable.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Aluminium = "green metal" for eco-friendly sustainability. It can substitute wood, but saving forests is not the substantive reason (A true, R false).
    • Living root bridges (tree shaping): southern part of Meghalaya.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Distinguish potential from actual resources.

      Potential exist and are usable in future; actual are surveyed, measured and in present use. p. 136

    2. Why is "forests enhance the environment" a wrong reason for "forests are renewable"?

      Renewability comes from reuse after sustainable exploitation; both claims are true but unconnected. p. 136

    3. Name the state with living root bridges.

      Meghalaya. p. 137

    Deforestation and Its Impacts

    Population pressure drives forest loss in developing countries; urbanization is a cause, not an effect.

    • Forest loss in India is directly linked to demographic transition; forest cover negatively related to population growth (R explains A). Drivers: population growth, density, distribution, migration, urbanization; more food demand converts forest to farmland.
    • Generally negative link in developing countries; not implied for developed countries, where forest area rises through awareness and sensitivity.
    • Book's main cause of forest loss: industrialization. Urbanization also cuts forest; empty land goes to agriculture.
    • COMMON TRAPUrbanization is a cause, not an impact; towns and cities expanding shrink forest area.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Nagaland hills turning barren mainly through shifting cultivation.
    • Jharkhand: forest fire is a major issue affecting climate; Hazaribagh, Jamshedpur, Palamu, Bokaro; problem of reserved and conserved forests.
    • Over 90% of forest fires anthropogenic: deliberate (personal gain, rivalry), negligence or accident. NTFP collectors ignite fires that may spread in extreme heat.
    • Main NTFP linked to fire in Jharkhand: Mahua flowers (Madhuca indica), boiled with Sal (Shorea robusta) seeds for a local beverage; pickers burn dry leaves to clear a small circle under a tree.
    • Valparai, Coimbatore district, Tamil Nadu: specialised in plantation (among options).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Identify and correct: "Urbanization is an impact of deforestation."

      False. It is a cause; drying of water sources, biodiversity loss and soil erosion are impacts. p. 138

    2. Which forest product links to Jharkhand's fires, and how?

      Mahua flowers. Pickers burn dry leaves to clear the ground, and the fire spreads in hot weather. p. 138

    3. Explain the population-forest relationship in one sentence.

      Population growth raises food demand and converts forest to farmland, chiefly in developing countries. pp. 137–138

    National Forest Policy (1952)

    The policy sets the one-third forest target; 1952 classified forests while 1988 set programmes.

    • NFP 1952 classes: Protected/Conserved, National, Village, Tree Lands. National Parks are not a class.
    • CORE CONCEPTCurrent policy: one-third (33%) of total land under forest or tree cover.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’99 · 4 papers
    • Mountain and hilly regions: two-thirds under such cover, to prevent erosion and land degradation and stabilise fragile ecosystems.
    • NFP 1988 includes: afforestation and wasteland development; reforestation and replantation in existing forests; wood substitutes and other fuels. Not included: wide use of insecticides and pesticides.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’96
    • Draft National Forest Policy, 2018 cleared by various ministries at a meeting on November 21, 2019.
    • International Day of Forests: 21 March, proclaimed by United Nations General Assembly in 2012; 2018 theme "Forests and Sustainable Cities"; 2025 theme "Forests and Food" (p. 139: "The future of food begins in the forests"; book prints "beings"). ↗
    Basic objectives (NFP)
    1. Environmental stability: preserve and restore ecological balance disturbed by depletion of forests.
    2. Conserve natural heritage: remaining natural forests, flora and fauna, biological diversity, genetic resources.
    3. Check soil erosion and denudation in catchments of rivers, lakes, reservoirs; mitigate floods and droughts; retard reservoir siltation.
    4. Check extension of sand-dunes in the desert areas of Rajasthan and along coastal tracts.
    5. Substantially increase forest/tree cover through massive afforestation and social forestry, especially on denuded, degraded, unproductive lands.
    6. Meet fuel-wood, fodder, minor forest produce and small timber needs of rural and tribal populations.
    7. Increase forest productivity for essential national needs.
    8. Efficient use of forest produce; maximum substitution of wood.
    9. Massive people's movement involving women, to minimise pressure on existing forests.
    Source check Book keys Q0569 as (c), statements 1 and 4 (UDA/LDA 2001), but the same book lists one-third forestation as a main objective (p. 139) and social forestry among objectives (p. 140), which the key leaves out.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the forest-cover targets for the country and for hill regions.

      One-third; two-thirds. p. 139

    2. Which item is excluded from NFP 1988: wood substitutes, or wide pesticide use?

      Wide pesticide use. p. 139

    3. Name the four forest classes of NFP 1952.

      Protected/Conserved, National, Village, Tree Lands. p. 138

    India State of Forest Report, 2023

    ISFR 2023 is the latest report in the book: forest cover 21.76% of geographical area; forest + tree cover 25.17%.

    Report and method
    • Biennial; released by MoEF&CC December 2024; 18th in series. Covers growing stock in forests and trees outside forests, bamboo, carbon stock and other parameters.
    • Book links Nagar Van Yojana to India's aim of additional 2.5–3 billion tonnes CO₂-equivalent carbon sink by 2030.
    • Forest cover: all patches with canopy density ≥10% and area ≥1 ha, irrespective of land use, ownership or species. Mapped at 1:50000.
    • Increase in forest cover is not uniform.
    • 33% target is from National Forest Policy, 1988; also a key target of Strategy for New India @ 75 (NITI Aayog, 2018).
    National figures
    • Forest cover 21.76% (7,15,342.61 km²) + tree cover 3.41% (1,12,014.34 km²) = 25.17% of geographical area (82.7 million ha).
    • Forest cover +156.41 km²; forest + tree cover +1445.81 km² vs 2021 assessment.
    • Book's dense forest: 12.37% of geographical area; very dense 3.12%, moderately dense 9.36%. see flag
    • Dense forest = 57.34% (4,10,175.48 km²) of forest cover, not about 40%. National Forestry Action Programme aims at one-third of India under tree/forest cover.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’05
    • Increase observed in very dense forest. Top three by forest cover increase: Mizoram 241.73, Gujarat 180.07, Odisha 151.89 km².
    • Highest forest cover increase inside RFA/GW: Mizoram 192.92, Odisha 118.17, Karnataka 93.14, West Bengal 64.79 km². Reasons: plantation and agro-forestry.
    • Forest + tree cover improvement credited to afforestation, better forest management, community participation, decrease in forest fires; top states Chhattisgarh, Uttar Pradesh, Odisha, Rajasthan.
    • Northeast: forest cover down 327.30 km²; forest + tree cover 174394.70 km² = 67% of these states' area.
    • Recorded Forest Area 23.59% of India; recorded forest = area recorded as forest in government records.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    State rankings
    • Largest forest cover by area: Madhya Pradesh (77073.44), then Arunachal Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha, Maharashtra; MP also has largest recorded forest area.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • CORE CONCEPTHighest % of geographical area (states): Mizoram 85.34%, then Arunachal 78.67, Meghalaya 75.65, Manipur 74.29, Nagaland 73.72. Not Nagaland, as one option claimed.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • Top five States/UTs by %: Lakshadweep, Mizoram, A&N Islands, Arunachal Pradesh, Meghalaya (all >75%).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • 33–75%: Manipur, Nagaland, Tripura, Goa, Kerala, Sikkim, Uttarakhand, Chhattisgarh, Dadra & Nagar Haveli and Daman & Diu, Assam, Odisha, Jammu & Kashmir (12 states/UTs; 17 in all above 33%, stated for ISFR 2021 in Q0599 and repeated p. 140).
    • Largest very dense forest: Arunachal 20,985.32 km² (Maharashtra 9865.62, Odisha 7224.42, MP 7021.31).
    • Least forest cover (by area and %) among states: Haryana.
    • Descending % (2019 → 2023): Manipur 75.46 → 74.29; Odisha 33.15 → 33.67; Maharashtra 16.50 → 16.53; Haryana 3.62 → 3.65.
    • Ascending % (ISFR 2023): Maharashtra 16.53, MP 25.00, Odisha 33.67, Chhattisgarh 41.28.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • RFA highest in Himachal Pradesh (68.16%) among that question's options (forest cover remains Mizoram).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    Forest fires
    • Fire season 2023-24: 203544 forest fires. Fire season 2020-21: around 3,45,989.
    • Severe fires in many forest types, especially dry deciduous; evergreen, semi-evergreen, montane temperate less prone.
    • States: Uttarakhand 21033, Odisha 20,973, Chhattisgarh 18,950; Andhra Pradesh 4th.
    • Districts: Gadchiroli (Maharashtra) 7042; Alluri Sitharama Raju (AP) 6399; Bijapur (Chhattisgarh) 5018.
    • Maximum fire-affected area: AP 5286.76, Maharashtra 4095.04, Telangana 3983.28 km². Highest % RFA/GW affected: Telangana 14.82, AP 13.94, Bihar 10.71.
    Source check Book's heading reads "Increase in forest fires" (p. 141), but its numbers fall from 3,45,989 (2020-21) to 203544 (2023-24), and p. 141 credits "a decrease in forest fires".
    Other findings
    • Hill districts: forest cover 40.00% of their area; 2023 increase 234.14 km².
    • Tribal districts (2021 assessment): 37.53% of their area; −655 km² inside RFA/GW, +600 km² outside.
    • Mangrove cover 4991.68 km²; down 7.43 km² vs 2021. Max increase: AP 13.01, Maharashtra 12.39, Karnataka 2.54 km².
    • Bamboo-bearing area 154670 km²; +5227 km² vs ISFR 2021.
    • Growing stock 6429.64 million m³ (4478.89 inside forest, 1950.75 outside recorded forest); +262.32 million m³.
    • Forest carbon stock 7,285.5 Mt; +81.5 Mt vs 2021; annual increase 40.75 Mt.
    • Soil organic carbon: largest pool; 55.06% of forest carbon.
    • Carbon stock by state: Arunachal 1021.16 Mt, MP 608.42 Mt, Chhattisgarh 504.61 Mt.

    Second largest mangrove area: Gujarat (1164.06 km²). Mangroves: shrubs or small trees of coastal saline water.
