    IUCN

    IUCN's Red List tracks threatened species; Indian fauna questions test status, habitat and scientific names.

    • IUCN: established 1948; headquarters Gland, Switzerland; works on nature conservation and sustainable use of natural resources; seeks practical solutions to environmental and developmental challenges.
    • Red list gives information on endangered plants and animals.
    • At question time (2003), Indian crocodile and Asian Elephant both listed endangered. At present: Mugger (Crocodylus palustris) vulnerable; Asian Elephant endangered.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’03
    • Gharial (Gavialis gangeticus; fish-eating Crocodilia): Ganga tributaries; mostly India and Nepal; most numbers in Chambal and Girwa. Best place to see it: Chambal river. IUCN: critically endangered.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Gharial, Leatherback turtle, Swamp deer: all in book's "endangered/threatened" group (IAS 2013).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Kharai camel (swimming camel): only in Kutch, Gujarat; swims up to three km into sea for mangroves, its primary food; lives in coastal and dry ecosystems. Statement that it stays wild and can't be domesticated is wrong.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Recognised by National Bureau of Animal Genetic Resources as a separate breed, one of nine in India (book p. 152 says "species" and calls it endangered); merchant community's livelihood depends on it.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Whale shark: India's largest fish, one of largest in world; up to 50 feet. Habitat: coasts of Australia and South Africa; comes to Gujarat coast March–May for gestation; protection programme 2004.
    • Naturally in India: Star Tortoise, Monitor lizard, Pygmy hog. Not Spider monkey: tropical forests of Central and South America, southern Mexico to Brazil.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Pugmark technique estimates populations of wild animals; identifies animals by footprints ("Pug" means foot in Hindi); each individual species has a distinct pugmark.
    AnimalScientific name
    Onager (Asiatic wild Ass)Equus hemionusPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’02
    Swamp deer (Barasingha)Rucervus duvaucelii (Cervus duvauceli in Q)PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’02
    ChinkaraGazella bennettiiPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’02
    NilgaiBoselaphus tragocamelusPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’02
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name IUCN's founding year and headquarters.

      1948; Gland, Switzerland. p. 152

    2. Which listed animal is not naturally found in India: Pygmy hog or Spider monkey?

      Spider monkey (Central and South America). p. 153

    3. Correct: "Kharai camel lives only in the wild."

      False. It is a recognised breed used by graziers, feeding on mangroves. p. 153

    4. State the IUCN status of the Gharial given in the book.

      Critically endangered. p. 152

    Indian efforts related to Conservation of Forest Resources

    India's framework joins wildlife and forest statutes, specialised bodies, people's movements and state schemes.

    • India: 2.4% of world's land; about 8% of known biodiversity; about 7.28% of world's animal species ('Faunal Diversity of India', J.R.B. Alfred). Q0644 marked (*): 8% and 7.28% match no single option.
    Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972
    • First comprehensive wildlife law; passed by Parliament; assented by President 9 September 1972. Protects wild animals in India; aims to stop illegal hunting and trafficking.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Book: 66 sections in 7 chapters, 6 schedules. see update
    • Covers wild animals, birds and plants, not wild animals only.
    • Tiger is in Schedule I; a tortoise declared protected under Schedule I gets the same level of protection as the tiger.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Schedule VI plant: cultivation prohibited except under licence from Chief Wild Life Warden or an officer authorised by the State Government.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • Gharial, Indian Wild Ass and Wild Buffalo can't be hunted by anyone except under provisions in law.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Section 39(1): every wild animal is State Government property; if hunted in a sanctuary or National Park declared by Central Government, Central Government property. Hence "property of the government" held correct.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Equal protection inside and outside protected areas. Capture/killing only if animal has become a danger to human life or is diseased/disabled beyond recovery, by Chief Wildlife Warden; mere apprehension of danger is not enough.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22

    Community reserves fall under protected areas with marine protected areas, National Parks, sanctuaries and conservation reserves.

    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's 6 schedules is the pre-2022 position (p. 154). Wild Life (Protection) Amendment Bill passed Lok Sabha 2 Aug 2022, Rajya Sabha 8 Dec 2022: total schedules cut to four, vermin schedule removed, new schedule for CITES specimens. The page does not state the plant schedule's new number; check current text before quoting "Schedule VI". source
    Forest and environment statutes
    • Chronology: WLPA 1972 → Project Tiger 1973 → Project Elephant 1992 → Biological Diversity Act 2002.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Forest Conservation Act 1980; Environment Protection Act 1986; Air Act 1981; Water Act 1974.
    • Forest (Conservation) Act enacted 27 December 1980; book: implemented from 25 October 1980.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • EPA 1986: lays down standards for emission/discharge of pollutants. Doesn't empower Government to state public-participation requirements.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Indian Forest Act, 1927, as amended in 2017: bamboo grown in non-forest areas removed from definition of trees; tribals and poor farmers can use it economically.
    • Felling/transport permitted for bamboo grown on non-forest areas (Indian Forest (Amendment) Ordinance, 2017); the claim that forest dwellers may fell bamboo grown on forest areas is wrong.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006: minor forest produce = all non-timber forest produce of plant origin, including bamboo; ownership of MFP to forest dwellers.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • "Critical wildlife habitat" defined only in FRA 2006; Baigas first in India to get Habitat Rights; Ministry of Tribal Affairs (not MoEFCC) decides Habitat Rights for Primitive and Vulnerable Tribal Groups.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • New Forest Inventory Design (2016): revisit time cut from 20 years to 5.
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Forest (Conservation) Amendment Bill, 2023 passed Lok Sabha 26 Jul and Rajya Sabha 2 Aug 2023. It limits the Act to certain land, excludes land converted to non-forest use before 12 Dec 1996, exempts some border-security projects and small roadside amenities, and allows zoos, safaris and eco-tourism. Book's 1980 dates remain the original enactment. source
    Institutions
    BodyKey facts
    Forest Research InstituteDehradun, Uttarakhand; under ICFRE; est. 1906 as Imperial Forest Research Institute; deemed university by UGC, 1991; trained forest officers and rangers.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    Indian Council of Forestry Research and Education (ICFRE)Apex body of national forestry research; HQ Dehradun; MoU with TIFAC for forest-based communities.
    IIFMBhopal; autonomous; est. 1982 under Ministry of Agriculture, later under Environment and Forest Ministry.
    Survey of IndiaNational survey and mapping body, set up 1767; under Department of Science and Technology; not an environment body.
    National Bureau of Plant Genetic ResourcesNew Delhi; est./rechristened 1977; plant introduction, germplasm augmentation; ten regional stations: Shimla, Jodhpur, Thrissur, Akola, Shillong, Bhowali, Cuttack, Hyderabad, Ranchi, Srinagar.
    Animal Welfare Board of IndiaStatutory advisory body; est. 1962 under Section 4, Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act, 1960 (not EPA 1986).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    National Tiger Conservation AuthorityStatutory body under MoEFCC.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    National Ganga River Basin AuthoritySet up 20 February 2009; chaired by Prime Minister; Union Ministers concerned and Chief Ministers of Ganga states as members. Later dissolved → Ch 10.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    National Research Centre for AgroforestryJhansi, UP; unit of ICAR (Indian Council of Agricultural Research) est. 1988; renamed Central Agroforestry Research Institute (CAFRI) December 2014; about 10 km from Jhansi station; "KRISHIVANIKI".
    National Afforestation and Eco-Development Board (NAEB)Set up 1992.
    People's movements and tribal practice
    • Chipko (1970s): against large-scale forest cutting (deforestation).REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Raini village, Joshimath (Chamoli): Forest Department auctioned about 680 ha; Gaura Devi led hundreds of women who clung to trees.
    • Chandi Prasad Bhatt: protagonist; founded the organisation that became Chipko's base; Ramon Magsaysay Award 1982.
    • Sundarlal Bahuguna: leading role; book accepts him as a leader of Chipko (MPPCS 2014).
    • Maiti Movement: Kalyan Singh Rawat, teacher at Government Inter College, began 1995 at Gwaldam. Newly-wed bride plants a sapling on leaving home ("mait" = married woman's parental home); groom gives parents money for its care.
    • Post-Kargil: women of Ochati village + Border Security Force raised a Maiti forest dedicated to soldiers: Shaurya Van (Gallantry Forest).
    • Gond and Korku: plant fruit-bearing saplings during a month-long ecological festival every year.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    Added concept
    • UPSC Prelims 2026 asked whether the yearly arrival of Amur Falcons at Doyang Lake, Nagaland, from Mongolia reflects sustained local conservation (statement 1), advanced tracking technology (2) or permanent residency in India (3). Coaching-consensus key: 1 only; no official answer seen; not in the book.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Source check Q0646 (MPPCS 2016): MPPSC held (c) Sunderlal Bahuguna; the book holds (d) Chandi Prasad Bhatt and says the commission is wrong (p. 160). Know both names; expect either as "leader".
    Awards, schemes and programmes
    • Rajiv Gandhi Wildlife Conservation Award: annual; two awards of Rupees One Lakh; for education and research institutions, organisations, forest and wildlife officers/research scholars/scientists, wildlife conservationists.
    • Amrita Devi Smriti award: Forest Department, Government of Rajasthan; cash award to individuals/institutions for wildlife protection.
    • SAVE (Saving Asia's Vultures from Extinction): 10-year programme launched February 2011 by RSPB (British Royal Society for the Protection of Birds), Zoological Society of London and Nepal government; vultures poisoned by medication in carrion.
    • "Apna Van Apna Dhan": Himachal Pradesh; promotes afforestation.
    • Mukhyamantri Jan Van Yojana: Jharkhand, under CM Raghubar Das: greener area, less pressure on notified forests, groundwater conservation through plantation, income from trees.
    • Mukhyamantri Vriksharopan Protsahan Yojana (Chhattisgarh): began 1 June 2021 (not 1 July); Rs. 10,000 per acre incentive; open to all farmers, village and joint forest management committees; CAMPA fund usable for high-tech nursery.
    • Jharkhand Wildlife Management Plan: 10 years, for wildlife sanctuaries.
    • Jharkhand Reserve Forests: all activities prohibited unless permitted. Latitude 22° 00' N–24° 37' N; longitude 83° 15' E–87° 01' E; area 79,716 km² (2.42% of India); RFA 25118 km² (Reserve Forest 4500 + Protected 18922 + Unclassed 1696), about 31.51%.
    • Project Elephant: launched February 1992, centrally sponsored; protects elephants, habitats, corridors; tackles man-animal conflict; welfare of domesticated elephants too (wild and captive, not wild only).
    • Green India Mission: one of eight missions under National Action Plan on Climate Change; launched February 2014.
    • UPPCS 2020 keys population growth as "not a major cause" of wildlife extinction; book names loss of natural habitat, forest fire and illegal trade as major, adding that population growth raises competition for food and shelter.
    Source check Q0658's key (p. 162) sits uneasily with p. 137, where population growth drives forest loss. Treat it as that exam's key, not a general rule.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Put in sequence: Project Elephant, WLPA, Biological Diversity Act, Project Tiger.

      WLPA 1972, Project Tiger 1973, Project Elephant 1992, Biological Diversity Act 2002. p. 156

    2. Who declares and who manages a Community Reserve?

      State Government declares (s. 36C); Community Reserve Management Committee manages (s. 36D). p. 157

    3. Correct: "Animal Welfare Board of India was established under the EPA, 1986."

      False. Under Section 4 of the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act, 1960, in 1962. p. 161

    4. Who led the Raini village protest and how?

      Gaura Devi; hundreds of women clung to trees. p. 160

    5. Which ministry decides Habitat Rights for PVTGs?

      Ministry of Tribal Affairs. p. 162

    Global efforts of the Conservation of Forest

    Global action spans land-use treaties, forest declarations, country-level forest shares and observance days.

    Forest area by country (World Bank 2022 est.)
    Forest % of land areaCountries
    >90Suriname 94.5; Guyana 93.5; Micronesia 92.1; Gabon 91.2; Palau 90.4; Solomon Island 90.1
    70–90Finland 73.7; Seychelles 73.3; Lao PDR 71.6
    50–70Guinea-Bissau 69.8; Sweden 68.7; Japan 68.4; Belize 55.0; Venezuela 52.3
    25–50Russian Federation 49.8; Indonesia 48.0; Vietnam 47.2; Mozambique 46.1; Tuvalu 33.3
    <25India 24.4; China 23.8; Pakistan 4.7; Afghanistan 1.9
    • Among China, India, Indonesia, Japan: Japan highest (68.4%).
    • Among Japan, Indonesia, Suriname, Guyana: Suriname highest (94.5%).
    • Bhutan: constitutional provision (Q0667: "resolution") to keep forest on at least 60% of land; about 71.6% forested (WB 2022 est.).
    Conventions and declarations
    • United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD), established 1994: sole legally binding international agreement linking environment and development to sustainable land management; drives SDG 15 and Land Degradation Neutrality.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • UNCCD aims at effective action through innovative national programmes and supportive international partnerships; bottom-up approach, local participation. No special South Asia/North Africa focus or allocation of funds to them.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • New York Declaration on Forests: first endorsed at UN Secretary-General's Climate Summit, New York, 2014; voluntary, not legally binding; cut natural forest loss in half by 2020, strive to end by 2030.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Also calls for restoring forests and croplands larger than India's area; meeting goals cuts 4.5–8.8 billion tons carbon pollution a year (about current US emissions).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Endorsed by over 200 entities: more than 50 governments, 50 big companies, 50 civil society and indigenous organisations. Not endorsed by India.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    WWF, observance days and tiger forum
    • WWF (World Wide Fund for Nature, founded 1961): symbol Giant Panda (Ailuropoda melanoleuca), for all endangered species able to thrive in their natural range.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • Giant Panda habitat: temperate broad-leaf and mixed forests of Southeast China (book's wording); about 1864 in China.
    • World Wildlife Day 3 March: proposed at CITES CoP 16 (March 2013); United Nations General Assembly 68th session decided 20 December 2013; 2025 theme "Wildlife Conservation Finance: Investing in People and Planet". Q0662 marked (*): date not among options.
    • India's Wildlife Week: 2nd to 8th October. World Environment Day: June 5. World Forestry Day/International Day of Forests: March 21.
    • First International Tiger Forum (World Tiger Summit): 21–24 November 2010, Saint Petersburg, Russia.
    Other tested points
    • Miyawaki method: creates mini forests in urban areas; fast forest cover on degraded land, using native trees and natural regeneration principles.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Hyderabad: recognised twice as Tree City of the World (2020 and 2021) by Arbor Day Foundation and FAO, not UNCDF. Statement 2 correct (commitment to grow and maintain urban forests). Mumbai recognised 2021.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • Arbor Day Foundation: non-profit, founded 1972 in Nebraska, USA, by John Rosenow; largest membership organisation for tree planting.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    • WWF founded 1961. June–July 2000: tigers in Nandankanan zoo died of Trypanosomiasis (statement said "13 lions"). Eucalyptus called environmental enemy, not friend. Biggest aquarium in India: Kolkata.
    • Desert plants: hard waxy leaves, tiny leaves, thorns all limit water loss; wax cuts evaporation. → Ch 10PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Source check Q0668 (UPPCS 2002) is keyed (*): the book says no option fits; its text has tigers, not the statement's 13 lions, and calls eucalyptus an environmental enemy (p. 165).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name the sole legally binding land-degradation treaty and its year.

      UNCCD, 1994. p. 164

    2. Is the New York Declaration binding, and did India endorse it?

      Not binding; India did not endorse. p. 164

    3. Which three days fall on March 21, March 3 and June 5?

      International Day of Forests; World Wildlife Day; World Environment Day. pp. 162–163

    4. Explain what the Miyawaki method does in one sentence.

      It builds quick mini forests on degraded land using native trees, following natural regeneration. p. 163

    Sanctuary/Biosphere Reserve

    National Park

    Protected areas are a principal method of conserving biodiversity. National Parks are the strictest category; sanctuaries allow limited activity.

    India's protected-area network
    Category20182020Feb 2025 (ENVIS)
    National Parks104104106
    Wildlife Sanctuaries552562574
    Conservation Reserves8994145
    Community Reserves147216309
    Total protected areas––1134 = 187162.91 km² = 5.69% of geographical area
    • Four PA types: National Parks, Wildlife Sanctuaries, Conservation Reserves, Community Reserves.
    • Most wildlife PAs are surrounded by dense forests, which is very beneficial.
    • National Board for Wildlife (NBWL), chaired by the Prime Minister: policy framework for wildlife conservation.
    • Most NPs (Feb 2025): Madhya Pradesh 11, Assam 7; Andaman & Nicobar, Kerala, Maharashtra, Uttarakhand, West Bengal 6 each.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’08 · 2 papers
    • Most sanctuaries: Andaman & Nicobar Islands 96 (ENVIS, Feb 2025).
    • Assam Cabinet (Feb 2025) cleared its 8th NP, Sikhna Jwhwlao; not shown as the 107th on wiienvis.nic.in.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • MP declared Dr. Bhimrao Ambedkar Sanctuary, Sagar district (April 2025); not on the ENVIS website.

    National Park vs Sanctuary vs Biosphere Reserve
    • NP eligibility: rich biodiversity, almost pristine habitat; of ecological, faunal, floral, geomorphological and hydrological importance; needs protection and conservation.
    • BR: representative parts of natural and cultural landscapes over a large area of terrestrial or coastal/marine ecosystems.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    Source check Book says Biosphere Reserves "entertain no human activity in their sphere" (p.167). Its own banner on that page allows settlements and crop cultivation in the transition zone, grazing and tourism in the buffer, and p.170 says each reserve reconciles conservation with sustainable use. Only the core is free of human activity.
    Jim Corbett National Park
    • CORE CONCEPT India's first and oldest NP: established 1936 as Hailey National Park; first NP in mainland Asia.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • Renamed after James Edward Corbett, noted hunter turned conservationist.
    • Uttarakhand: NP in Nainital district. Corbett Tiger Reserve spans Pauri, Nainital, Almora.
    • NP 521 km²; Tiger Reserve 1288.31 km² after additions.
    • Project Tiger launched here, 1973.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION7 papers
    • Fauna: Royal Bengal Tiger, Asiatic Elephant, reptiles, birds.
    • Corbett and Rajaji use remote sensing and GIS for wildlife management; book's answer: small-scale aerial photographs suit this.

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Who may alter a National Park's boundary, and how does this differ from a sanctuary's permitted activity?

      Only on a State Legislature resolution. A park allows no human activity except as the Chief Wildlife Warden permits; a sanctuary allows limited activity. p. 170

    2. Correct this: "Biosphere Reserves are declared to conserve a few specific species."

      False. They conserve in situ all forms of life with their support system. p. 170

    3. Reconstruct Rajaji's formation with years.

      Rajaji 1948, Motichur 1964, Chilla 1977 merged 1983; Tiger Reserve 2015. p. 168

    4. Name the rivers of Corbett and the park's original name.

      Ramganga and Kosi; Hailey National Park (1936). pp. 167–168

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Arrange earliest to latest: Silent Valley, Kanha, Kaziranga, Corbett.

      Corbett 1936, Kanha 1955, Kaziranga 1974, Silent Valley 1984. p. 174

    2. Which park is the last natural habitat of the Sangai, and what are phumdis?

      Keibul Lamjao, on Loktak Lake. Phumdis are floating decomposed plant materials. pp. 175, 180

    3. Correct: "Pin Valley NP is in Jammu and Kashmir."

      False. It is in Lahaul and Spiti, Himachal Pradesh. p. 181

    4. What makes Kanha's Barasingha sub-species distinctive?

      Hard-ground Barasingha, exclusively graminivorous. p. 177

    Biosphere Reserve

    Biosphere reserves conserve genetic variation of species while reconciling conservation with sustainable use, through concentric zones.

    Concept and zones
    • "Biological diversity": first used by Raymond F. Dasmann, 1968, in A Different Kind of Country. Contracted form "biodiversity": coined by W.G. Rosen, 1985.
    • BR programme: effective method of conserving genetic variation of species. Reserves cover terrestrial, marine and coastal ecosystems.
    • Core area(s): strictly protected ecosystem conserving landscapes, ecosystems, species, genetic variation.
    • India: 18 BRs set up by MoEFCC, 12 within UNESCO-MAB (book's position).
    • Keyed pair (UPPCS 2013): Biosphere reserves – Edward Suess; the other three pairs were wrong as printed (see note).
    Source check "Biosphere reserve" first used by Edward Suess (p.183, the keyed answer). Not verified against an outside source here; treat as the exam key, and check before quoting. On "biodiversity", p.183 credits Dasmann alone, while p.182 separates Dasmann ("biological diversity") from Rosen (the contraction). On p.183 "Ecology" is credited to Reiter and Ernst Haeckel; Ecosystem to A.G. Tansley.
    UNESCO-MAB list
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book: 12 of 18 in the World Network. PIB (3 Nov 2025): India has 18 notified BRs (about 91,425 km²), 13 recognised by UNESCO, after the Cold Desert BR was included in September 2025. PIB
    • IAS 2008 framed four reserves on the network (then Nilgiri, Gulf of Mannar, Sunderbans, Nanda Devi). Marked (*) because the list has since grown to include Khangchendzonga.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’08
    • Rann of Kachchh: not in UNESCO's network.
    • Nanda Devi BR: around Nanda Devi peak, Uttarakhand; on the World Network.
    • COMMON TRAP Nanda Devi BR is a World Heritage Site (UNESCO, 1988; extended to Valley of Flowers 2005) as well as a MAB reserve (2004): two different lists.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Agasthyamala BR contains Neyyar, Peppara and Shendurney WLS, and Kalakad Mundanthurai Tiger Reserve.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Latest in RAS 2010: Cold Desert (added 2009), as at question time.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the blanks: Transition zone allows ___; buffer allows ___; core allows ___.

      Settlements and crop cultivation; tourism and grazing; no human activity. p. 167

    2. Distinguish the terms coined by Dasmann and Rosen.

      Dasmann: "biological diversity" (1968); Rosen: "biodiversity" (1985). p. 182

    3. Name the three Indian reserves added to the MAB network in 2009.

      Nokrek, Pachmarhi, Similipal. p. 182

    MAB programme and world network
    • MAB: intergovernmental scientific programme launched by UNESCO in 1971; scientific basis for improving people–environment relations. Asked 2013 with no 1971 option, so (*).
    • Earliest Indian entries: Nilgiri, Sundarban, Gulf of Mannar, Nanda Devi. Then Similipal, Pachmarhi, Nokrek (among 22 new reserves from 17 countries); Achanakmar-Amarkantak (July 2012); Great Nicobar (2013).
    • UPPCS 2010 option set is (*): every option is on the network today.
    • Bundala BR: south-east coast of Sri Lanka, near Hambantota; designated 2005; wintering ground for migratory birds; central-area NP with four saline lagoons; native and migrating birds.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’06

    Project Tiger

    India's pioneering tiger programme: a viable population in natural habitats, protected from extinction.

    Launch, aims and numbers
    • CORE CONCEPT Project Tiger launched 1 April 1973; tiger conservation programme to save the national animal from extinction.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • International Tiger Day: 29 July; decided at Saint Petersburg Tiger Summit, 2010.
    • India: 3682 tigers (tiger census 2022, MoEFCC), highest in the world.
    • Census 2018 detailed report (July 2020): India has 70% of world tigers; nearly 30% of India's tigers live outside tiger reserves.
    • Madhya Pradesh has the highest tiger population.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION4 papers
    • Natural range: Asiatic lion only in India (Gir). One-horned rhino in India and Nepal. Double-humped (Bactrian) camel native to Central Asia; in India, Nubra Valley.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • International Biodiversity Day 22 May; theme 2022 "Building a shared future for all life"; 2024 "Be Part of the Plan". The statement calling it National biodiversity was keyed wrong.
    Source check Book puts the world tiger population at 3000–4000 (p.196) while India alone has 3682 and "70%" of world tigers (p.200). These cannot all stand together; use India's 3682 and treat the world range as the book's unreconciled figure.
    Source check (*) keys, launch and count: UPPCS 2018 options omit 1973; MPPSC 1994 (all four parks are listed, so no "not listed" option); BPSC 1995 (none of the options fits).
    M-STrIPES and drones
    • M-STrIPES: Monitoring system for Tigers – Intensive Protection and Ecological Status; software-based monitoring across tiger reserves; in the news in the context of their maintenance.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Two components: (a) field protocols for patrolling, law enforcement, recording wildlife crimes, ecological monitoring; (b) customised software for storage, retrieval, analysis, reporting.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Gives interpretable reports and maps for management and policy; managers assess intensity and spatial coverage of patrols in a GIS-based tool.
    • Cuts response time to poaching or habitat degradation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Bandipur Tiger Reserve (Karnataka) monitored by drones/unmanned aerial vehicles; better than traditional on-land monitoring.
    All Tiger Reserves by State

    Numbers are the book's serial order.

    StateTiger Reserves

    Miscellaneous

    Project Elephant, UNESCO World Heritage sites, wetland and river links, and stray facts.

    Project Elephant and elephant biology
    • Launched 1992 by MoEF as a centrally sponsored scheme: long-term survival of elephants; protect elephants, habitats, migration corridors; address man-animal conflict; welfare of captive elephants. Financial and technical support to major elephant-bearing States. UPPCS 2007 options omit 1992 (*).
    • Census 2017: synchronised population about 29964.
    • Karnataka highest (6,049), then Assam (5,719), Kerala (5,706), census 2017.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • Group leader: generally a female. Gestation 18–22 months, longest of all mammals. First birth at 16–17 years; can calve until 60 or more.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • Order Proboscidea. African: Loxodonta africana. Asian/Indian: Elephas maximus.
    • Asian elephant passage (IAS 1999, dated): India has the largest population; about 20,000 to 25,000 in natural habitat; evergreen forests, dry thorn forest, swamps, grasslands; prime habitat moist deciduous forests. North-West (Dehradun, Nainital; Bijnor), Western Ghats (Karnataka, Kerala, Tamil Nadu), Central (Southern Bihar, Orissa), East (North Bengal, Assam).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’99
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book: 29964 (2017). DNA-based SAIEE 2021–25 estimates 22,446 (range 18,255–26,645); Karnataka 6,013, Assam 4,159. Scientists say this is not comparable with past estimates, so do not read it as a decline. Mongabay

    Non-Conventional Energy

    Energy and Energy Resources

    Energy is either renewable (non-conventional) or non-renewable (conventional). Fossil fuels are stored ancient solar energy.

    • Two categories: Renewable = Non Conventional; Non Renewable = Conventional.
    • Renewable energy: from sources naturally replenishing over a relatively short period but flow-limited; virtually inexhaustible in duration, limited in energy available per unit time. Solar and wind electricity qualify; thermal-plant and coal electricity do not.
    • Renewables replenish faster than consumed. Fossil fuels (coal, oil, gas) take hundreds of millions of years to form; burning them emits GHGs such as CO₂.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Fossil fuels: from decomposition of buried plants or dead organisms over millions of years. Non-renewable; major energy source at present; once used, cannot be recovered. Examples: coal, natural gas, petroleum. COMMON TRAP Uranium is not a fossil fuel.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Ultimate source of energy stored in fossil fuels: Sun.
    • Energy crisis: danger of exhaustion of fossil fuels such as crude oil, natural gas, coal, petrol.
    • Uranium: raw material for nuclear power. Nuclear energy comes from the nucleus of an atom through Nuclear Fusion or Nuclear Fission.
    • UPPCS 2022 key: nuclear energy is not a biomass source; usually treated as non-renewable, most often using uranium.
    • Biomass feedstocks: dedicated energy crops, agricultural crop residues, forestry residues, algae, wood processing residues, municipal waste, wet waste (food waste, industrial wastes, sorted municipal solid waste [MSW], urban wood waste, purpose-grown grasses, woody energy crops).
    • UN General Assembly unanimously declared 2014–2024 the "Decade of Sustainable Energy for All"; the Sustainable Energy for All initiative supports it.
    • Sustainable energy is SDG 7 of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals: affordable, reliable, sustainable, modern energy for all by 2030.
    • UPRO/ARO 2021 key: solar is the second most important source after fossil fuels for Indian energy need; book puts solar's share in total non-fossil fuel at about 12.5 per cent (no year given).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the figure: name the two non-renewable sources besides coal, petroleum and natural gas.

      Atomic energy and shale gas. p. 206

    2. Correct this: "Uranium is a fossil fuel."

      Uranium is a raw material for nuclear power, not a fossil fuel. pp. 206–207

    3. Why is the Sun the ultimate source of the energy in coal?

      Fossil fuels formed from dead organisms whose stored energy came from solar radiation. p. 207

    Renewable and Non-Renewable Energy Resources

    India's renewable mix has flipped from wind-led (2014–15) to solar-led (2025); 2030 targets rest on non-fossil capacity.

    Source check Same five 2025 figures are dated 28.02.2025 in the chapter banner (p.206) and "April, 2025" in the text (p.208). Quote them as early 2025. Later official figures could not be opened in this pass.
    • Examples of renewable sources: solar, wind, tidal, hydroelectric.
    • Best electricity source for sustainable development, among coal, mineral oil, gas, atomic energy: hydroelectricity, as it uses water without depleting it (UPPCS 2012; comparison limited to those options).
    • Chhattisgarh's major power source: thermal.
    • 2030 goals per book: reduce carbon intensity, 50 percent cumulative electric power installed from renewables, net zero carbon emission by 2070, 500 GW of renewable energy capacity. → Ch 4
    Source check Wording drifts. p.208: carbon intensity "be less than 45%" and 500 GW "renewable"; p.109: 500 GW "non-fossil", 50% of "total energy requirement"; p.118 INDC frames intensity as a percentage reduction from 2005 level. Read 45% as a reduction, and check whether a question says energy or installed power capacity.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Which renewable led India in 2014–15, and with what share?

      Wind, 58.54%. p. 208

    2. Why did the book call hydro best for sustainable development?

      It generates electricity without depleting water, unlike finite coal, oil, gas and atomic fuel. p. 208

    3. Fill in: India's net-zero year is __; its renewable target is __ GW by 2030.

      2070; 500 GW. p. 208

    Alternative Energy/Solar Energy

    Sun is the largest alternative source. Exams focus on ISA facts, DCR and India's solar "firsts".

    Solar basics
    • Sun: most comprehensive, unlimited source; Solar Energy is a renewable resource from solar radiation and the largest source (storehouse) of alternative energy.
    • Most used in organic form: essential for Photosynthesis and survival of living organisms.
    • Technologies: solar heating, photovoltaics, solar thermal; broadly passive or active, by how energy is captured and distributed or converted.
    • Solar cell (photovoltaic cell): converts light directly into electricity by the photovoltaic effect; most eco-friendly of the given options.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • No atmospheric pollution during conversion of solar to electrical energy; pollution-free source.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Solar power has the greatest renewable potential in India; panels called the fuel of the future.
    • Conveniently used for street lighting, minor irrigation, hot water to residential buildings, railway signal, solar cooker.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’96
    • IAS 1996 position: electrifying clusters of villages and small towns was not cost-effective; a few pilot projects only (dated).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’96
    • Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE): 50% subsidy on installation cost of a solar plant in North-Eastern States.
    International Solar Alliance (ISA)
    • Launched at UN Climate Change Conference, Paris, 30 November 2015, by PM Narendra Modi and then President of France Francois Hollande (then UN Secretary-General Ban Ki-Moon present).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Sun-rich countries lying fully or partially between Tropics of Cancer and Capricorn. As at the IAS 2016 question it did not include all UN members.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Headquarters/Secretariat: Gurugram, Haryana.
    • Target: raise USD 1 trillion for solar energy by 2030.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • First ISA Summit: New Delhi, 11 March 2018. 2nd: New Delhi, 31 October 2019.
    • Sixth Assembly: New Delhi, 30 October–2 November 2023; Seventh: November 2024, Delhi.
    • Pact with Asian Development Bank (March 2018) to promote solar deployment in Asia and Pacific.
    Source check Membership differs inside the book: 122 (104 Members + 18 Signatories) on p.209; "about 116+" on p.210. p.209 says countries "located between" the Tropics; p.210 says "fully or partially". Later ISA pages could not be opened in this pass, so eligibility and current count need a fresh check.
    Missions, DCR and tariffs
    • National Solar Mission: part of NAPCC → Ch 4; aims to make India a global leader in solar; renamed Jawaharlal Nehru National Solar Mission (JNNSM), January 2010.
    • Domestic Content Requirement (DCR): relates to developing solar production. Both solar photovoltaic (SPV) cells and modules must be made in India, to MNRE specifications and testing; for crystalline Silicon, all process steps at PV manufacturers' works in India.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Target (2018 question): 175 GW renewable by 2022 = 100 GW solar + 60 GW wind + 10 GW bio-power + 5 GW small hydro. Target date has passed; book gives no outcome.
    • IAS 2018 (both rejected; position as at 2018): India was not third in Silicon Wafers; panels were only assembled here with material from China, Europe etc. Solar tariffs are set by Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC), not Solar Energy Corporation of India.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Highest installed solar capacity as on March 2023 (MPPSC 2025 key): Rajasthan.
    Source check For Q0898 the book's support is generation in January 2025 (MU: Rajasthan 3854.00, Gujarat 1822.79, Karnataka 1455.99, Tamil Nadu 1223.51; Monthly Renewable Energy Generation Report), not installed capacity at March 2023. Key stands; the table does not prove it.
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key: all three correct): PM Surya Ghar Muft Bijli Yojana targets one crore solar rooftop panels in the residential sector; MNRE aims to train at grassroot levels on installation, operation, maintenance and repairs; aims at more than three lakhs skilled manpower through fresh skilling and up-skilling.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Solar firsts and largest
    • Cochin International Airport (Kochi), Kerala: first airport in the world powered entirely by solar; first Indian international airport developed under Public-Private Partnership (PPP) Model.
    • Surat, Gujarat: first district with 100% solar-powered Primary Health Centres (PHC): all 52, as on 26 March 2018.
    • Diu: first Union Territory to run 100 percent on solar; plants on more than 50 acres in 2018.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Diu Smart City: first Indian city on 100 percent renewable energy during daytime.
    • Bhadla Solar Park: world's largest (approx 14,000 acres), Phalodi tehsil, Jodhpur district, Rajasthan. So "Gujarat has India's largest" was rejected (UPPCS 2022).
    • India's largest floating solar: Ramagundam, Telangana, 100 megawatt (MW), by National Thermal Power Corporation (NTPC) through Bharat Heavy Electricals (BHEL) under Engineering, Procurement and Construction (EPC); not Goa (as at UPPCS 2022).
    Source check p.209 calls the Tamil Nadu plant at "Kameethi", Ramnathapuram, the "world's largest solar plant at a single location"; p.212 calls Bhadla the world's largest solar park. No dates given for the first. Treat "largest" claims as time-bound.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Where and when was ISA launched, and where is its secretariat?

      Paris, 30 November 2015; Gurugram. p. 209

    2. Explain what DCR demands of solar projects.

      Both cells and modules must be made in India. p. 211

    3. Correct this: "The Solar Energy Corporation of India determines solar tariffs."

      Book credits the Central Electricity Regulatory Commission. p. 212

    4. Match: Surat, Diu, Bhadla, Ramagundam.

      Solar PHC district; solar UT; largest park; floating solar. pp. 211–212

    Biodiesel

    Biomass is indirect solar power. Know which crop gives which fuel, and what biomass gasification actually yields.

    • Biomass energy: from breaking chemical bonds formed through photosynthesis, so an indirect source of solar power.
    • Biomass: renewable organic material from plants and animals; contains stored chemical energy from the sun; burned for heat or converted to liquid and gaseous fuels.
    • Sources: wood and processing waste (firewood, pellets, chips, lumber and furniture mill sawdust, black liquor from pulp and paper mills); agricultural crops and waste (corn, soybeans, sugar cane, switchgrass, woody plants, algae, crop and food residues); biogenic municipal waste (paper, cotton, wool, food, yard, wood); animal manure and human sewage for biogas/renewable natural gas.
    • Jatropha: biodiesel crop; resists dry environment and harmful insects; seeds rich in oil (40%); oil used after extraction in diesel generators and engines; used for decades by remote rural and forest communities.
    • Mahua and Neem etc. can also be used as bio-fuels.
    • Pongamia pinnata: high productivity, less maturity cycle; grows naturally in most arid regions of India; tolerates waterlogged, saline, alkaline soil and harsh agro-climates; seeds rich in lipid, nearly half oleic acid.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’10
    • COMMON TRAP Bio-fuels are not cost-effective against fossil fuels; quite expensive in the market.
    • Biomass gasification: incomplete combustion giving combustible gases, carbon monoxide, hydrogen and traces of methane (not only H₂ and CO₂). Coconut shells, groundnut shells and rice husk can be used.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Resulting producer gas is a fuel usable in internal combustion engines too. Book also calls it Synthetic Gas; biological or fossil-based carbon substances can be transformed to CO, Hydrogen, CO₂ and Methane; an important renewable source.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • First Indian airline to use biofuel as ATF: SpiceJet, 27 August 2018, Dehradun-Delhi route.
    • Compost: earthworms speed up decomposition (worm composting yields vermicompost, a soil amendment).
    Source check p.213 and p.214 print Pongamia seeds rich in "liquid" content; the IAS 2010 stem says "lipid". Lipid used here.
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key (d)): the claim that Brazil out-produces the United States in ethanol was rejected. Corn is the principal US feedstock; sugarcane is Brazil's.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • Mains 2025 asked how India can reach energy independence through clean technology by 2047 and how biotechnology can help. Biofuels and biomass above are the book's relevant material; the paper has no official answer.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Correct this: "Gases from biomass gasification are only hydrogen and carbon dioxide."

      They are carbon monoxide, hydrogen and traces of methane. p. 213

    2. Why is biomass called indirect solar power?

      Its chemical bonds are formed by photosynthesis. p. 213

    3. Which energy crop in the book is for ethanol rather than biodiesel: Maize, Jatropha or Pongamia?

      Maize. p. 213

    Green Fuel

    Ethanol, hydrogen, fuel cells, algae fuel and bio-asphalt, plus organic farming facts placed in this section's questions.

    Ethanol and green fuel sources
    • Green Fuels reduce Environmental Pollution. Ethanol = ethyl alcohol; from sugary substances (sugarcane's sugar, glucose, Mahua's flower) or starch.
    • Bio-ethanol can be obtained from sugarcane crops and used as fuel energy.
    • Book treats Pine, Karanja (Pongamia pinnata) and Fern as green-fuel sources via fermentation to ethanol; question marked (*).
    Source check Q0910 (UPLDA 2010, p.215) is marked (*): book treats Pine, Karanja and Fern all as green-fuel sources (fermented to ethanol), so no single option. Karanja (Pongamia) is listed for biodiesel on p.213. Separately, p.214 lists "saltpetre" among sugary substances; saltpetre is not a sugar, so this looks like a misprint.
    Hydrogen
    • Least polluting fuel: Hydrogen. Burns with oxygen to form water, releasing huge energy (combustion); consumed in a fuel cell, produces only water. Diesel, Coal, Kerosene produce CO₂.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Highest calorific value; pollution-less; called the future fuel.
    • Energy carrier: stores, moves, delivers energy from other sources. Producible from natural gas, nuclear power, biomass, solar and wind. Used in cars, houses, portable power.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Commonest methods: natural gas reforming (thermal process) and electrolysis; also solar-driven and biological. Book also lists temperature differentiation, photolysis and electrical reduction of water.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Available as product from chloro-Alkali, ammonia, refineries.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • Green hydrogen (GH2): from electrolysis of water using renewable electricity (electrolyzers with solar, wind, biomass).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23RECENT PYQ — 2026
    • IAS 2023 key: all three correct: direct internal-combustion fuel; blend with natural gas for heat or power; fuel cell vehicles. Two engine types: fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs) make electricity for a motor; hydrogen internal combustion engines (H2ICE) burn it.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • National Hydrogen Energy Board set up 2003. Target: 1000 MW hydrogen-based capacity up to 2020 (dated).
    Added concept Not in the book.
    • UPSC 2026 also asked whether natural-gas reforming with CCS counts as green hydrogen, and about a National Green Hydrogen Mission goal of abating nearly 50 MMT of annual GHG by 2030. Coaching consensus (b), not an official key: reforming statement rejected, electrolysis and the 50 MMT statement accepted.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Fuel cells
    • Fuel cell: electrochemical cell converting chemical energy of fuel into electricity via hydrogen–oxygen reaction. With pure hydrogen, by-products = heat and water. Output is not Alternating Current.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Generate Direct Current (DC), not AC. Stationary cells serve commercial, industrial, residential and backup power. "Buildings but not small devices" was rejected (IAS 2015).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Microbial Fuel Cell (MFC) (book also: Microbiological Fuel Cells): bio-electrochemical device; microorganisms such as bacteria act as catalyst, turning organic substrate directly into electricity.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Substrates: carbonic substances such as glucose, sucrose, cellulose, starch, formate, acetate, ethanol, methanol, amino acid, protein. Best near-term use: wastewater treatment plants, cutting purification cost while producing electricity.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    Source check IAS 2011 key rejects "a variety of inorganic materials as substrates" (p.216), yet p.214 says some inorganic substances such as sulphides are used. Keep the key; note the exception.
    Algae biofuel and bio-asphalt
    • Algae-based biofuel possible in both seas and continents ("seas only" rejected). Limits for developing countries: high expertise and technology; heavy capital; large-scale facilities may raise ecological and social concerns.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Bio-asphalt: substitute for petroleum-based asphalt (which contains toxic and heavy metals); renewable, made without petroleum.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Sources (p.214): sugar, molasses, paddy, maize, lignin, cellulose, waste of palm oil, waste of white mustard oil.
    • Also potato starches, corn, tree resins, natural rubber, vegetable oils. Used in roofing and street paving; two streets of Norway paved in 2007. Helps lower road surface temperature, so eco-friendly for road surfacing. Union Budget 2011-12 extended full basic customs duty exemption.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    Source check IAS 2011 key (b) accepts statement 3, "can be made from non-organic waste materials", but every feedstock the book lists is organic or plant-based. Treat statement 3 as exam-key only.
    Organic farming
    • Sikkim: first fully organic state in India and the world, 2016; process started 2003; chemical fertilisers and pesticides phased out, composting pits dug, import and use of chemicals forbidden.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Won Future Policy Gold Award, 2018, from UN Food and Agriculture (FAO), beating 40+ nominations.
    • National Programme for Organic Production (NPOP): launched 2001 by Ministry of Commerce and Industry, not Rural Development; Agricultural and Processed Food Products Export Development Authority (APEDA) is its secretariat. Covers accreditation of Certification Bodies, standards, promotion and marketing.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. What are the by-products of a pure-hydrogen fuel cell, and what current does it produce?

      Heat and water; direct current. pp. 215–216

    2. Distinguish FCEV from H2ICE.

      FCEV makes electricity in a fuel cell to drive a motor; H2ICE burns hydrogen in an engine. p. 215

    3. Correct this: "Algae-based biofuels can only be produced in seas."

      They are possible in both seas and continents. p. 216

    4. Which ministry's programme is NPOP, and who is its secretariat?

      Commerce and Industry; APEDA. p. 217

    Miscellaneous

    Geothermal provinces, biogas, coal and acid rain, and nuclear project concerns.

    • Biogas: primary forms methane and carbon dioxide. Matching (UPPCS 2015): Minerals – Exhaustible; Solar energy – Inexhaustible; Biogas – Methane and CO₂; Deforestation – Rainfall.
    • Geothermal energy: Earth's natural heat, from fiery consolidation of dust and gas over 4 billion years ago, continually regenerated by decay of radioactive elements in all rocks.
    • Seven geothermal provinces in India: Himalayas, Sohana, West Coast, Cambay, Son-Narmada-Tapi (SONATA), Godavari, Mahanadi. Not found in Ganga Delta (UPPCS 2016 key).
    • Sulphur in coal → sulphur dioxide → primary cause of acid rain. Oxides of carbon are emitted too, but do not explain it (IAS 2003: A and R true, R not the explanation).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’03
    • Conventional: Coal, Petroleum, Natural gas, Atomic/Nuclear energy. Non-conventional: Solar, Wind, Hydroelectric etc. (question marked (*)).
    • Nuclear project effects to be studied: radioactive contamination of air, soil and water; deforestation and loss of flora and fauna; radioactive waste disposal.
    Source check p.217's list (Himalaya, Khambhat Basin, Sonata, Western Ghat, Godavari Basin, Mahanadi Basin) omits Sohana and has "Western Ghat" where p.218 has West Coast. Khambhat = Cambay. Use p.218's seven.
    Source check Q0922 (Jharkhand 2016, p.218) is marked (*): book says Coal, Petroleum, Natural gas and Atomic/Nuclear energy are all conventional, while the options include both coal and nuclear. Solar, Wind, Hydroelectric are non-conventional. No single option.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name the seven geothermal provinces.

      Himalayas, Sohana, West Coast, Cambay, SONATA, Godavari, Mahanadi. p. 218

    2. Which element in coal causes acid rain, and through what gas?

      Sulphur, through sulphur dioxide. p. 218

    3. Correct this: "Oxides of carbon explain why coal plants cause acid rain."

      Carbon oxides are emitted, but sulphur dioxide is the cause. p. 218
