    Pollution

    Pollution and its types

    Pollution = unexpected change in physical, chemical and biological characteristics of soil, water and air, due to disproportionate accumulation of pollutants.

    • Pollution caused by humans = anthropogenic pollution; mainly raises non-biodegradable pollutants, which are not decomposed by organic decomposers or decompose very slowly, so very difficult to control.
    • Environment modified by human activities = anthropogenic environment; "anthropogenic" = change caused or influenced by people, directly or indirectly.
    • Biodegradable pollutants: broken into simpler, harmless substances in due course by micro-organisms (certain bacteria); e.g. domestic waste (garbage), urine, faecal matter, sewage, cattle dung, animal bones, agriculture residues, vegetable stuff.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Biodegradable waste mainly from plant or animal sources; decomposed by other living species. Non-biodegradable e.g. pesticides, plastics, metals.
    • Biodegradable = capable of decomposition by bacteria or other organisms. Among plastic, polythene, mercury and rubber, book's key picks rubber.
    • Asbestos, DDT and plastic are non-biodegradable.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Name the four bases on which the book classifies pollution.

      Physical properties, part of environment, origin, ecosystem. p. 219

    2. Why is anthropogenic pollution hard to control?

      It mainly raises non-biodegradable pollutants, which decomposers break down very slowly or not at all. p. 219

    3. Correct this: "DDT is a biodegradable pollutant because it is an organic chemical."

      DDT is non-biodegradable in the book's classification. p. 219, 250

    Air Pollution

    Air pollution: atmospheric gases present in definite proportion change into undesirable proportion (poisonous gases or particulate matter), by man-made or natural causes.

    Sources
    • By part of environment, three main types: air, water, land pollution.
    • Natural sources: forest fires, volcanic eruptions; SO₂ and NOx generated by degradation of biological wastes.
    • Man-made sources: gases, smoke, particulate matter, heat from combustion of fuel.
    • Combustion of wood, coal, petrol, diesel gives gases and smoke; mainly oxides of carbon and nitrogen = main factor behind air pollution.
    • Burning of fossil fuels = major cause; PM2.5 from it causes millions of premature deaths in India. Higher-dependence regions: China, India, Eastern United States, Europe, South East Asia.
    • SO₂: air pollutant released by burning fossil fuel, not a water pollutant; high in the atmosphere it reacts with water, oxygen, other chemicals → acid rain.
    • Rain and dew clean the atmosphere; washing works much better on larger particles (above 10 or above 2.5 microns); rain also makes dust stick to the ground. Particles under 2.5 μ are not cleaned by rain.
    • Delhi winter peak: cold air, heavier than warm air, does not rise, so dust and pollutants form a layer near the surface. Reason "vehicle combustion increases in winter" was false; book says it decreases.

    Typical sources of air pollutants in ambient air:

    PollutantTypical major sources
    SO₂Fuel combustion, power station, industrial and chemical processes, diesel vehicles, solid waste disposal, smelters
    NOxTransport (road, rail, passenger, commercial), fuel combustion, power station, industrial boilers, chemical processes, waste incinerators, smelters
    Particulate matter (SPM, RSPM-PM10, RSPM-PM2.5)Fuel combustion, power station, construction, industrial processes, diesel exhaust, resuspended road dust, domestic refuse burning, domestic wood
    COTransport, combustion, industrial processes, solid waste disposal, refuse burning
    OzoneSecondary pollutant formed during photochemical reaction
    Organic compoundsTransport, oil-based fuel combustion, chemical processes, solvent use, waste incinerator, vaporization of fuel
    BenzenePetrol combustion products, petrol filling stations, chemical process
    Poly-nuclear aromatic hydrocarbons (PAH)Fuel combustion, industrial emission
    Volatile organic compounds (VOC)Transport, solvents (especially industrial and domestic)
    Trace metalsFuel combustion, chemical process, transport, metal production and finishing, product manufacture
    LeadLead additives in gasoline, soil-originated particles
    CadmiumFuel combustion, metal production process, transport
    Source check "Main air pollutant(s) in metropolitan cities" (MPPSC 2018): book's key is (*), with only this source table as explanation (p. 229). No option is confirmed.
    Photochemical smog and PAN
    • CORE CONCEPT Vehicle and industrial smoke has NO and NO₂, which react with hydrocarbons in sunlight → secondary pollutants: ozone, formaldehyde, PAN (peroxyacetyl nitrate). "Photochemical" = sunlight + chemical pollutants.
    • Photochemical smog: secondary air pollutant; a brown-orange film; generally in traffic-congested metros with warm conditions and intense solar radiation.
    • Occurs more in summer (most sunlight). Common components: ozone, nitric oxide, acrolein, formaldehyde, PAN. Forms when NOx and volatile organic compounds react to sunlight → brown haze above cities.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’03
    • IAS 2013: results from reaction among NO₂, O₃ and PAN in sunlight.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Automobile exhaust contains HC and NO; key in forming O₃ and PAN in urban areas.
    • Smog = smoke + fog (visible air pollution): nitrogen oxides, sulphur oxides, ozone, smoke, other particulates. Man-made smog from coal combustion, vehicular and industrial emissions, forest and agricultural fires, and their photochemical reactions. UPPCS 2019 key: oxides of nitrogen and sulphur.
    • Health: irritation of eye and nose, throat and respiratory problems. Visibility falls with heavy smog.
    • NO₂: reddish-brown gas, sharp harsh odour at higher concentrations. Absorbs light → NO + nascent oxygen (O), which is highly reactive and joins O₂ → O₃.
    • Plant damage: O₃ enters via open stomata into mesophyll cells; reacts with cell water → oxygen free radicals and peroxy radicals (O₂⁻, OH⁻, O⁻, HO₂⁻).
    • Peroxy radicals react with oxygen atoms → ozone, or with NO₂ → PAN.
    • PAN destroys chloroplast (loss of photochemical capacity and plant development); hinders Electron Transport Chain (ETC) in mitochondria; affects enzyme system; PAN with air and oxygen → smog.
    • PAN = powerful eye irritant (lachrymatory: irritates lungs and eyes). Secondary pollutant, no direct emissions; thermally unstable, decomposes to peroxyethanoyl radicals + NO₂.
    • More stable than ozone, so long-range transport: carries NOx into rural regions, causes ozone formation in global troposphere. Formed by photochemical oxidation of hydrocarbons to peroxyacetic acid radicals with NO₂. Next to ozone and hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂), important smog component.
    Source check Book says photochemical smog "is generated by the combination of smoke and fog" (p. 220). Its own mechanism on the same page is NOx + hydrocarbons + sunlight; smoke + fog is the etymology of "smog" in general (p. 230).
    Source check Q "produced during formation of photochemical smog" (IAS 2003): book's key is (b) nitrogen oxides (p. 223), yet its reactions show NOx as precursor and O₃ as the product formed (p. 220). Official key not verified; do not memorise "NOx is produced" as the rule.
    Primary and secondary pollutants
    PrimarySecondary
    OriginInjected directly from sourceFormed by reaction of primary pollutants with common atmospheric substances
    ExamplesCO, CO₂, SO₂, oxides of sulphur (mainly SO₂), oxides of nitrogenOzone, PAN, smog
    Specific air pollutants
    • Carbon monoxide: colourless, odourless; from incomplete combustion (motor vehicles, cigarette). Main primary pollutant; main pollutant gas in vehicle exhaust; among options given, most dangerous.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • CO joins haemoglobin → carboxyhaemoglobin (COHb), cutting oxygen-carrying capacity. Hb binds CO 200 to 300 times more than O₂. About 50% conversion → respiration obstructed → death.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Molecular weight of CO similar to air; mixes freely in any proportion; not detectable by taste or smell.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Lead: anti-knocking agent; burning petrol releases lead. Hinders brain development in children, reduces IQ; heart and respiratory diseases, high blood pressure and kidney damage in adults.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • CNG (Compressed Natural Gas) major component: methane (CH₄); replacing petrol and diesel as harmful products are in very small amounts.
    • Fly ash: by-product of coal burnt at thermal power plants. Low-grade coal carries 30–45% ash, high-grade imported coal 10–15%; most Indian thermal coal is low-grade, so ash needs large landfill or ponds.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Fly ash used for construction bricks; most common use: replacement for Portland cement in concrete. Components: SiO₂, Al₂O₃, occasionally CaO, plus toxic lead, arsenic, copper; so "only SiO₂ and CaO, no toxic elements" is false.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Cigarette smoke: CO, hydrogen cyanide, nitrogen oxide, arsenic; unstable compounds formaldehyde, acrolein, benzene; nicotine in very small amounts. Causes irritation of eye, nose, throat; cancer, bronchitis, asthma. Key in UPPCS 2015: CO and benzene.
    • Benzene: colourless or light yellow liquid at room temperature; natural and human origin; evaporates quickly. Exposure: automobile exhaust, tobacco smoke, wood burning, varnished wooden furniture, polyurethane products (all five).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • Mercury: global pollutant; WHO top-ten chemical of major public health concern. Sources: gold mining (major), coal burning (released as gas). No known safe level of exposure.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    • Main human exposure: methylmercury (organic) via fish and shellfish. Forms: elemental (metallic) and inorganic (occupational), organic (diet); they differ in toxicity and effects on nervous, digestive, immune systems, lungs, kidneys, skin, eyes.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’23
    Source check Book presents petrol as releasing lead in the present tense (p. 221, 247), yet the IAS 2012 stem it reproduces says addition of lead to petrol has been banned (p. 248). Read lead-in-petrol as historical.
    Asian Brown Cloud
    • Mainly spread over South Asia, due to air pollution, particularly January–March (2002 cloud in question).
    • Approx. 85% aerosol; also sulphate, black carbon, other deadly chemicals → respiratory problems, cancer.
    • Effects: significant change in solar radiation in India and China, uneven atmospheric temperature, changed monsoonal rainfall pattern.
    Control devices and measurement
    • Bag filter: particulates under 50 micrometre. Cyclone separator / collector, Wet Scrubber: particles over 50 micrometre. Cyclone divider is not an air pollution device.
    • Catalytic converter: emission control via redox reaction; catalyst usually a precious metal mix (platinum, palladium, rhodium). CO → CO₂; NOx → N₂; hydrocarbons → CO₂ + H₂O.
    • WAYU (Wind Augmentation Purifying Unit): air pollution control device, Sept 2018, traffic junctions at ITO intersection and Mukarba Chowk, Delhi; designed by NEERI and IIT (Bombay). Filter converts PM2.5, PM10, CO, volatile organic compounds to CO₂. Claimed 40–60% cut at junctions; purifies 500 sq m. Book notes BPSC's "VAYU" is wrong.
    • Ringelmann scale: grades opacity, appearance, density or shade of smoke, i.e. its light-obscuring power.
    Added concept Not in the book; from a recent paper, key as reported.
    • UPSC 2025: artificial rainfall to reduce air pollution uses silver iodide and potassium iodide (reported official key (a)).RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Source check Book prints "2CO + O₂ → 2CO₂ + O₂" and "HC₅" (p. 223). The equation does not balance as printed; read 2CO + O₂ → 2CO₂ over palladium, and hydrocarbons → CO₂ + H₂O.
    Bio-indicators of air pollution
    • Bio-indicators respond in an especially clear way to environmental change. Lichens: useful indicators of air pollution, especially SO₂, since they take water and nutrients mainly from the atmosphere, not soil.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • Lichens do not grow in polluted areas, hence absent near cities. Symbiosis of algae (phycobiont, autotrophic) and fungi (mycobiont, heterotrophic): algae prepare food; fungi give shelter and absorb minerals and water.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    Source check Book places a nitrification note here: Nitrobacter "converts ammonia in the soil to nitrate", plants return food and shelter (p. 222). The ammonia-to-nitrate step is credited to one genus and the link to air pollution is unclear; not verified here, so do not rely on it without checking a biology text.
    Most polluted places
    • WHO report, 2 May 2018: eight of ten most polluted cities in India; four in Uttar Pradesh (Kanpur, Varanasi, Lucknow, Agra). Kanpur = world's most polluted city in that list.
    • IQAir AirVisual 'World Air Quality Report, 2019' (Feb 2020): Ghaziabad most polluted city in the world.
    • World Air Quality Report, 2024: India 5th most polluted country; Delhi 2nd most polluted city (1st Byrnihat, Meghalaya).
    • Most polluted Indian city, dated: 2009 Ankleshwar; 2016 data Wapi (Gujarat), Comprehensive Environmental Pollution Index 85.31, then Ghaziabad 84.13; World Air Quality Report 2022 Bhiwadi.
    • Greenpeace India 'Airpocalypse' (January 2020): 231 of 287 Indian cities severely polluted; Jharia (Jharkhand), coal-belching, most polluted.
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's position is the 2024 report. IQAir's 2025 report (24 Mar 2026): top five countries Pakistan 67.3, Bangladesh 66.1, Tajikistan 57.3, Chad 53.6, D.R. Congo 50.2 μg/m³; most polluted city Loni, India, 112.5 μg/m³; India has three of the four most polluted cities. source
    Air Quality Index
    • India's AQI: 8 pollutants: PM10 (<10 μm), PM2.5 (<2.5 μm), NO₂, CO, O₃, SO₂, NH₃, Pb.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • COMMON TRAP CO₂ and CH₄ are not in AQI; they are used for measuring global warming.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Averaging: CO and O₃ over 8 hours; others 24 hours. Units: NO₂, O₃ in μg/m³; CO in mg/m³.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • National Air Quality Index (NAQI): released 17 October 2014 by Environment Ministry, under Swachh Bharat Abhiyaan; "One Number, One Colour, One Description".PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Six categories, set by ambient concentrations and likely health impacts. Sub-index and health breakpoints for the eight pollutants, for which short-term (up to 24-hour) National Ambient Air Quality Standards exist.
    IndexCategoryColourPossible health impact
    0–50GoodGreenMinimum impact
    51–100SatisfactoryLight greenMinor breathing discomfort to sensitive people
    101–200Moderately pollutedYellowBreathing discomfort to people with asthma and heart diseases
    201–300PoorOrangeBreathing discomfort to most people on prolonged exposure
    301–400Very PoorRedRespiratory illness on prolonged exposure
    401–500SevereDark red / maroonAffects healthy people; seriously impacts those with existing diseases
    • WHO guidelines (updated): PM2.5 annual mean ≤ 5 μg/m³, 24-hour mean ≤ 15 μg/m³.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • PM2.5 (not PM10, as the IAS 2022 statement said) penetrates the lung barrier and enters blood: cardiovascular and respiratory disease, cancers. Book calls it the most dangerous pollutant.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    • Ozone: common pollutant behind smog or haze; very irritating to lungs and airways, triggers asthma. "Highest ozone in inclement weather" was marked incorrect.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’22
    Law and programmes
    • Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1981, amended 1987: prevention, control and abatement of air pollution. 1987 amendment added 'noise'.
    • s.2(a) 'air pollutant' = any solid, liquid or gaseous substance that may harm environment, humans, plants, animals or property. Provides for standards on smoke and other emissions from motor vehicles and other sources (p. 247).
    • Book: Act defines 'air pollution' as presence of any dangerous pollutant that makes the air unbreathable; State Pollution Control Boards (SPCB) to carry out directives of Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB), whose powers extend to whole of India.
    • National Clean Air Programme: launched by MoEFCC January 2019; book's stated target 20–30% reduction in PM10 and PM2.5 by 2024, base year 2017.
    • Climate and Clean Air Coalition (CCAC): voluntary partnership of over 160 governments, intergovernmental and non-governmental organisations; founded February 2012; not a G20 initiative. Started by Bangladesh, Canada, Ghana, Mexico, Sweden, United States with UNEP.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • CCAC focus: methane, black carbon, hydrofluorocarbons. Short-lived climate pollutants treated as urgent, collective challenge; raises awareness, mitigation, new regional and national operations; complements CO₂ reduction.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    Source check Book says s.2(g) of the Air Act "set up" the CPCB (p. 229). Not confirmed here: the official pages could not be opened in this check. Verify against the bare Act before quoting a section number.
    Indoor (domestic) air pollution
    • Most significant indoor pollutant: Radon: colourless, odourless, radioactive noble gas; decay product of radium; released naturally from soil.
    • Accumulates in houses with reduced ventilation; probability of lung cancer and blood cancer.
    • WHO: lakhs die yearly from domestic air pollution; ambient + household air pollution together linked to 6.7 million premature deaths annually.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Reconstruct the sequence by which an engine's NO ends up as ozone.

      N₂ + O₂ → 2NO; 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂; NO₂ (UV) → NO + O; O + O₂ → O₃. p. 220

    2. Correct this: "PM10 crosses the lung barrier into blood, and AQI includes CO₂."

      PM2.5 crosses the lung barrier; AQI excludes CO₂ and CH₄. p. 222, 228

    3. Which devices handle particles below and above 50 micrometre?

      Below: bag filter. Above: cyclone separator or collector, wet scrubber. p. 221

    4. In one sentence, why are lichens good SO₂ indicators?

      They draw water and nutrients mainly from the atmosphere, so polluted air reaches them directly. p. 222

    5. State the AQI band and colour for an index of 250.

      Poor, orange (201–300). p. 222

    Acid Rain

    SO₂ and nitrogen oxides rise high, react with water, oxygen and other chemicals to form more acidic pollutants; these fall to the surface with rain.

    Cause and chemistry
    • CORE CONCEPT Main causes: sulphur dioxide and nitrogen oxides. CO, ozone, methane are not the causes marked in these questions.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’13 · 13 papers
    • Oxide of sulphur, dissolved in rainwater, turns it acidic.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Formed by reaction of cloud water and SO₂.
    • pH: acid rain below 5.6; pure water 7.0. pH scale: 7 neutral, lower more acidic, higher more alkaline; normal rain about pH 5.6.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Acids present: sulphuric (H₂SO₄) and nitric (HNO₃); includes any precipitation with acidic components, wet or dry.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • SO₂ absorbs moisture → H₂SO₄. Book's term for SO₂: "cracking gas" (continued flow mangles stone).
    • Small part of SO₂ and NOx from natural sources (volcanoes); most from burning fossil fuels. Major sources: fossil fuel for electricity (two-thirds of SO₂, one-fourth of NOx from electric generators), vehicles and heavy equipment, manufacturing, oil refineries, other industries. BPSC 2005 key: industries.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • SO₂ and NOx are transported by wind and air currents before falling.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    Acidic gasNatural sourcesMan-made sources
    N₂ and oxidesVolcanic eruption, biological decay, lightning strikesOil, coal, combustion of natural gas, forest fire
    Sulphur and oxidesVolcanic eruption, sea, fires, phytoplanktonCombustion of coal, petroleum products; refining of crude oil; chemical and fertilizer industry
    Carbon dioxideRespiration, decomposing vegetation, outgassing from oceansDeforestation, industrial processes, combustion of fossil fuel
    • UPPCS 2024 explanation: CO₂ → carbonic acid (H₂CO₃); SO₂ → sulfurous acid (H₂SO₃); NO₂ → nitric acid (HNO₃).
    Source check UPPCS 2024 key (a) accepts CO₂ as producing acid rain (p. 234), while RAS 2015 and others exclude CO₂ from the causes (p. 231–233). Book reports both keys; in statement questions expect SO₂ and NOx as the causes.
    Effects
    • In the book's flowchart, NO₂ emission from fossil fuel: light particles mix with air and vapour → acid rain; heavy particles fall to cause the local damage.
    • Damages lakes, streams, forests, plants, animals; contaminates rainwater and snow; adverse impact on rivers, lakes, ponds.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Bricks of buildings shed layers of red powder. SO₂ from Mathura Oil Refinery blamed for corrosion of Taj Mahal, Agra.
    • Taj Trapezium Zone (TTZ): defined area of 10,400 sq km around the Taj to protect it from pollution.
    • Soil: excess acidity exchanges hydrogen ions with nurturing cations (K⁺, Mg⁺⁺), which leach away; fertility falls.
    • Aquatic: effects most clearly seen in streams, lakes, marshes. Acidic water flowing through soil leaches aluminium from clay particles into streams and lakes; more acid, more aluminium.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Trees: at high elevations acidic fog and clouds strip nutrients from foliage, leaving brown or dead leaves and needles; less sunlight absorbed; weak, less able to withstand freezing.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Materials: dry deposition is when dust particles turn acidic; nitric and sulphuric acid damage statues, buildings; corrode metal, deteriorate paint and stone; dirty surfaces.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • COMMON TRAP Walking in acid rain or swimming in an acid lake is no more dangerous than in normal rain or lakes. Harm comes when SO₂, NOx, sulfate and nitrate particles are in air: fine particles are inhaled.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION13 papers
    • Lowers soil pH; hits micro-organisms whose growth depends on pH. Corrosion of metals, in particular iron, steel, copper, bronze, rises; these enter drinking water and soil and affect the nervous system.
    Regions and treaty
    • Norway among most affected: southern half; most lakes and rivers acidic; lakes biologically dead, so acid rain = "Lake Killer". Among China, Japan, Norway, USA, Norway receives most.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Canada and Norway suffer most among options given; also other Scandinavian countries. SO₂ and NOx from factories in UK and Germany reach Norway and Sweden.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Acid Rain Information Centre (ARIC): Manchester, England.
    • 1985 Helsinki Protocol: reduce sulphur emissions or transboundary fluxes by at least 30%.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Define acid rain by pH and name its two main acids.

      Rain below pH 5.6; sulphuric and nitric acid. p. 230, 232

    2. Fill the labels: acid deposition splits into moist accumulation (by ___) and dry accumulation (by ___).

      Rain, fog, ice; particles dragged by air flow and acidic gases. p. 230

    3. Correct this: "Bathing in an acidified lake is the main human health risk of acid rain."

      False. Risk comes from inhaling sulfate and nitrate particles formed from SO₂ and NOx. p. 233

    4. Why does acid rain reduce soil fertility?

      Hydrogen ions displace K⁺ and Mg⁺⁺, which then leach away. p. 230–231

    Water Pollution

    Excess nutrients, organic waste, geogenic contaminants and industrial effluents degrade water; DO and BOD are the standard gauges.

    Eutrophication and algal bloom
    • Eutrophication: nutrient enrichment (organic and inorganic) of a water body, frequently from land run-off; increasing nitrogen and phosphorus by unnatural means. Depletes dissolved oxygen.
    • Often induced by nitrate- or phosphate-containing detergents, fertilizers, sewage.
    • Algal bloom: excessive growth of planktonic (free-floating) algae, dense layers on the surface, on overabundance of nutrients; surface also covered by blue-green algae with other plants. Worsens water quality, causes fish mortality. Harmful blooms, dead zones, fish kills result from eutrophication.
    • Eutrophication = water pollution, so not an air-pollution term. Smog, acid rain, asbestosis (long exposure to asbestos in air) relate to air pollution.
    BOD and DO
    • BOD (Biochemical, also Biological, Oxygen Demand): amount of dissolved oxygen needed by aerobic organisms to break down organic material in a sample at a set temperature and period. Standard criterion for pollution assay in aquatic ecosystems; main measure of water pollution.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’17 · 3 papers
    • Commonly mg of O₂ consumed per litre during 5 days at 20°C. Surrogate for organic pollution; gauges effectiveness of wastewater treatment plants.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • River pollution is measured through dissolved oxygen amount.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Low BOD = good quality water; high BOD = polluted, e.g. contaminated by sewage. Less pollution, higher DO, and vice versa.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • More organic waste (sewage) → faster decomposition → more O₂ used → DO falls. BOD ∝ 1/DO; greater BOD, greater infection level of water.
    • DO below 8.0 mg/L = contaminated; below 4.0 mg/L = highly polluted.
    • Only few tolerant species (Annelida, eggs of some other insects) survive in highly polluted, low-DO water; identified as pollutant species.
    • BOD maximum in Ganga between Kanpur and Prayagraj (Allahabad): Kanpur discharges heavy pollutants.
    Source check Book's explanation of Q0996 says BOD is oxygen needed by an "anaerobic" organism (p. 236); its explanation of Q0999 (p. 236) says aerobic. Aerobic is the consistent usage, as oxygen is what is being demanded.
    Arsenic
    • Abiotic (non-biotic) pollutant of groundwater; natural geological presence in local bedrock; high in areas adjoining banks of Ganga. Pesticides, weedicides and insecticides add to it. Also in vapours of volcanoes, oceanic and mineral water.
    • WHO limit 0.01 mg/litre (= 10 microgram per litre).
    • Carcinogen: skin, lung, bladder, kidney, liver cancer; also dermatological, developmental, neurological, respiratory, cardiovascular, immunological, endocrine effects. Continuous contact → Black Foot disease.
    • High absorption by paddy; reaches grains through groundwater; whole food chain affected.
    • Groundwater worst in Ganga-Brahmaputra plains (India), Padma-Meghna plains (Bangladesh). Seven states: West Bengal, Jharkhand, Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Assam, Manipur, Chhattisgarh (Rajnandgaon). First Indian case 1978, West Bengal.
    • Bangladesh: natural arsenic contamination first found in tubewell water, 1993, a northern district; fifty million people estimated at risk.
    Rivers and industry
    • Damodar = 'Biological Desert' over 300 km between Giridih and Durgapur; flows through coal mines and industrial areas; book calls it most polluted river of the country. Once 'Sorrow of Bengal' for floods; pollutants from mines, industries, cities, fields.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Gomti: UP's most polluted river; key for the UP river declared a 'Biological Disaster'. Unfit even for bathing, known since 2010. CPCB: Lucknow-Jaunpur stretch among most polluted. Causes: sewage, industrial effluents; ecological flow drastically reduced.
    • Highest chemical pollution from industrial effluents: leather industry; mainly responsible for water and soil pollution.
    Drinking water and fluoride
    • Groundwater pollutants in parts of India: arsenic, fluoride, uranium; sorbitol and formaldehyde were excluded. Largest source of water pollution: untreated sewage.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Substantial uranium in Punjab groundwater samples; over-exploitation for irrigation aggravates uranium mobilisation, likely one reason for geogenic contaminants.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • About 85% of rural population depends on groundwater; remaining 15% on open wells or open sources (p. 235). The Q1009 explanation says "nearly 85% of the population".PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Drinking water quality highly worrisome: Manipur, Tripura, Odisha, Meghalaya, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh.
    • Fluoride: small amount protects gums and teeth; excess → Fluorosis, 'Humped back', weak joints especially spinal cord.
    • Knock-Knee Syndrome = fluoride pollution (book: only cause). Book: above 0.5 ppm over 5–10 years → fluorosis; affinity for calcium, accumulates in bones → pain, outward bending of legs from knees.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • National Programme for Prevention and Control of Fluorosis (NPPCF): initiated 2008–09, 11th Five Year Plan.
    Bio-toilets and solid waste
    • Railway bio-toilets (Railways + Defence Research Development Organization, DRDO): colony of anaerobic bacteria, not fungal inoculum, turns waste to water and a few gases, not only ammonia and water vapour. Gases to atmosphere; water discharged on tracks after chlorination.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 (as at IAS 2019): provide exact and elaborate criteria for identifying sites for landfills and waste processing facilities.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Source check "Which mega city generates the largest solid waste per capita?" (UPPCS 2010): key is (*) (p. 238). Book gives only totals: latest CPCB data Mumbai the largest producer; during 2009–10 Delhi. Per-capita answer not given.
    • Largest solid waste producer: Mumbai per latest CPCB data in the book; Delhi during 2009–10.
    Added concept Recent Mains question, described as asked (no model answer implied).
    • Mains 2026: challenges to solid waste management in India, the governmental policy framework, and the success or failure of Delhi and Indore.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Reconstruct the eutrophication sequence from nutrient input to biodiversity loss.

      Nutrients → algal bloom → spread on surface → O₂ falls → organisms die, biodiversity lost. p. 234

    2. Explain the BOD-DO relationship in one sentence.

      BOD is proportional to 1/DO: more organic waste raises oxygen demand and lowers dissolved oxygen. p. 234

    3. State the DO levels for contaminated and highly polluted water.

      Below 8.0 and below 4.0 mg/L. p. 235

    4. Correct this: "Railway bio-toilets use a fungal inoculum and release only ammonia and water vapour."

      False on both counts: anaerobic bacteria; water and a few gases. p. 237

    5. Match: Damodar, Gomti, leather industry with their labels.

      Biological desert; 'Biological Disaster' (per key); highest chemical pollution from effluents. p. 235, 237–238

    Sea Pollution

    The sea stores about one-third of man-made CO₂, and that absorption is what acidifies it.

    • Ocean acidification: ongoing fall in ocean pH from intake of atmospheric CO₂.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’12 · 2 papers
    • Harms growth and survival of calcareous phytoplankton, of coral reefs; survival of some animals that have phytoplankton larvae. Effect on cloud seeding and cloud formation was the statement marked out (IAS 2012).REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’12 · 2 papers
    • Excess nitrogen → fast growth of algae.
    • Harmful algal blooms, lethal to humans and marine ecosystems, increasing in Indian waters. Causes: upwelling, mud banks, nutrient discharges from estuaries, land run-off during south-west and north-east monsoons.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • International Maritime Organization: convention adopted at 1948 Geneva conference as Inter-Governmental Maritime Consultative Organization (IMCO); renamed IMO in 1982. UN specialized agency; HQ London.
    • Role: safety and security of shipping, prevention of marine and atmospheric pollution by ships; global standard-setter, regulatory framework for the shipping industry.
    • SACEP = South Asia Cooperative Environment Programme; Union Cabinet approved India–SACEP MoU on oil and chemical pollution in South Asian Seas region, 28 March 2018.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the chain: Increase in CO₂ → ___ → lack in carbonate ion → ___ → obstruction in skeleton formation.

      Increased ocean acidity; blockage in calcification. p. 239

    2. Give the original name of IMO and the year of renaming.

      IMCO; 1982. p. 239

    3. Which natural process, besides nutrient run-off, can cause harmful algal blooms in Indian seas?

      Upwelling (also mud bank formation). p. 239

    Sound Pollution

    Noise is sound of raised intensity; unwanted sound causes unrest and discomfort in human beings.

    • Unit: decibel (dB), named in honour of Alexander Graham Bell. Logarithmic ratio of two power levels: dB = 10 log₁₀(P2/P1); reference is a sound just perceptible to the human ear. Also used in electronics, signals, communication.
    • Sources: machines and transportation (motor vehicles, aircraft, train), unplanned industrial development. Jet take-off and landing: excessive noise; among heavy traffic, election meetings, rock music, jet flight, the most intense is jet flight.
    • Other values: breathing 10, rustling leaves 20, quiet rural farmland 30, birds calling 40, conversation at home 50, restaurant conversation 60 dB. Up to 70–80 dB generally comfortable; some find above 75 uncomfortable.
    • Noise from jet aircraft moving faster than sound = sonic boom; Mach-1 = speed of sound, Mach-2 = double.
    • Green muffler → noise pollution. Row of trees and shrubs absorbing noise; large green plants in high-noise areas; Ashoka and Neem near residential localities. Also cuts air pollution by absorbing gases and settling dust from vehicles.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Source check Book calls the noise of objects faster than sound "Mach-1" (p. 240), then treats Mach as a speed multiple: Mach is a speed ratio, dB the noise unit. Also, its green-muffler explanation adds a technique for an engine's exhaust noise (p. 241), which conflicts with the tree-row definition in the same box.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the scale: normal conversation ___ dB, truck ___ dB, jet engine ___ dB.

      60; 80–85; 120. p. 240

    2. Which two tree species does the book name under the Green Muffler scheme?

      Ashoka and Neem. p. 241

    3. Distinguish decibel and Mach number.

      Decibel measures sound level; Mach is speed relative to the speed of sound. p. 240

    Soil Pollution

    Excess fertilizer pollutes soil, water and air, and reaches people through crops.

    • Indiscriminate fertilizer → soil, water and air pollution: nitrogen loss and land degradation; surface and groundwater contaminated; nitrogen fertilizer made using much oil and natural gas, so air pollution.
    • Enters food chain of man and animals through crops; residue in crops acts as slow poison.
    • Phosphate and nitrate dissolve into water → eutrophication. Nitrate also pollutes drinking water. Fertilizer increases infertility, decreases water absorbing capacity of soil.
    • Fertilizer figure: toxic effect on marine flow; more nitrogen and phosphorus → rapid algal bloom → blocks sunlight → loss of useful sea grasses → negative effect on marine ecosystem; poison for fish and marine life.
    • Book's position (UPPSC GIC 2010): soil pollution is more dangerous than industrial pollution, as fertilizers and pesticides enter the food cycle.
    • Carbofuran, methyl parathion, phorate, triazophos = pesticides in agriculture. Kerala's Agriculture Department ban also listed monocrotophos, methyl demethon, prophenophos; Kerala Agriculture University asked for alternatives.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Crop rotation: different crops in sequence on same plot; manages fertility, reduces erosion, raises available nutrients, yields and workability.
    SourceReleased as (nitrogen pollution)
    Synthetic nitrogen fertilizer in agro soilsPlants absorb as nitrate (about 33%); also released as oxides of nitrogenPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Cattle wasteAmmoniaPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Poultry wasteReactive nitrogen compoundPYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Heavy fertilizer + denitrification-favourable soil → large nitrous oxide from farmland. Reactive nitrogen: NH₃, NO₃, NO, N₂O.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Match: cattle waste, poultry waste, nitrogen fertilizer to what they release.

      Ammonia; reactive nitrogen compound; nitrogen oxides. p. 242

    2. Explain how a nitrogen fertilizer can cause air pollution even before it is applied.

      It is produced with large amounts of oil and natural gas, fossil fuels whose use pollutes air. p. 241

    3. Name four pesticides from the Kerala ban list.

      Any four: carbofuran, phorate, methyl parathion, monocrotophos, methyl demethon, prophenophos, triazophos. p. 242

    Plastic Pollution

    Polythene's linked ethylene molecules are not biologically decayed, so discarded plastic persists.

    • Polythene: atoms of carbon and hydrogen; polymer of ethylene (C₂H₄); non-biodegradable. Plastic bag = thermoplastic from polymerisation of ethylene; pollutes atmosphere and soil.
    • Not destroyed by bacteria: plastic, tin, lead. Non-biodegradable, retain form long: metal, tin, glass, plastic. Among cigarette bud, leather shoe, photo film, plastic bag, the bag takes maximum time.
    • Giulio Natta: stereoregular polymers (spatially ordered chains; varying crystallinity gives different properties). Natta and Karl Ziegler (book: Carl Zeigler): Nobel Prize for Chemistry, 1963.
    • Microbeads: usually polyethylene; used in shampoo, face cream, baby lotions; harmful, toxic to marine ecosystem.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Chewing gum (IAS 2024): many contain plastic as gum base, so a source of pollution; Statement II explains I. Gum base: waxes, synthetic rubber (food-grade butyl rubber), plastics; polyisobutylene or polyvinyl acetate, resistant to biological breakdown.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’24
    • World Environment Day, 5 June 2024: theme "Land Restoration, Desertification and Drought Resilience"; slogan "Our Land, Our Future. We are #GenerationRestoration."PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Added concept Not in the book; from a recent paper, key as reported.
    • UPSC 2025: cigarette butts, eyeglass lenses and car tyres all three contain plastic (reported official key (c)); hidden sources of plastic waste.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Polythene is a polymer of which monomer, and why does it persist?

      Ethylene, C₂H₄; its molecules are combined so they do not biologically decay. p. 242

    2. Correct this: "Microbeads are mainly a threat because crop plants absorb them."

      False. The concern is harm to the marine ecosystem. p. 243

    3. Who shared the 1963 Chemistry Nobel for polymer work?

      Giulio Natta and Karl Ziegler. p. 242

    Radioactive and other Gaseous Pollution

    Radiation from disintegrating radioactive nuclides harms tissues, organs and genetic properties.

    Radioactive pollution
    • Chernobyl: nuclear accident, 26 April 1986, Ukraine (then USSR); many deaths and injuries.
    • Radioactive pollution: hereditary changes in all living organisms; hinders blood circulation; causes carcinogenesis. Imbalance among soil minerals was the statement marked out.
    • Nuclear reactors → thermal pollution: river or lake water cools reactors, returns hot, harmfully raising water temperature.
    Source check Q1040's stem "nuclear energy in nuclear reactor is produced by" (p. 245) is garbled; the book's explanation shows the intended sense is the pollution caused by nuclear energy use.
    Bhopal Gas Tragedy
    • Night of 2–3 December 1984; Union Carbide India Limited (UCIL) pesticide plant, Bhopal, Madhya Pradesh. Book: world's worst industrial disaster; India's first major industrial disaster.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • Gas: Methyl Isocyanate (MIC), CH₃NCO; very flammable liquid, readily evaporates in air.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • Toll, by source: over 500,000 exposed; 3787 died (state government records); estimated approx. 3,000 within first few days; around 25000 later from gas-caused disease.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the labels: radioactive effects divide into ___ and ___.

      Somatic and genetic. p. 244

    2. Give the date, place and gas of the Bhopal tragedy.

      2–3 December 1984; UCIL plant, Bhopal; methyl isocyanate. p. 244

    3. How does a nuclear plant cause thermal pollution?

      Cooling water returns hot to rivers or lakes. p. 245

    Bio-Remediation

    Detoxifying the environment with micro-organisms, by enhancing the same biodegradation that occurs in nature.

    • Bio-remediation: elimination of toxic pollutants by organisms; overcomes adverse effects at particular places; cleans surface and groundwater and soil.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • COMMON TRAP Pollutants with heavy metals like cadmium and lead cannot be readily and completely treated by micro-organisms (IAS 2017).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Genetic engineering can create purpose-built microbes; e.g. superbug Pseudomonas putida, role in ending ocean oil spills.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Oilzapper (TERI, The Energy and Resources Institute): eco-friendly, cost-effective consortium of crude oil and oily sludge degrading bacteria from natural cultures.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Used in August 2010 after collision of M.V. Khalijia and MSC Chitra in the Mumbai port area.
    Added concept Not in the book; from a recent paper, key as reported.
    • UPSC 2025 (reported official key (a)): activated carbon removes pollutants from effluent streams because it has large surface area and strong potential for adsorbing heavy metals, and it can be easily synthesized from high-carbon wastes; both reasons explain its use.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Classify as in-situ or ex-situ: biosparging, biopiles, bioaugmentation, bio reactor.

      In-situ: biosparging, bioaugmentation. Ex-situ: biopiles, bio reactor. p. 245

    2. Correct this: "Bio-remediation readily and completely treats cadmium and lead."

      False. Such heavy-metal pollutants cannot be readily and completely treated by it. p. 245–246

    3. What is Oilzapper and who developed it?

      A bacterial consortium degrading crude oil and oily sludge, from TERI. p. 245–246

    Euro Standards

    EU limits on exhaust emission for new vehicles; India's Bharat Stage norms follow the same path.

    • Regulated: nitrogen oxides (NOx), total hydrocarbons (THC), non-methane hydrocarbons (NMHC), carbon monoxide (CO), particulate matter (PM), for most vehicle types: cars, trucks (lorries), locomotives, tractors and similar machinery.
    • Euro-I is more liberal than Euro-II.
    • Euro-II: sulphur in ultra-low sulphur diesel 0.05% or less.
    • Dr. Mashelkar Committee on Auto Fuel Policy: implement Euro standards in India in a phased manner.
    • GoI leapfrogged from BS-IV to BS-VI, skipping BS-V, from 1 April 2020.
    Source check Key for Q1044 (UPPCS 2013) is "all of the above", including lead, but the book's explanation lists only NOx, HC, NMHC, CO and PM (p. 247). Separately, book says the International Council on Clean Transportation (ICCT) "has given relaxation" for India to adopt Euro VI directly (p. 246), while the same page attributes the leapfrog to the Government of India.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the dates of BS-IV and BS-VI in India.

      1 April 2017 and 1 April 2020. p. 246

    2. Which committee recommended phased Euro norms for India?

      Dr. Mashelkar Committee on Auto Fuel Policy. p. 246

    3. State the Euro-II sulphur limit for ultra-low sulphur diesel.

      0.05% or less. p. 246–247

    Pollution and Disease

    One in eight deaths in India in 2017 was attributed to air pollution, a greater disease burden than tobacco use.

    Metals and matching
    PollutantDisease / organDetail
    Mercury (Hg)Minamata diseaseContaminated water; amalgam in wastewater turns to methyl mercury. Ataxia, numbness of hands and feet, blurred eyes, muscle weakness, narrowed field of vision, hearing and speech damage
    CadmiumItai-Itai (ouch-ouch)Excess in drinking water; severe pain in bones, joints. Respiratory poison; heart disease by raising blood pressure
    Nitrate ionBlue baby syndromeCorrect pair in UPPCS 2017
    Fluoride ionFluorosis, Knock-Knee"Indigestion" was the wrong pair. Prolonged high intake harms teeth, bones, other organs, altering anatomy and physiology; see drinking water
    LeadNervous system, brain, digestive systemBook: most toxic metal in automobile exhaustREPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • Lead still a hazard after petrol ban: smelting units, paints (keyed correct); lead paint major route in children; also soil, water, lead-containing products, bullets. Pens and pencils, hair oils and cosmetics were left out by the key.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Matching (UPPCS 2012): asbestos dust → lung; lead → brain; mercury → stomach; carbon monoxide → bloodstream.
    • Matching (UPPCS 2008, key): CO → damage to liver and kidney; oxides of nitrogen → cancer; soil particles → ailments of breathing; lead → central nervous system.
    • Asbestos dust can cause lung cancer; dust particles cause breathing-related disease; carbon monoxide displaces oxygen in bloodstream.
    Source check (1) Q1052's key gives CO → liver and kidney, whereas Q1051 and p. 221, 247 give CO → bloodstream/oxygen; expect the bloodstream link unless the options force otherwise. (2) Q1048 (IAS 2012) keys out cosmetics as a lead source, yet Q1050 (IAS 2017) keys lipstick–lead as correct (p. 248).
    Chemicals in products and other agents
    • Likely in products (IAS 2017, all correct): lipstick–lead (FDA tests found lead in 100% of lipsticks tested); soft drinks–brominated vegetable oil (BVO; keeps citrus flavour from separating); Chinese fast food–monosodium glutamate.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • MSG: sodium salt of glutamic acid; flavour enhancer used roughly 100 years; natural in certain foods; additive in Chinese recipes, canned vegetables, soups, processed goods.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Steel industry (IAS 2014, all four): CO, oxides of sulphur, oxides of nitrogen, CO₂. Coke reacting with iron ore releases CO and CO₂; coal gives SOx, NOx.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Aflatoxins: poisonous, cancer-causing, produced by certain moulds in soil, decaying vegetation, grain; not destroyed by normal cooking. In improperly stored cassava, chilli peppers, corn, cotton seed, millet, peanut, rice, wheat.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs): organic compounds resistant to chemical, biological and photolytic degradation; some brominated flame retardants are included.
    • Brominated flame retardants: organobromine compounds that inhibit combustion chemistry; in mattresses, upholstery. Highly resistant to degradation, accumulate in humans and animals; some persistent, bio-accumulative, toxic; suspected neuro-behavioral effects and endocrine disruption.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Nanoparticles: pollute water, soil, air; taken up by bacteria, can affect whole food chain; zinc oxide and titanium dioxide nanoparticles produce harmful free radicals.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Respirable micro particles enter lungs during respiration. Book: under 0.5 μ not stopped by nasal membrane; greater than 0.5–3 micron stopped.
    • RSPM (UPPCS 2017): key "less than 5 μm"; produced by combustion, vehicles, industrial sources.
    Source check The Q1056 explanation also defines RSPM as ≤ 10 micrometres aerodynamic diameter, and p. 229 labels PM10 and PM2.5 as RSPM, against the key's "less than 5 μm" and p. 248's 0.5 μ. Book's figures are not reconciled; note the question-specific key only.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Match Minamata, Itai-Itai, Blue baby to their pollutants.

      Mercury; cadmium; nitrate ion. p. 247–248

    2. Who produces aflatoxins, and on which commodities?

      Certain moulds; improperly stored staples such as corn, peanut, rice, wheat. p. 247, 249

    3. Correct this: "Nanoparticles of zinc oxide and titanium dioxide are inert."

      False. They produce free radicals. p. 248, 250

    4. Name the product paired with lead, brominated oil and MSG in IAS 2017.

      Lipstick; soft drinks; Chinese fast food. p. 248

    Pollution (Miscellaneous)

    Waste-to-energy technologies, e-waste responsibility, fuel blends and geo-engineering, plus a few standalone facts.

    Waste and producer responsibility
    • Incinerators: burning wastes; incineration = combustion of organic substances in waste materials.
    • Pyrolysis and plasma gasification: waste-to-energy thermal processes at high temperature. Plasma gasification allows safe disposal of all organic wastes; inorganics (glass, metals) minimized to slag.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Products: carbonaceous matter, methane, CO, hydrogen, CO₂, water; gases have calorific and commercial value. NITI Aayog's three-year Action Agenda (2017–2020) assessed benefit-cost ratio.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR): producer responsible for environmentally sound management of the product until end of life. First in India in 2011: Plastic Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2011 and E-Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2011.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • R2 Code of Practices: environmentally responsible practices in electronics recycling; R2-certified firms gain higher margins, market share, status.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’21
    Added concept Not in the book; recent Mains question, described as asked (no model answer implied).
    • Mains 2025: why mining is an environmental hazard, and remedial measures. Book links: coal mines and the Damodar ↗; gold mining and mercury ↗.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Other facts
    • Asbestosis: chronic lung disease from inhaling asbestos fibres; scarring, shortness of breath; can cause mesothelioma cancer.
    • La Nina (book: La-Nino): below-average sea surface temperatures across east-central equatorial Pacific; intense cold in northern India, January 2012.
    • Mustard seed adulterant: Argemone seeds; Argemone mexicana, poppy species found in Mexico; in mustard oil can cause epidemic dropsy.
    • Coal combustion at thermal power plants emits CO₂, oxides of nitrogen, oxides of sulphur (all three).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’11
    • Matching: acid rain–SO₂; photochemical smog–unsaturated hydrocarbons; combination with haemoglobin–CO; ozone layer depletion–chlorofluorocarbon.
    • H-CNG (IAS 2019): up to one-fifth hydrogen by volume blended with CNG for buses; reduces CO₂ and hydrocarbons; CO cut up to 70%, not eliminated; not cheaper than CNG.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Geo-engineering to reduce global warming: sulphate aerosol injection into stratosphere (reflects sunlight; models show even 1% of incident solar radiation reflected offsets significant temperature rise).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Cirrus cloud thinning: these ice-crystal clouds pass sunlight but trap outgoing infrared; reducing them lets infrared escape and cools the planet.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    Source check Bihar pollution-free target (69th BPSC 2023): key (*), question deleted. Book: MoU with UNEP in February 2021 to make Bihar pollution-free from 2040, a year not in the options (p. 252).

    Bihar–UNEP MoU: February 2021, target year 2040 per the book.

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Under which 2011 rules did EPR first appear in India?

      Plastic Waste and E-Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2011. p. 251

    2. Correct this: "H-CNG eliminates CO emissions and is cheaper than CNG."

      False on both. CO cut up to 70%; not cheaper. p. 252

    3. Explain how cirrus thinning cools the Earth.

      Fewer cirrus clouds let outgoing infrared escape to space. p. 252

    4. Match the phenomenon to the compound: acid rain, ozone depletion.

      SO₂; chlorofluorocarbon. p. 251

    Water Conservation

    Water Resources

    Water is not a biotic resource; water conservation rests on observance days, UN programmes and individual champions.

    • Biotic resources: from living and organic material (forests, animals). Fossil fuels (coal, petroleum) included, being decayed organic matter. Fish biotic. Fresh water not biotic.
    • Drinking water on Earth is available in very small amount, hence conservation techniques.
    • World Water Day: 22 March; focus on freshwater, sustainable management. Themes: 2024 "Water for Peace"; 2025 "Glacier Preservation".
    • First World Water Day designated by United Nations: 1993; decided at UN Conference on Environment and Development (book prints "conservation").
    • UN water programme running since 2003: clean water resources, cleanliness, water-related disasters.
    • Rajendra Singh ("Jal Purush", Waterman of India): Alwar district, Rajasthan; runs NGO Tarun Bharat Sangh (TBS); Stockholm Water Prize 2015; Ramon Magsaysay Award 2001 for community-based water harvesting and management.
    • Lisbon Declaration (UN Ocean Conference): conservation of oceans; "Our Ocean, Our Future, Our Responsibility"; asks governments to prevent, reduce and eliminate marine pollution; protects marine and coastal ecosystems.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Correct: "Coal is not a biotic resource but freshwater is."

      False, both ways. Coal is biotic (decayed organic matter); freshwater is not. p. 253

    2. Give the World Water Day date and first year.

      22 March; 1993. p. 253

    3. Name the NGO run by the Waterman of India.

      Tarun Bharat Sangh. p. 253

    Water Pollution and Prevention

    Disinfection and groundwater issues sit alongside the National Water Mission and the 1974 Water Act.

    Disinfection and water quality
    • Making water bacteria-free: temperature and other physical factors; ultraviolet rays and radioactive ions; oxidation by compounds of Ozone, iodine, chlorine.
    • Disinfectants: Ozone, Chlorine dioxide (ClO₂), Chloramine (NH₂Cl): all three correct. Purification removes undesirable chemicals, biological contaminants, suspended solids, gases.
    • Chlorine gas and bleaching powder make drinking water bacteria-free; chlorine used at primary stage to deactivate bacteria.
    • CORE CONCEPTUV (electromagnetic radiation): inactivates/kills harmful micro-organisms only, destroying 99.99% and their ability to reproduce; no chemicals; taste, smell, colour unchanged. Does not remove odours or turbidity/sedimentation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Arsenic in drinking water: highest in Kolkata, West Bengal (Jadavpur University study: many municipal wards).
    • Heavy sand mining in riverbeds: pollution of groundwater, lowering of water-table. Not decreased salinity.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Sand removal raises water velocity, erodes banks; sand is a sponge recharging water table and a filter for pollutants.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    Source check Book says UV makes water "insect free" (p. 254), while its own explanation of Q1082 says harmful micro-organisms (p. 256). Read as micro-organisms.
    National Water Mission and groundwater
    • NWM: part of National Action Plan on Climate Change (started 30 June 2008). Objective: conserve water, minimise wastage, more equitable distribution across and within states through integrated water resources development and management.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Five goals: (i) comprehensive water database in public domain; (ii) assess climate change impact on water resources; (iii) promote citizen and state action for conservation, augmentation, preservation, with focus on vulnerable and over-exploited areas; (iv) raise water use efficiency by 20%; (v) basin-level integrated water resource management.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Impact (IAS 2012): urban needs partly met by recycling waste-water; coastal cities use ocean water by suitable technology. River linking and full bore-well reimbursement are not part of it.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’12
    • Central Ground Water Authority (CGWA): formed under Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, to regulate ground water development and management.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    • India has the largest groundwater-irrigated area in world, followed by China and USA. Statement "36% of districts over-exploited or critical" was marked wrong (IAS 2020; book gives no reason).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’20
    Added concept
    • UPSC Mains 2025 GS-III asked for factors depleting groundwater in India and government steps to mitigate it. Not treated as such in the book; link to CGWA regulation and NWM's focus on over-exploited areas above.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    • UPSC Mains 2025 GS-III asked for causes of seawater intrusion in coastal aquifers and remedial measures. Not in the book. Concept: over-pumping near the coast lowers the fresh groundwater level, letting saline water move into the aquifer.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Water Act and Cess Act
    • Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act enacted 1974; prevents and controls water pollution, maintains or restores wholesomeness of water; amended 1988.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Created Central Pollution Control Board and State Pollution Control Boards; they monitor water bodies and industrial discharges, enforce standards, act against violators; penalties for violation.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION3 papers
    • Water Cess Act enacted 7 December 1977; levies cess on water used by certain industrial activities; enforced 01.04.1978.
    Source check Q1080 (U.P.R.O./A.R.O. Mains 2013) is keyed (*): book's explanation gives enactment 1977 but enforcement 01.04.1978, an option that was not offered (p. 256).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Correct: "UV radiation removes odour and speeds sedimentation."

      False. It only inactivates micro-organisms. p. 256

    2. Name NWM's parent plan and start date.

      National Action Plan on Climate Change, 30 June 2008. p. 254

    3. Which Act created the CPCB and SPCBs? When?

      Water Act, 1974. p. 256

    4. How does sand mining lower the water table?

      Sand acts as a sponge recharging groundwater; removing it reduces that recharge. p. 257

    Water Conservation

    Plants conserve water by leaf change; lakes and watersheds are conserved through central schemes.

    • Desert leaf modifications limiting water loss: hard waxy leaves, tiny or no leaves, thorns; all correct. Leaves become spines; gummy substance cuts loss; thorns condense air moisture that drips to roots. → Ch 6PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’13
    • National Lake Conservation Plan (NLCP): centrally sponsored; started 2001 by Central Forest and Environment Ministry. Restores urban and semi-urban lakes degraded by wastewater, integrated ecosystem approach.
    • Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA), 7 February 2013: merged NLCP + National Wetlands Conservation Programme into National Plan for the Conservation of Aquatic Ecosystem (NPCA).
    • Under NLCP: Bhoj (MP), Sukhna (Chandigarh), Pichola (Rajasthan). Chilika (Odisha) is not.
    • Recently included (question time): Bhimtal. Ooty was in from the beginning. Book's reason: Pulicat and Sambhar are not freshwater lakes.
    • Hariyali: central watershed project; villagers take up small works for drinking, irrigation, fisheries, afforestation; adds rural employment.
    Lake (NLCP list, p. 257)Place/State

    Channapatna; BellandurKarnataka; Bengaluru
    Sagar, Rani Talab, ShivpuriMadhya Pradesh

    Ooty, KodaikanalTamil Nadu

    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. NLCP merged with which programme to form what, and when?

      National Wetlands Conservation Programme; NPCA; 7 February 2013. p. 257

    2. Which lake is outside NLCP: Bhoj or Chilika?

      Chilika. p. 258

    3. How do thorns help a desert plant?

      They reduce water loss and condense moisture that drips to the roots. p. 258

    Ganga Conservation

    Ganga governance moved from the 1985 authority through NRCA and NGRBA to Namami Gange.

    Plans and authorities
    • Central Ganga Authority: constituted 1985 under PM Rajiv Gandhi. September 1995: renamed National River Conservation Authority (NRCA), PM as Chairman.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • NRCP aims to improve river water quality through pollution abatement works. Book notes activities have not attained expected success.
    • NRCP activities: interception and diversion of raw sewage from open drains; sewage treatment plants; low-cost sanitation against open defecation on banks; electric and improved wood crematoria; public awareness and participation.
    • NGRBA: 20 February 2009, gazette notification under s. 3(3), Environment Protection Act, 1986. Financing, planning, implementing, monitoring, coordinating body.REPEATED FACT / QUESTIONUPSC ’16 · 2 papers
    • COMMON TRAPNGRBA Chairman is the Prime Minister (not a rotating CM); Chief Ministers of Ganga states are members; apex body headed by PM for policy decisions. River basin is unit of planning; aim of effective pollution abatement and conservation by 2020; river cleaning extended to other major rivers.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Jharkhand State Ganga River Conservation Authority: 2009; Chief Minister ex-officio Chairman.
    • July 2016: Union Water Resources Ministry set a committee under Madhav Chitale (expert member, NGRBA) to prepare guidelines on the river's silt.
    • Namami Gange: Budget 2014-15, then Finance Minister Arun Jaitley; integrated conservation flagship; Rs. 20,000 crore; extended till March 2026 with Rs. 22,500 crore. Aim: abatement of pollution, conservation, rejuvenation.
    • Pillars: Sewage Treatment Infrastructure; River Front Development; River Surface Cleaning; Biodiversity; Afforestation; Public Awareness; Industrial Effluent Monitoring; Ganga Gram.
    • Provisions: Nirmal Dhara, Aviral Dhara, Ecological entity, Geographic entity. Ghat development and river-front beautification: Kedarnath, Haridwar, Kanpur, Varanasi, Prayagraj, Patna, Delhi.
    • Yamuna Action Plan: formally launched 1993; bilateral with Japan. YAP-I (April 1993–2003): Delhi, eight UP towns, six Haryana towns. YAP-II (from December 2004): 22 km Delhi stretch. YAP-III launched in 2010s under Namami Gange (book's phrasing).
    • 'Maili Se Nirmal Yamuna Revitalisation Project': enforced from 2017.
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's NGRBA position (2009) is superseded: CPCB states NGRBA was dissolved with effect from 7 October 2016, replaced by the National Council for River Ganga (Rejuvenation, Protection and Management), constituted under notification S.O. 3187(E) under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986. source
    Source check Book says the 2016 committee's guidelines were for "distillation" of the Ganga (p. 259). Read as desiltation; the term was not confirmed from an opened source in this run, so no link is given.
    Ganga river dolphin
    • One of four obligate freshwater dolphins; scientific name Platanista gangetica; a mammal; mostly in deep water and river confluences.
    • National aquatic animal: declared 2009; notified by MoEF on 18 May 2010.
    • Habitat: Ganges–Brahmaputra–Meghna and Karnaphuli–Sangu systems of Nepal, India, Bangladesh.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Decline: dams and barrages; accidental fishing-net entanglement; pesticides, fertilisers, industrial effluents. Not crocodile increase.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • IUCN figure in book: approximately 1200–1400. Book also gives Gharial critically endangered, crocodile vulnerable.
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Against the book's 1200–1400: the government report "Population Status of River Dolphin in India" (3 March 2025) estimated 6,327 river dolphins in the Ganga, Brahmaputra and Indus systems; UP 2,397, Bihar 2,220. The article's species split does not reconcile, so only the total is given. source
    Other tested points
    • Agricultural soils: organic matter improves water-holding capacity (soil texture + organic matter decide it); plant residues cut crusting, raise infiltration, reduce runoff.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Soil is part of the sulphur cycle (sulphur cycled between soil, living matter, atmosphere). Salinization: irrigation over time can deposit salts in some farmland to levels harmful to crops.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Shrunk by human activity: Aral Sea only (not Black Sea, not Lake Baikal). Central Asian saline lake, once 4th largest salt lake; once 26,300 square miles; by 2007, 10% of original area.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’18
    • Central Water Commission established Irrigation Research and Management Organization in 1984.
    Source check Q1099 (Chhattisgarh P.C.S. 2016): Chhattisgarh Public Service Commission's first answer sheet gave (a) 1984, the revised sheet (e); the book holds (a) and says the revised key is wrong (p. 262).
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Put in order: NRCA, Central Ganga Authority, NGRBA, Namami Gange.

      Central Ganga Authority 1985; NRCA 1995; NGRBA 2009; Namami Gange 2014-15. p. 259

    2. Correct: "A Chief Minister chairs NGRBA by rotation."

      False. The Prime Minister chairs it. p. 261

    3. Name three causes of decline of Ganga dolphins other than poaching.

      Dams and barrages; accidental fishing-net entanglement; agricultural chemicals. p. 261

    4. State the name of the scientific taxon of the Ganga dolphin.

      Platanista gangetica. p. 260

    Miscellaneous

    Agenda 21 and other Conventions

    One UN plan, one disarmament body, Indian quality marks and two cooperation instruments.

    Agenda 21
    • Agenda 21 = legally non-binding, voluntarily implemented UN action plan on sustainable development.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Aim: conserve the world's environment in the 21st century.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Product of UNCED (United Nations Conference on Environment and Development, the Earth Summit), Rio-de-Janeiro, Brazil, 1992.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Structure: 40 chapters grouped into 4 sections.
    Source check Page count differs inside p. 263: note says 351, explanation says 350. Q on p. 263 asks how many "agreements" (key 4); explanation on p. 264 says "4 agreement in Agenda-2021". The 4 are the book's sections or groups, so read that key as "4 sections", not 4 treaties.
    OPCW
    • Organization for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons: intergovernmental; implementing body of the Chemical Weapons Convention (CWC, in force 29 April 1997).
    • Headquarters: The Hague, Netherlands. Members: 193 countries (book's "currently"), 98% of global population and landmass.
    • Mission: world free of chemical weapons and the threat of their use.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Credible, transparent verification of destruction; prevents re-emergence, monitoring chemical industry, while protecting legitimate national security and proprietary interests.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Gives assistance and protection to States Parties against chemical weapons threats.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Also: peaceful uses of chemistry; universal membership through international cooperation and national capacity building.
    • Book: Hundred percent of declared stockpiles of possessor states verifiably destroyed.
    • 2013 Nobel Peace Prize for "extensive efforts to eliminate chemical weapons".
    • COMMON TRAP OPCW is not an organisation of the European Union working with NATO and WHO; that statement was false.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    Standards and quality marks
    Mark or lawFacts
    Indian Standards Institution (ISI)Set up January 1947; organised start of industrial development, consumer protection and standards formation.
    Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS)National Standards Organization of India; BIS Act 1986, in force 1987; aim: standardization, symbolization of objects, quality certificate.
    ISI markMain BIS symbol; on processed food and daily-use goods such as electric equipment, cement, iron pipes.
    BIS standard mark on tyres and tubesMandatory for automotive tyres and tubes; pneumatic tyres for two and three-wheeled motor vehicles need ISI Certification before launch or import into India. Granted only to product manufacturers (Indian or foreign), not to traders, dealers or distributors.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    AGMARK ActAgricultural Produce (Grading and Marking) Act, 1937; grading and marking of agriculture and other produce; in force 1937.
    AGMARK markIssued by Directorate of Marketing and Inspection, Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare (not FAO). Directorate set up 1935; categorises and symbolises food products.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    Eco-MarkGovernment of India eco-labelling scheme, 1991; easy identification of environment-friendly products. Drugs and Antibiotics are not in the list (soaps and detergents, paper and plastics, cosmetics and aerosols were the other options).

    Food Safety and Standards (Packaging and Labelling) Regulations, 2011: every package carries (1) name of food, (2) List of Ingredients, (3) Nutritional information, (4) Energy value in Kcal, (5) protein, carbohydrate (specify sugar) and fat in gram or ml, (6) any nutrient with a nutrition or health claim, (7) vegetarian or non-vegetarian declaration, (8) food additives declaration. Key: ingredients including additives, nutrition, veg/non-veg are mandatory; a "medical profession recommendation on allergy" is not.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16

    UN-Habitat and Asian Rhinos
    • United Nations Human Settlements Programme (UN-Habitat): UN agency for human settlements and sustainable urban development; established 1978, Nairobi, capital of Kenya.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Mandated by UN General Assembly: socially and environmentally sustainable towns and cities, adequate shelter for all.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Contributes to UN objective of reducing poverty and promoting access to safe drinking water and basic sanitation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • COMMON TRAP Partners are not only governments and local municipal authorities; also private and non-governmental partners.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • "New Delhi Declaration on Asian Rhinos 2019": five rhino range nations = India, Nepal, Malaysia, Indonesia, Bhutan.
    • Second Asian Rhino Range Countries meeting, 26–28 February 2019, New Delhi; organised by MoEFCC with IUCN Asian Rhino Specialist Group, WWF-India and Aaranyak.
    Added concept Not in the book; from the recent-paper file.
    • Nature Solutions Finance Hub for Asia and the Pacific: launched by the Asian Development Bank (ADB), as per the reported official key for UPSC Prelims 2025.RECENT PYQ — 2025
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Give the status, origin and layout of Agenda 21.

      Non-binding UN action plan, Earth Summit Rio 1992, 40 chapters in 4 sections. Voluntarily implemented. p. 263

    2. Correct this: "OPCW is a European Union body working with NATO."

      It is an intergovernmental organisation implementing the CWC, based at The Hague. p. 263–264

    3. Who issues the ISI mark and who issues AGMARK?

      BIS issues ISI; Directorate of Marketing and Inspection issues AGMARK. FAO issues neither. p. 263–264

    4. Name the fifth signatory after India, Nepal, Malaysia and Indonesia in the 2019 rhino declaration.

      Bhutan. p. 265

    Organizations and Institutions

    Mostly "which place" recall: Antarctic bases, institute locations, climate missions.

    India in Antarctica
    • Dakshin Gangotri = India's first Antarctic research station, part of Indian Antarctic Programme.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Established (FY) 1983 during the third Indian expedition; first time an Indian team wintered there for scientific work.
    • Built in eight weeks by an 81-member team that included geologist Sudipta Sengupta.
    • 2,500 km (about 1,600 mi) from the South Pole; named after Dakshin Gangotri Glacier; book: now a supply base and transit camp.
    • Maitri (book also "Maitree"): second station, 1989.
    • Bharati: third station.
    • Book credits India with three permanent bases, but only two are operational: Maitri and Bharati.
    Source check Book dates Bharati to March 2013 (p. 265). NCPOR says it was commissioned on 18 March 2012. source

    • National Mission for Green India (GIM): one of the eight missions of the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC).PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Aim: protect, restore and enhance India's diminishing forest cover and respond to climate change through combined adaptation and mitigation.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Holistic view of greening; multiple ecosystem services, especially biodiversity, water, biomass, preserving mangroves, wetland, critical habitats.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Not its aim: green accounting in budgets; a second green revolution.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Green accounting (separate concept): comprehensive estimation of economic activities and their effects on the environment.

    Various Awards

    Awards exist to build environmental consciousness; exams ask field and recipient.

    Indian awards
    • Indira Gandhi Paryavaran Puraskar: instituted 1987 by the then Ministry of Environment and Forest, to encourage public participation; for individuals and organizations with significant, measurable contribution to environment protection and improvement.
    • Rajiv Gandhi Environment Award: to industrial units for new or innovatively modified technologies, or clean technologies and practices that substantially reduce or prevent pollution.
    Source check Book's note says "new technology" (p. 268); the option reads "Neat technology" (p. 269); explanation says clean technologies (p. 270). Read the field as clean technology and development.
    Tyler Prize
    • Tyler Prize for Environment Achievement: premier award for environmental science, environmental health and energy, conferring great benefit on mankind; regarded as the "Nobel Prize for Environment".
    • Administered by University of Southern California; established by John and Alice Tyler.
    YearRecipient as given in book

    2017Jose Sarukhan Kermez, ecologist, Mexico

    2025Sandra Diaz and Eduardo Brondizio
    Global 500 and Champions of the Earth
    • Global 500: launched 1987 by UNEP; honoured environmental and humanitarian achievement.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • Tribute to front-line action; individuals and organizations combating environmental issues despite social, political and logistical obstacles.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION6 papers
    • 2003: India's Dr. Bindeshwar Pathak (founder of Sulabh International).
    • Replaced by 'Champions of the Earth' from 2005; called the UN's highest environmental honour.
    • 2013 included Veerabhadran Ramanathan (American of Indian origin); 2016 included Afroz Shah, Mumbai.
    • 2018: Prime Minister Narendra Modi, conferred by UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres at a ceremony in Delhi.
    Category20232024

    Entrepreneurial VisionBlue Circle; Jose Manuel MollerSEKEM
    Science and InnovationCSIRLu Qi
    Lifetime AchievementNot listedMadhav Gadgil
    Agricultural honours
    • Borlaug Award: for outstanding research and contribution in agriculture.
    • COMMON TRAP Norman Ernest Borlaug (American agronomist, Green Revolution) won the Nobel Peace Prize, not one in "agriculture"; also Presidential Medal of Freedom and Congressional Gold Medal.
    • UNEP recognised Dr. M.S. Swaminathan as Father of Economic Ecology.

The I.A.S. 2001 match keys it to the listed "October 4", yet the explanation still says first Monday (p. 272). The U.P.P.C.S. 2000 question is marked "Ans. (*)" (p. 271).
    • WHO: Constitution came into force 7 April 1948, the date now celebrated as World Health Day.

    World Heritage and Creative Cities

    From the book's "Various Facts Regarding Environment": inscription years and the Creative Cities table.

    World Heritage Sites
    • Book's "currently": 43 World Heritage Sites in India: 35 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed.
    • Sun Temple of Konark 1984; Mahabodhi Temple complex, Bodh Gaya, Bihar 2002 (the "recently included" site as at 2002).
    • Jantar Mantar of Jaipur (2010) took India's count to 28.
    • 2023 inscriptions: Santiniketan (book also Shantiniketan; West Bengal), Sacred Ensembles of the Hoysalas (Karnataka). Rani-ki-Vav (the Queen's Stepwell, Patan, Gujarat) is 2014.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’24
    • 2024: Moidams – the Mound-Burial System of the Ahom Dynasty (Assam), India's 43rd property.
    • COMMON TRAP Bihar has two, so "only site" is wrong: Mahabodhi Temple Complex, Bodh Gaya (2002) and Archaeological Site of Nalanda Mahavihar, Nalanda (2016).
    • India Gate is not a World Heritage Site; Red Fort Complex (New Delhi) 2007 and Qutb Minar and its monuments (Delhi) 1993 are.
    • Jaipur (the city) was listed at the 43rd session of the World Heritage Committee, Baku, capital of Azerbaijan.
    Verified update (checked Nov 2026) Book's position: 43 sites (35 + 7 + 1), latest Moidams 2024. Wikipedia's list now shows 45 sites (37 cultural, 7 natural, 1 mixed), naming the Ancient Buddhist Site of Sarnath (2026) as the latest; UNESCO's own page was not accessible. source
    Source check p. 273 prints a garbled sentence: "Maharashtra is having highest number of sites and Moidams…". Maharashtra's lead is the book's claim; the Moidams date comes from p. 275. It also prints "Scred Ensembles of the Haysotas".
