    Disaster Management

    Global decades, the Indian statutory ladder, phases of management, and hazard facts.

    Global decades and alliances
    • UN General Assembly designated 1990–99 as International Decade for Natural Disaster Reduction (IDNDR).
    • Objective: cut loss of life, property destruction, social and economic disruption from earthquakes, tsunamis, floods, landslides, volcanic eruptions, droughts and other natural disasters.
    • 2021–2030: United Nations Decade on Ecosystem Restoration.
    • International Drought Resilience Alliance (IDRA): launched by Spain with Senegal at UNFCCC COP27, November 2022; first announced by Spain at the 77th UN General Assembly, September 2022. (COP 28: Nov–Dec 2023, Dubai.)
    Source check IDNDR question (p. 277) is marked "Ans. (*)": none of its options (1991–2000 etc.) matches the book's 1990–99.
    Disaster Management Act and authorities
    • Disaster Management Act: passed 2005 (enacted 23 December 2005), for efficient management of disasters; book: came into force January 2006.
    • Act envisaged NDMA headed by the Prime Minister and SDMAs headed by Chief Ministers, for a holistic, integrated approach.
    • NDMA = apex body: lays down policies, plans and guidelines for timely and effective response.
    • DDMA: headed by District Magistrate or District Collector, as the case may be.
    • Section 14(1): book says it empowers the Governor to establish a State Disaster Management Authority, after notification under Section 3(1).
    • National Institute of Disaster Management (NIDM): New Delhi; book dates founding to 1995 (named National Center for Disaster Management, NCDM, on p. 274).
    • Book: National Disaster Management Institute established under the Disaster Management Act, 2005.
    • SRIJAN (Development of Disaster Management Knowledge-cum-Demonstration Centre): creates such centres, spreads awareness, provides local need-based information.
    • Designed for disasters such as drought, flood, forest fire; a major centre for local information, communication and technology equipment.
    Source check NDMA "formed": key says 2005 (p. 277) but the explanation says it was notified on 27 September 2006 through the DM Act, 2005 (p. 278). Remember both: Act 2005, notification 2006, and read the year asked.
    Phases and vulnerability
    • Book's five main phases: prevention, preparedness, response, mitigation, recovery.
    • CORE CONCEPT Reconstruction is a post-disaster measure.
    • India is prone to many natural and man-made disasters: droughts, floods, earthquakes, cyclones, avalanches.
    • Floods are the most common natural disaster in India; heavy southwest monsoon rain makes the Brahmaputra and other rivers extend their banks.
    • Bhopal gas tragedy was man-made; mangroves reduce cyclone impact; natural disasters cause maximum damage in developing countries.
    Added concept Mains prompts not treated in the book; they state what was asked, with no model answer.
    • GS-III 2026: managing the contradiction between rapid infrastructure development and disaster-risk reduction in ecologically-sensitive areas of India, with examples.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    • GS-III 2026: "Community participation is the cornerstone of effective disaster management": analyse with Indian examples; challenges to participation and measures to strengthen it.RECENT PYQ — 2026
    Source check Book prints key (c) for this set (p. 278) but also says the Hindi version made statement 3 read "India is a disaster-prone country", so UPPSC removed the question from evaluation. Treat it as deleted; India is not disaster-free.
    Tsunamis and hurricanes
    • Tsunami: series of large waves from abrupt ocean-floor movement.
    • Causes: earthquake, underwater landslide, volcanic eruption, very rarely a large meteorite strike; powerful undersea (submarine) earthquakes cause most.
    • Hurricane Sandy (2012) struck the north-east and east coast of the USA; Katrina, Wilma (book: Vilma) and Rita were 2005.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Fill the heads: NDMA, SDMA, DDMA.

      Prime Minister, Chief Minister, District Magistrate or Collector. p. 278

    2. Sort into pre and post: reconstruction, mitigation, response, preparedness.

      Pre: mitigation, preparedness. Post: reconstruction, response. p. 279

    3. Correct this: "IDNDR was 1991–2000."

      1990–99. p. 277

    4. Explain the "NDMA 2005 or 2006" conflict in one sentence.

      The Act dates to 2005; the book says NDMA was notified on 27 September 2006. p. 278

    Biodiversity, Parks and Species

    Stray "Various Facts" on megadiversity, productivity, protected areas, fossils and particular plants and animals.

    Megadiverse countries
    • Megadiverse countries: world's top biodiversity-rich countries, where maximum species are found.
    • American non-profit Conservation International identified 17 in 1998.
    • The 17: U.S.A., Mexico, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Venezuela, Brazil, Democratic Republic of Congo, South Africa, Madagascar, India, Malaysia, Indonesia, Philippines, Papua New Guinea, China, Australia.
    Primary productivity
    • Primary productivity: rate at which photosynthetic and chemosynthetic autotrophs convert energy to organic substances.
    • Total productivity in a region or system = gross primary productivity.
    • Lowest mean net primary productivity among the four biomes below: temperate grasslands.
    Source check Book prints these as "gm per cm² per year" (pp. 274, 280). Grams per square centimetre would be implausibly high for any ecosystem, so read the values as relative magnitudes and confirm the area unit from a standard ecology text.
    Parks and sanctuaries

    Fuller protected-area lists: → Ch 7

    SiteState
    KazirangaAssam
    GirGujarat
    KanhaMadhya Pradesh
    CorbettUttarakhand
    NamdaphaArunachal Pradesh
    BandipurKarnataka
    PeriyarKerala
    LamjaoManipur
    • Other Kerala names given: Idukki, Parambikulam, Chinnar, Wayanad, Muthanga and Aralam Wildlife Sanctuaries; Thattekad Bird Sanctuary; Eravikulam and Silent Valley National Parks.
    • Marine National Park, Gulf of Kutch (Kachchh): southern shore of the Gulf, Jamnagar district, Gujarat; India's first national marine park, 42 islands.
    • Bhitarkanika: rich estuarine ecosystem of Brahmani-Baitarani rivers, north-eastern corner of Kendrapara district, Odisha.
    Source check Q stem on p. 280 says Bhitarkanika was "recently included in the list of World Heritage Sites". The explanation only describes its location and does not support inscription, and it is absent from the inscriptions the book names, so it is not recorded here as a World Heritage Site.
    Fossils and geological heritage
    • National Dinosaur Fossil Park (sometimes 'Dinosaur Fossil National Park'; book: "being established"): Dhar district, Madhya Pradesh, on recommendation of the Madhya Pradesh Wildlife Board.
    • Near Bagh Caves, Dhar Forest Division; 89.74 hectares. In 2006, more than 100 Dinosaur eggs found in Dhar, estimated at 65 to 70 million years old.
    • Montana, USA: called the graveyard of Dinosaurs.
    • Tillyardembiids: fossils of the world's first plant pollinators; rare earwig-like insects; palaeontologists found them along a river bank near the village of Chekarda, Russia.
    Geological heritage siteLocation
    National Fossil Wood ParkTiruchirappalli
    Shivalik ParkSirmaur (Sirmur)
    Stromatolite ParkChittaurgarh
    Lonar LakeBuldhana
    Animals and plants
    • Dr. Salim Ali: Indian ornithologist and naturalist, 'Birdman of India'.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • His "Handbook of the Birds of India and Pakistan Vol. 5": Common Myna stalks alongside cattle, seizing insects disturbed through grasses.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Painted stork: book says “southern Himalayas” (generally described as wetlands of the plains south of the Himalayas); feeds on small fishes near wetlands.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • Black-necked crane: Tibetan Plateau, Bhutan, Arunachal Pradesh, Ladakh; omnivore eating plant roots, insects, fish, frog.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’14
    • National aquatic animal of India: Gangetic River Dolphin, notified by Environment and Forest Ministry on 18 May 2010; said to represent the purity of the Ganga, surviving only in pure, fresh water.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’15
    • Uttar Pradesh declared Ganga Dolphin its State aquatic animal (2023) and launched "Meri Ganga Meri Dolphin 2023".
    • Tharparkar is a breed of Cow, not Sheep; found in Gujarat and Rajasthan. Bhadawari: Buffalo breed, Uttar Pradesh and Madhya Pradesh.
    • Loquat: evergreen fruit plant; China is the biggest producer.
    • Prosopis juliflora: exotic plant that wipes out most native plants and animals around it; tends to reduce biodiversity.
    • Sita Ashok: among the given options (with Mahua, Poplar, Neem), the book's best safeguard against dust pollution.
    • Teak: Madhya Pradesh has the largest area of teak forest (ISFR 2023, per book).
    • Himalayan nettle (Girardinia diversifolia): sustainable source of textile fibre; a livelihood option in the Himalayan region.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’19
    • Eichhornia crassipes (water hyacinth): most widely distributed, vicious and world's most invasive aquatic weed, from the Amazonia basin of South America; harms environment, economy, human health; hard to manage.
    • Sea weeds: important source of Iodine; other sources are seafood and dairy products.
    Source check Breed match (p. 281): book says the UPPSC official key is (a), but the correct answer should be (d), as Tharparkar is cattle and Bhadawari really is a buffalo. Both are kept here; do not treat the official key as a fact.

    3. Fill the labels: Stromatolite Park, Shivalik Park, Lonar Lake, National Fossil Wood Park.

    Miscellaneous Facts

    Under the book's last heading, plus similar one-off questions from earlier sections.

    Environment links
    ProblemLinked cause or measure

    Noise PollutionDecibelREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Global WarmingCarbon dioxideREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    Ozone HoleChlorofluorocarbonsREPEATED FACT / QUESTION2 papers
    • Anthropogenic biomes (anthromes, human biomes): terrestrial biosphere in its contemporary, human-altered form, as units defined by sustained direct human interaction.
    • Cropland is an anthropogenic biome; crop field, park, aquarium, garden are anthropogenic ecosystems.
    • Afforestation: increases greenery and atmospheric oxygen; reduces air pollution, global warming and soil erosion.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’09
    Climate, soils and earth history
    • 'Vegetation is the true index of climate': Koppen Climate Classification, Wladimir Petrovich Koppen; first published 1900, revised 1918.
    • Koppen: Uttar Pradesh and Bihar are Cwg. Book: Aw covers south India; Dfc covers Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh and parts of Assam.
    • Winterline: from mid October to January at sunset, the western horizon turns yellow, red, orange and mauve; book: unique to Mussoorie and a part of Switzerland.
    • Book's key: glaciation of peninsular India occurred in the Pleistocene Ice Age.
    • Soil order: most general level of USDA (United States Department of Agriculture) Soil Taxonomy.
    • India, ICAR-USDA, decreasing area, per book's key: Entisols > Alfisols > Aridisols > Ultisols.
    • Explanation's shares are world, not India: Entisols about 16%, Alfisols about 12%, Aridisols about 10% of ice-free land.
    • Entisols occur on recently deposited parent materials, or where erosion or deposition outpaces soil development: dunes, steep slopes, flood plains.
    Source check Key on p. 285 says Pleistocene, with only a one-line explanation. A geology overview instead dates glacial deposits across central India to Permo-Carboniferous glaciations, which matches option (b), Carboniferous. Treat this key as doubtful. source
    Energy terms
    • Net metering: domestic or commercial users generating their own electricity through solar panels or photovoltaic systems export surplus energy to the grid.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’16
    • Domestic Content Requirement (DCR): provided for developing solar power production in India.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • National Solar Policy (earlier Jawaharlal Nehru National Solar Mission), January 2010; aim: India as global solar leader; solar utilities and batteries manufactured in India used for projects.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • First phase: Ministry of New and Renewable Energy gives 30% subsidy on decentralized solar devices such as lighting equipment, water heating equipment or solar coolers.PYQ-LINKED FACTUPSC ’17
    • Natural gas: colourless, odourless, environment-friendly hydrocarbon mixture, primarily Methane.
    Instruments and techniques
    • SODAR (Sonic Detection and Ranging): meteorological instrument used as wind profiler, measuring scattering of sound waves by atmospheric turbulence and wind speed at various heights.
    • In India, SODAR is established at Kaiga, Kalpakkam, Tarapur and Trombay.
    • Helium: inert gas filled in balloons for weather science dispatch.
    • LANDSAT: data for an area adjacent to the west comes after a certain number of days at the same local time, due to the satellite's revolution around the earth.
    • DNA barcode: unique DNA sequence pattern, like a universal product code (UPC); non-experts can objectively identify species even from small, damaged or industrially processed material.
    • COMMON TRAP It distinguishes look-alike species and detects undesirable plant or animal material in processed foods; it does not assess the age of an organism.
    • Used for food authentication, especially fish and seafood, where species outnumber cattle, sheep, goat, horse; also exposed contaminants in roasted barley tea (Hordeum vulgare) exported from China and returned.
    Stray items
    • Uttar Pradesh's first biotech park: Lucknow; Department of Biotechnology (DBT) partnership since 2003.REPEATED FACT / QUESTION5 papers
    • India's largest 'Charkha': made of waste plastic, inaugurated in Noida on the eve of Mahatma Gandhi's 150th birth anniversary.
    • Blue Flag Certification: eight Indian beaches across five states and two union territories, by a jury of UNEP, UNWTO (United Nations World Tourism Organization), FEE (Foundation for Environment Education) and IUCN.
    • The eight: Shivrajpur (Dwarka, Gujarat), Ghoghla (Diu), Kasarkod (book: Kararkod) and Padubidri (Karnataka), Kappad (Kerala), Rushikonda (Andhra Pradesh), Radhanagar (Andaman & Nicobar Islands), Golden beach (Puri, Odisha).
    • Book's 2023 count: 12 Blue Flag beaches in India; they give community pride and attract tourism.
    Test Your Memory — Original Practice

    1. Explain net metering in one sentence.

      A solar-generating consumer exports surplus electricity to the grid. p. 286

    2. Correct this: "DNA barcoding can tell the age of a plant."

      It identifies species and detects undesirable material; age is not its use. p. 284

    3. Which Koppen type covers Uttar Pradesh and Bihar?

      Cwg. p. 282

    4. List the four Indian SODAR sites.

      Kaiga, Kalpakkam, Tarapur, Trombay. p. 279

    Coverage and source note

    What these notes cover, what the PYQ counts rest on, and what could not be checked. Research date: 11 November 2026.

    Source and page numbering

    • Source: Ghatna Chakra PREVIEW (Purvavlokan), General Studies Part 6, Environment & Ecology, 9th edition, 2025, in English. The PDF has 286 file pages.
    • All references are PDF file pages. File pp. 7–286 carry printed numbers F-9 to F-288 (printed = file + 2), and that run is continuous.
    • File pp. 1–6 are cover and front matter: cover (p. 1), preface F-2 (p. 2), index F-3 (p. 3), About the Book F-5 and F-6 (pp. 4–5), Question Paper Analysis F-7 (p. 6). These are image-only and were read from their page images. Printed F-1, F-4 and F-8 are not in this file.

    Stated book coverage (F-5 to F-7)

    • IAS Pre 1993–2024. Environment & Ecology is Part 6 of an 8-part series, in 11 chapters.
    • UP: PCS Pre 1990–2024, PCS Mains 2002–2017, Lower Subordinate, UDA/LDA, RO/ARO, BEO 2019, GIC 2010 and 2017, RI 2014.
    • Other State PSCs: Uttarakhand PCS and UDA/LDA, MP PCS Pre 1990–2025, Chhattisgarh PCS Pre 2003–2024, Rajasthan PCS Pre 1992–2024, Bihar PCS Pre 1992–2024, Jharkhand PCS 2003–2023 and Mains 2016.
    • The cover states coverage to February 2025.
    Source check F-7 states that 5,233 questions were collected, 541 repeats removed and 4,692 included in this section. This PDF contains 1,211 solved question records (one per “Ans.” line). The F-9 to F-288 page run has no gaps, so no pages are missing from the file, and the book gives no reason for the difference. The counts here use what the file actually contains.

    PYQ corpus and how counts were made

    • Book corpus: 1,211 solved question records, 1,207 distinct after merging questions printed twice, giving 1,298 occurrences across 224 distinct papers: UPSC CSE Prelims 260 (labelled I.A.S., 1993–2024); State Prelims 823; State Mains 215.
    • The book holds no UPSC Mains questions. Its “Mains” questions are State MCQs (mostly UPPCS) and are reported separately.
    • 66 records carry several exam labels, which the book prints for exact repeats. 3 records have no exam label and are excluded from every count (notes still use their facts).
    • Each recurrence in a different paper counts as one occurrence. The same exam question printed twice counts once. Re-exams and special papers count as separate papers.
    • Recent supplement: UPSC CSE Prelims GS-I 2025 (17 questions) and 2026 (10); UPSC CSE Mains GS-III 2025 (6) and 2026 (4). Latest paper checked: Mains GS-III, 23 August 2026.
    • Every question was tagged by theme and by the proposition it tests. All counts, rankings and “repeated” badges are computed from those tags, so they depend on the tagger’s judgement about what each question tests.
    • Priority rankings are revision guidance only. No probability or forecast is implied.

    Recent papers: what was and was not checked

    • upsc.gov.in refused automated access (HTTP 403), so no official UPSC page or PDF was opened directly. Recent question text comes from coaching and news reproductions, each matched against at least two of them.
    • Prelims 2025 answers are UPSC’s official key, released about May 2026, read from coaching-hosted copies of UPSC’s scan and mapped to Set B by question content.
    • Prelims 2026 answers are not final. They match UPSC’s provisional key of 27 May 2026 as hosted by a coaching site, and the final key has not been published. Answers can still change.
    • Mains papers have no official answer key.
    • Not analysed: State PSC papers after the book’s coverage, UPSC Mains before 2025, and borderline 2025–26 questions judged to test agriculture, geography, chemistry or finance and not environment.

    Known gaps and items not updated

    • Possibly outdated, left at the book’s dated position because no reliable page could be opened: COP30 and COP31 outcomes, Paris Agreement party count, ISA eligibility, current renewable capacity; Solid Waste Management Rules 2026, E-Waste Rules 2022, NCAP target revision; Biological Diversity Amendment 2023; Water Act 2024 amendment; Environment (Protection) Act amendments after 1991 (Jan Vishwas 2023); Disaster Management Amendment Act 2025; UNESCO World Heritage and global biosphere counts; post-2024 SDG ranks. Check these before relying on them.
    • Single-source or secondary updates: Ramsar count (103), tiger reserve count (58) and one World Heritage update rest on a press or encyclopaedia source because official pages were blocked.
    • Some Source checks have no external link because the correction rests on the book’s own inconsistency or on general knowledge; they are labelled in place.
    • Book text damaged at source: a truncated sentence on pp. 47–48 and a truncated explanation on p. 119; a garbled phrase on p. 130 was omitted. Arrows in the p. 182 figure were not reproduced.
    • The automated token check found 92–98% of each chapter’s names and numbers in the notes. The remaining misses were reviewed and logged as spelling variants, connective words or tokens from false statements.
